# rock-solid design

Terms such as primary, placement group, epoch or fencing are explained in the [glossary](glossary.md).

## Goals

Build a distributed storage system in Go ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) whose headline feature is **ReadWriteMany (RWX) shared filesystems** for a 3 to 10 node homelab Kubernetes cluster: many pods on many nodes mounting one directory, with no NFS gateway in the path, caching that stays correct when files are shared, and Longhorn-level ease of install. Block volumes come from the same core, at near-raw NVMe latency.

**Target user:** homelab and small-cluster Kubernetes operators. They have several NVMe or SATA solid-state drives per node, 10 to 25 Gigabit Ethernet (GbE), and some nodes with Remote Direct Memory Access (RDMA) capable cards. For RWX today they choose between a Network File System (NFS) server (single server, fragile), Longhorn RWX (an NFS server pod in front of each volume), JuiceFS (needs an external metadata database and object store) and CephFS (heavy on RAM, CPU and operator knowledge).

**Product goals, in priority order**

1. **RWX filesystem** (`volumeMode: Filesystem`, `ReadWriteMany`): a Filesystem in Userspace (FUSE) client on every node, talking straight to the storage nodes, with close-to-open consistency first and automatic per-file cache coherence next.
2. **Block volumes**: ReadWriteOnce (RWO) Persistent Volume Claims (PVCs), plus RWX block for KubeVirt live migration.
3. **S3** later, on the same core.
4. **Easy to run:** a single binary (one daemon per node, which also serves as the FUSE client) plus a Container Storage Interface (CSI) driver. No hugepages, pinned cores or external database to get started.
5. **Low CPU per operation**, so storage does not eat the cluster.
6. **Permissive license** (Apache 2.0).

**Performance targets.** Stated as round trips, because absolute numbers depend on the network and drives.

| Operation | Target cost |
| --- | --- |
| Read of a cached file (one writer, or readers only) | Page-cache speed: no FUSE or network trip (0.3 µs per 4K measured) |
| Read or write of a file being written from two or more nodes | One network round trip to the chunk primary, plus about 5 to 20 µs of FUSE overhead |
| `stat`, `lookup` within the cache timeout | Local |
| `stat`, `lookup` after a cache miss | One round trip to the MetaStore shard leader (lease read, no disk write) |
| `create`, `unlink`, same-directory `rename` | One Raft commit on one shard |
| `open` of a file | One round trip to the inode's owner (memory only, no disk write) |
| Block 4K write at queue depth 1 | Around 0.1 ms (Vitastor class) on drives with power-loss protection; Ceph is around 1 ms. Consumer drives without it floor at about 1 ms per durable write ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)) |

**Learning goals:** Go for systems code, the Storage Performance Development Kit (SPDK), the Data Plane Development Kit (DPDK) and RDMA, extended Berkeley Packet Filter (eBPF) tracing, plus distributed-systems correctness testing.

**Non-goals for v1:** Server Message Block (SMB) file sharing for macOS and Windows clients, GPU-cluster scale, shared-writable `mmap` across nodes, cross-directory hard links, a custom kernel module, hard disk drives (HDDs), which can come later as a capacity tier behind flash ([Addresses belong to the log](#on-disk-format)), and authentication or encryption between daemons. v1 trusts the cluster network: any host that can reach a daemon's port can read and write any volume.

## Landscape and positioning

The open gap is **RWX without a gateway**: every client reads and writes the storage nodes directly, caches aggressively while it is safe, and stops caching automatically when it is not. It installs like Longhorn and is licensed like SeaweedFS.

| System | What it covers | Why it doesn't fill the gap |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Longhorn | Easy Kubernetes block; RWX through a share-manager pod running NFS-Ganesha | All RWX traffic goes through one pod per volume: a bottleneck and a failover pause |
| [JuiceFS](https://github.com/juicedata/juicefs) | FUSE file system, CSI driver, Apache 2.0 | Needs an external metadata engine (Redis, TiKV, SQL) and an object store; close-to-open consistency only |
| CephFS | POSIX file system with full client capabilities | Around 1 ms writes, heavy CPU and RAM, steep operations learning curve |
| [SeaweedFS](https://github.com/seaweedfs/seaweedfs) | Single binary, S3, FUSE mount, CSI driver | Built around objects; no cache-coherence protocol for shared writes |
| [Vitastor](https://vitastor.io) | Ceph-like block, file, S3 at around 0.1 ms | Solo project; server under a GPL-based network-copyleft license that deters adopters |
| [OpenEBS Mayastor](https://github.com/openebs/mayastor) | Rust + SPDK + NVMe over Fabrics (NVMe-oF) block | Block only; requires hugepages and dedicated cores |
| [Weka](https://docs.weka.io/weka-system-overview/weka-client-and-mount-modes), DAOS, [DeepSeek 3FS](https://github.com/deepseek-ai/3fs) | AI-cluster file systems with kernel bypass | Built for GPU clusters; commercial or heavy to deploy |
| [MinIO](https://github.com/minio/minio), [RustFS](https://github.com/rustfs/rustfs), Garage | S3 | Object storage only |

**Takeaways**

- Homelab RWX today means NFS somewhere in the path. Removing it is the visible win.
- Weka shows that per-file cache-mode switching gives most of the benefit of full client capabilities with a much smaller protocol. Our spike shows it can be done on FUSE, without a kernel module (see [FUSE coherence spike](#appendix-fuse-coherence-spike)).
- Vitastor proves one person can get a 10x latency win over Ceph without DPDK, by shortening the code path. The same lean core serves block.
- Trust is the adoption gate. Storage people wait years before trusting a new system, so correctness work is the product.

## Strategy

Win on a short code path, correct caching and low CPU per operation. Kernel bypass is an opt-in fast path, never a requirement.

On homelab hardware the network caps throughput before the kernel does:

```
one NVMe Gen4 drive:  ~7 GB/s sequential, 4K write in ~0.04 ms (into its write cache)
10 GbE link:          ~1.2 GB/s
25 GbE link:          ~3 GB/s
kernel TCP stack:     saturates 10-25 GbE with one or two cores
```

So DPDK buys almost no throughput here. The latency gap between the disk and Ceph is almost all software path, which is what we attack. One caveat: a *durable* write is bounded by the drive's flush latency. On the consumer NVMe drive we measured, a 4K write plus flush takes 0.95 ms at p50, 98% of it inside the drive, and about 2 ms after the drive has idled for 100 ms ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)), so the 0.1 ms block target applies to drives with power-loss protection.

**Principles**

1. **RWX first:** the file frontend and its coherence protocol are the first usable product. Block and S3 reuse the same core.
2. **Lean data path:** client → chunk primary in one hop → log append in parallel with sends to replicas → acknowledge. Every frontend uses it.
3. **Cache where safe, never where unsafe:** a file is cached on a node only while no other node writes it. Correctness never depends on timeouts alone.
4. **Defaults that need no tuning:** `io_uring` for disk, kernel TCP for network, FUSE for the file client. No hugepages, pinned cores or kernel modules just to try it.
5. **Opt-in fast paths:** SPDK for dedicated drives, RDMA for nodes with capable cards. DPDK stays a learning experiment.
6. **Correctness before features:** deterministic simulation from day one, including the coherence protocol.
7. **Apache 2.0** to stand apart from Vitastor's network-copyleft license.

## Architecture overview

The system is a two-part core (ChunkStore for data, MetaStore for metadata) under three protocol frontends, over pluggable input/output drivers.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    subgraph FE[" "]
        direction LR
        FILE["<b>File: RWX (first)</b><br/>FUSE client on every node<br/>MetaStore: namespace, opens, cache modes"]
        BLK["<b>Block volumes</b><br/>ublk + CSI; NVMe-oF/TCP remote<br/>MetaStore: attach only"]
        S3["<b>S3 gateway (later)</b><br/>PUT writes, then seals chunks<br/>MetaStore: commit, LIST"]
    end
    subgraph CORE["Core: sans-IO state machines (events in, actions out)"]
        direction LR
        CS["<b>ChunkStore (data)</b><br/>Versioned chunks, deterministic chunk IDs<br/>Redundancy per pool:<br/>1 to 3 copies or erasure coding"]
        MS["<b>MetaStore (metadata)</b><br/>Ordered transactional key-value, multi-Raft<br/>Control group: pools, placement table + PG intervals<br/>Inodes, leases, S3 index"]
    end
    subgraph DRV["Drivers: pluggable, chosen by flags"]
        direction LR
        DISK["<b>Disk</b><br/>Default: io_uring<br/>Opt-in: SPDK on dedicated NVMe"]
        NET["<b>Network</b><br/>Default: kernel TCP<br/>Opt-in: RDMA or Soft-RoCE"]
        SIM["<b>Simulator (tests)</b><br/>Simulated disk, network, time<br/>One seed replays a whole run"]
    end
    FILE --> CORE
    BLK --> CORE
    S3 --> CORE
    CORE -- "actions out, events in" --> DRV
```

*Three frontends share one core; they differ only in how much metadata they need.*

The same daemon runs on every node. On storage nodes it serves chunks and MetaStore shards; on every node, including compute-only ones, it is also the FUSE client for RWX mounts. The core never touches real I/O, so the simulator can stand in for every driver.

## Data layer: ChunkStore

The ChunkStore is dumb, fast and versioned. It knows nothing about files, buckets or volumes.

```go
type ChunkStore interface {
	Write(c ChunkID, off uint32, data []byte, expectVer *uint64) (uint64, error)
	Punch(c ChunkID, off, n uint32, expectVer *uint64) (uint64, error) // zero a range; the whole chunk deletes it
	Read(c ChunkID, off, n uint32, atVer *uint64) ([]byte, error)
	Seal(c ChunkID) error     // now immutable -> eligible for erasure coding
	Flush(cs []ChunkID) error // durability barrier
}
```

**Deterministic chunk IDs.** `chunk_id = (volume_id, inode_id, offset / CHUNK_SIZE)` is computed, never looked up; a block volume uses inode 0. The hot path needs no metadata. A chunk ID packs into 128 bits: a 32-bit volume, a 64-bit inode and a 32-bit chunk index, which allows files up to 16 PiB. Volume IDs, like inode IDs, are never reused. `CHUNK_SIZE` is 4 MiB, Ceph RBD's default object size; the nightly benchmarks keep it honest. Inode IDs are scoped to their volume, so a [restored or cloned](#snapshots-and-clones) volume keeps every inode number and differs from its source only in `volume_id`.

**Versions.** Every write and punch returns a new version: a 64-bit number whose high 32 bits are the number of the chunk's PG [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) in which its primary ordered it, and whose low 32 bits count the chunk's writes in that interval. The primary takes the chunk's newest ordered version, pending ones included, and adds one, or starts at 1 when that version is from an earlier interval; interval numbers start at 1 too, so version 0 stays the absent chunk. Each interval has one primary, and a PG's interval numbers only grow, a split PG's two halves starting one above it, so versions grow across primaries and no two primaries can give one version to different data. A member that returns holding a write its new primary never saw is therefore told apart by the version alone, as with Ceph's `eversion_t` and Vitastor's per-PG epochs; a plain counter would take that stale write for the current one. A chunk whose counter runs out, after 4.3 billion writes in one interval, more than an hour even at a million writes a second, waits while its primary lets the PG's pending writes commit and asks the control plane for a new interval of its PG, which touches no other PG. The high half counts the PG's own intervals rather than [table epochs](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) because table epochs follow the whole cluster: in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike), one node flapping every 10 seconds with its members refilled one per table would use up 24 bits of table epochs in 19 hours and 32 bits in 207 days, while the busiest PG's intervals would take 272 years to fill 32 bits. A homelab year gave that PG at most 237 intervals. The layout is disk format: versions sit in every record entry, chunk index key and extent ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)), and 128-bit versions would add 8 bytes to each ([PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)). `expect_ver` gives compare-and-set on data. A primary keeps an old version only while a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) pins it. A read at a frozen version (`at_ver`) needs no ordering against writes, so any PG member holding that version can serve it, and it never waits for a writer.

**Reads see only acknowledged writes.** A version is *committed* once every live member of the chunk's PG holds it, which is also when its write is acknowledged. A read without `atVer` returns the newest committed version, never one still being replicated. Otherwise a reader could see a write that a primary crash then loses, since the new primary may be a member that never received it. So a primary maps a write into its [chunk index](#on-disk-format) only when the write commits; until then the write is pending, its record is in the log, and a read finds the previous version's blocks without waiting. A member that is not the primary maps writes as they become durable, since it serves no live reads, and a primary that restarts takes a new interval of each PG it leads and [peers](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) in it before it serves anything.

**Single order per chunk.** Each chunk has one primary that applies writes in one order, and every member holds them in that order, so RWX block and uncached shared files need no client-side protocol. The order is of attempts, not of writes: a client that resends a write whose first attempt committed but whose acknowledgment it never got, as after a primary crash, has it applied again under a newer version. If another node's write Y to the same bytes lands between the two, a reader can see the resent write X, then Y, then X again, so X took effect twice and the chunk's history is not linearizable; an `expectVer` write resent the same way fails although its first attempt succeeded. Ceph answers such a resend from the request IDs its PG log keeps, as the MetaStore's [replay records](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) do for metadata; whether chunk writes need the same is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

**Byte ranges become whole blocks at the primary.** The API takes byte ranges; records, checksums, the [chunk index](#on-disk-format) and snapshots hold whole 4 KiB blocks. The primary orders a chunk's writes and punches in arrival order, and for each block an operation covers only in part it *merges*: it takes the newest version of that block it has ordered, from a pending write's merged block if one covers it, else from its live map, zeros if the block was never written or was punched, and overlays the operation's bytes in a 4 KiB pool buffer. A write merged against a pending version depends on it: the primary acknowledges it only after that version commits, and if a member refuses the base, because a fence or a new interval reached it first, the primary refuses the dependent write too and the client retries against the committed version. So no byte of a write that never committed survives in a later one, within an interval by this rule and across intervals by [peering](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)'s, which never keeps a version whose base it drops. A base read from disk has its CRC32C checked, and one that fails is fetched from another member first, so a merge never carries rot into a new version; the chunk's later operations wait behind that read, so they keep their order, as Ceph's erasure-coded extent cache keeps one read outstanding per PG and never reorders. The primary sends and logs only the merged whole blocks, so members need no base and hold the same bytes under every version. Each loop keeps a small cache of recently merged blocks, and the [client](#client-design) sends whole blocks wherever it already holds the bytes, so the base read falls on write-shared files and 512-byte [block](#block-frontend) writes: one random 4 KiB read, plus at most two whole edge blocks per write on the wire to each replica, 3.3 µs each at 10 GbE.

The base must be the newest *ordered* version, pending ones included: in the [sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)'s model, merging against the committed version lost bytes on every seed as soon as two writes to one block were in flight, from one client pipelining or from two nodes. Merging on the client lost bytes without compare-and-set, and with it retried 0.64 to 1.28 times per write, since the version is per chunk and a client can only read committed ones; VitastorFS merges that way. Merging on every member, as BlueStore does, costs a read per member, and members then agree only if their histories do. Because a merged version holds the bytes of the one below it, a primary change must keep that dependency: peering drops only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions, keeping every version below one it keeps, and a new primary merges into a chunk only once it holds every version peering kept ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), [sub-block writes study](../studies/sub-block-writes/README.md)).

**Punch.** `punch` zeroes a byte range as a new version: whole blocks inside the range get a punch entry, and a block it covers in part is merged with zeros like a write, in the same record. Punched blocks are freed once no snapshot needs them, and later reads of the range return zeros. Punching a whole chunk deletes it and leaves a tombstone. File unlink and truncate, `fallocate` hole punches, block discard and volume deletion all free space this way (see [Freeing space](#freeing-space)).

**Fencing.** Each write carries the client's session ID. When a client session expires (see [Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)), the control plane publishes it as fenced in its session table and every storage node rejects its writes. The session's locks and cache rights are released only after every storage node has applied the fence or been marked down. This stops a paused or partitioned client from writing after its locks and cache rights are gone.

**Integrity.** Each 4K block, and each 4K block of an erasure-coded shard, carries a CRC32C checksum in its record's header in the [on-disk format](#on-disk-format), which catches bit rot, torn writes and misdirected writes. It is computed with Go's `hash/crc32`, which interleaves three SSE4.2 streams on x86-64: 167 ns per 4 KiB block, 7× faster than a table-driven implementation ([Go CPU spike](#appendix-go-cpu-per-operation-spike), C0). On arm64 it runs one stream of CRC instructions, 359 ns per 4 KiB block on an Apple M4 Pro.

**Placement groups.** Chunks are not placed one by one. Each chunk hashes into one of a fixed number of placement groups (PGs) in its volume's [pool](#pools-and-redundancy), as in Ceph, and placement is decided per PG:

```
chunk_id = (volume_id, inode_id, offset / CHUNK_SIZE)        // computed
pool     = volume record → pool                              // learned once, at attach or mount
h        = xxh3_64(chunk_id as 16 big-endian bytes, seed 0)  // computed
pg       = (pool, h >> (64 - log2(pool.pg_count)))           // top bits of the hash
members  = placement_table[pg]                               // in-memory; [primary, r1, r2] in rep3
```

The hash spreads a volume's consecutive chunks over many PGs, and so over every disk of its pool. A volume is therefore not bounded by any one drive; see [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity). The PG is also the unit of recovery and rebalancing: adding a disk moves whole PGs onto it, never individual chunks. The hash function and bit layout are part of the disk format and never change.

Each pool's `pg_count` is a power of two, sized for the largest expected cluster so that each disk holds around 100 PG members summed over all pools (`pg_count × width ÷ disks`): 2048 for a 3-replica pool alone on 60 disks. It can grow later by doubling. Because a PG is the top bits of the hash, it is one contiguous hash range, and doubling splits PG `p` into `2p` and `2p + 1` without rewriting the per-disk index.

**Placement table.** The table maps each PG to its members, in order with the primary first, and to its current *interval* number, its last activated interval and the members of any intervals since; the table as a whole carries a *table epoch*, its version. Its size depends on the pools' PG counts and widths, not on how much data is stored: 2048 PGs × 3 replicas × a 4-byte disk ID plus 8 bytes of interval numbers is 40 KiB, and even a 4096-PG `ec16-2` pool is about 320 KiB, whether the cluster holds 1 GB or 100 TB. So it is **not sharded**. It lives in the single control-plane Raft group, every node caches a full copy, and it changes only on pool, disk and node events, never on the I/O path. Every change (pool created, disk added, node failed, rebalance step) raises the table epoch, but only a change to a PG's members, their order or live marks (`maintenance` included), its pool's `replicas` or `min_live`, a split, or its primary's request starts a new interval of that PG ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), and a member rejects only requests from an earlier interval of their PG. Recovery and rebalancing while writes are in flight is where the hardest correctness bugs live; [Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) covers how a PG moves from one interval to the next.

The control-plane leader computes each new table and commits it as one Raft entry with a new table epoch, holding only the PG entries it changes and the CRC32C of the table it produces; Raft snapshots hold the whole table, and nodes never compute placement themselves. A node that holds the previous table applies the change and checks the CRC, and fetches the whole table after a gap or a mismatch, as Ceph's daemons do with incremental maps. Each PG entry carries its interval number, so a node that skips tables loses nothing, where Ceph's daemons must walk every map to work out each PG's interval; deltas only save bytes. In the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike), a homelab year with moves staged one per table pushed 2.25 GB of whole tables to 10 nodes against 105 MB of changes, and one flap of a node with its members moved away and back one per table committed 1,202 tables, 61 MB of whole tables to each node and to each control-plane voter's log; a flap shorter than `degraded_refill_after` moves no member, and one shorter than `refill_after` none of a PG it leaves with `min_live` live members. The computation is a pure function in the sans-IO core, and it starts from the previous table, never from scratch:

1. Refill every missing member onto the eligible disk furthest below its target. A member is missing once its disk has left the pool or lost its log, or once it has been down for `refill_after`, or for `degraded_refill_after` in a PG left below `min_live`, unless peering waits for it anyway ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)); a member that is merely down stays in its PG.
2. While any disk is outside its fill margin, move one member from the disk furthest above its target, even one inside its own margin, to the eligible disk furthest below; stop when no move brings that disk nearer its target.

A disk's target is its [capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) for the pool, what `write_limit` leaves of it after other pools' data, water-filled over failure domains so that no domain is asked for more than `max_per_domain` of a PG's width, and split within a domain by capacity. Capacity counts the pool's own data as space it holds, so a finished move does not shift the targets. Targets by free room did: each move's data changed them and the next computation proposed moves back, so after a disk was added to a pool holding data, settling took 1.5 to 2 times the moves and 3 to 5 computations, against 2 computations by capacity, the second proposing nothing ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). A disk's *fill margin* is max(5% of its target, 1 member). The 5% is Ceph's balancer deviation of 5 PGs at about 100 per disk and CockroachDB's rebalance threshold. The floor is needed because a 0.5 TB disk beside 8 TB disks has a target of 10 to 20 members, which no single move brings within 5%, so without it the loop stopped outside the margin in 15 to 18 of 200 random tables; Ceph recommends a deviation of 1 PG for disks that differ in size, and CockroachDB's floor of 2 replicas widened the tail in the study's model for no gain. Taking the source from the disk furthest above its target even inside its margin, as Ceph's balancer does once only underfull disks remain, left available + held a median 0.8 to 1.2% under balanced after a disk addition, for 11 to 17% more moves than taking it only from disks above their margins, which left 4.3 to 4.6%. Once the loop has converged, every disk is within its margin, so available + held ≥ balanced ÷ (1 + the largest margin of the pool's disks), and no closer bound holds: a disk with a target of about ten members can sit one member, 9.4%, above it until the pool has more PGs. With shards and `max_per_domain` above 1 (phase 10), two shards of a PG never share a disk, so a domain is asked for at most min(`max_per_domain`, its disks) members of a PG and a disk for one, and a PG's first `replicas` members, which hold its copies, sit in distinct domains. Both steps keep the pool's failure-domain rule. A target proportional to size alone cannot be met when failure domains differ: under `rep2`, nodes of 0.7 and 2 GB each hold one copy of every PG whatever their sizes, and in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike) a loop aiming at size-proportional shares never converged on such layouts, while capped targets converged within 2% and reached the pool's [balanced capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) within 1%. Weighting by capacity rather than size also keeps a disk that another pool filled from capping this one. This gives exact balance on small clusters with mismatched disks, where a placement function every node evaluates (Ceph CRUSH, Weka's algorithmic ownership) would leave disks unbalanced; Ceph patches the same problem with `pg-upmap` exception tables and a balancer that writes them, so in practice it runs a table too, and its balancer warns that on mismatched devices a tight deviation can leave it "shuffling data forever as it endeavors to meet an impossible expectation". Each table change moves only the members the algorithm chose, so moves can be staged and throttled, and an operator can drain a disk or pin a PG by editing the table. The table epoch is simply the table's version and an interval a counter per PG, which keeps simulation and models simple. At thousands of disks the table and its recomputation would grow enough to revisit computed placement; that is outside the target.

What does grow with data lives elsewhere. Each disk keeps its own [index](#on-disk-format) of the chunks and shards it holds, and file-to-inode metadata lives in the sharded MetaStore.

### Epoch changes and failure detection

A new table reaches nodes at different times, so for a while a PG's old primary may act on an older table than its new primary. What keeps that window safe is the PG's *interval*: a run of tables in which the PG's ordered members and their live marks stay the same, so the PG has one primary and one set of members that must acknowledge its writes. The leader starts a new interval of a PG, its number plus one, in a table that changes the PG's members or their order, and so its primary, a member's live mark (down, up, `maintenance`), its pool's `replicas` or `min_live`, or splits it (both halves take the parent's number plus one), and when the PG's primary asks because a chunk's counter ran out or it restarted. Every other change, to other PGs, to pools or to anything else in the table, raises the table epoch and leaves the interval alone. Ceph defines its intervals the same way (`PastIntervals::is_new_interval`). Three different numbers are called epochs, and none of them is in a version: the *table epoch* versions the placement table; a *support epoch* numbers one unbroken run of a node-pair lease (below); a volume's *snapshot epoch* counts its [snapshots](#snapshots-and-clones). A PG's interval numbers, which are in versions, are not epochs.

**Requests are checked against their PG's interval.** Every message about a PG carries the sender's table epoch and the PG's interval number in that table. A member accepts it only if that number is the PG's interval in its own table, whichever table is newer; it refuses a lower number, answering with its own table epoch and number, and fetches a table at least as new as the sender's before it judges a higher one. Put in epochs, a request tagged with table epoch `e` that reaches a member holding `e' > e` is valid exactly when no table after `e` up to `e'` started a new interval of its PG. Comparing the PG's members in the two tables would not do: a member removed and added back leaves them equal, though it missed the writes in between. A client's write carries no state that depends on the PG's replicas, so a primary takes it while its interval number is at least the one in which the primary's own term began, and orders again, once it has re-peered, the client writes it held when an interval ended under it, but only if no interval since the one it held them in had another primary, which peering shows. Otherwise it refuses them and the client retries with a newer table, since a held write could land after writes another primary acknowledged meanwhile, such as a dead daemon's write after its successor's reissue ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). A table change therefore makes only the writes of the PGs it changed retry. In the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)'s homelab year on 10 nodes, a table changed 22% of PGs on average, or 2 to 4% with moves staged one per table, so 4.4 to 46 times fewer writes were disturbed than by rejecting every older table epoch, and staging moves cost other PGs nothing. On 3 nodes, where every PG has a member on every node, a node event still changes every PG; per-PG checks then spare only disk events, moves, counter bumps and restarts. Ceph checks the same way: a replica drops an operation only if its PG started a new interval after the operation's map epoch (`can_discard_replica_op`), and a primary drops a client's only if the PG's primary changed since (`same_primary_since`), requeuing those it held ([PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)).

**Writes: the new primary peers before it accepts writes.** A write tagged with interval `j` completes only when every member live in `j` acknowledges it, and a member whose table has moved the PG past `j` refuses it. So before it accepts writes in interval `k`, the new primary *peers*. First it stops the earlier intervals: for every interval of the PG since its last *activated* one, the last whose primary finished peering, it gets at least one member that was live in that interval to adopt a table with interval `k`. From then on no write tagged with an earlier interval can complete, because each needs the acknowledgment of a member that now refuses it, and every write acknowledged in one of those intervals is on that member. One interval back is not enough, since a PG can change interval twice before the first new primary peers, and a member of the older interval may still complete its writes. An interval whose live members, counting any in `maintenance`, numbered fewer than `min_live` accepted no writes and is skipped, as Ceph skips intervals below `min_size`; if no member of another one can be reached, the PG waits, as Ceph's waits in its `down` state, until one returns or an operator declares its disks lost. Ceph also records each primary's activation in the map before it activates (`up_thru`), so that peering can skip intervals that never activated, at the price of a map change on every peering; at 3 to 10 nodes waiting is enough.

**Which versions survive.** The primary then collects, from each member it reaches, the newest version of every chunk and its *missing list*. That exchange grows with the chunks the PG holds, not with the writes a member missed, which RADOS's per-PG log of recent writes avoids for short outages (Weil et al., "RADOS: A Scalable, Reliable Storage Service for Petabyte-scale Storage Clusters", §3.4.1); whether it fits within the pause a peering costs is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions). Before a primary activates, every member persists, for each chunk it lacks or holds a dropped version of, the kept version it must receive, and recovery removes the entry as it copies, as Ceph's members keep a missing set; a member never serves a chunk while it is on its list. So the members live in the last activated interval know the version that interval's peering kept for every chunk, even where they lack its blocks, and those decisions are final. Of the versions assigned in the last activated interval itself, the primary cannot tell an acknowledged write from one that reached every member it sees but was never acknowledged, so it keeps one unless some member that was live in that interval neither holds nor lists it, which proves the write was never acknowledged. It keeps every version below a kept one, and reports a chunk whose kept version no reachable member holds as unfound rather than serve an older one. Whether this rule is complete across several intervals is for the phase 2 model to show ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). The versions it drops are always the newest of their chunk: a primary sends a chunk's writes to each member in version order, again in that order after a reconnect, and each member logs them in that order, so, since durability is a prefix of a disk's log, a member that lacks a version lacks every later one of the chunk. That matters because a write that [merges a partial block](#data-layer-chunkstore) carries the bytes of the version below it: peering never keeps a version whose base it drops.

**Recovery.** A version peering drops, or one that some member lacks, is recovered by writing the authoritative member's blocks under a new version to every member that lacks them or logged the dropped version, the new primary included, as Ceph recovers divergent objects. Every member so ends up with the same choice, and a member whose log holds the dropped write has a later record covering its blocks, so neither its replay nor a rebuild of its index brings the write back. Until a chunk's kept versions are on the new primary, a write that covers part of one of its blocks waits, since its merge needs the newest kept block, as Ceph's primary holds writes to a missing or degraded object; whole-block writes need no base and go ahead. Once its members have persisted their missing lists, the primary activates and reports it on its next heartbeat, and the leader records the PG's new last activated interval, dropping the member lists of the intervals before it, in a later table that starts no interval.

**Restarts.** A primary that restarts asks for a new interval of each PG it leads and peers in it before it serves. Peering again in its old interval is not safe: a write its previous run sent could still sit in a member's socket buffer and land after that member answered peering, under a version the restarted primary would then give to other data. A new interval makes members refuse the old run's messages once they answer, and costs one table change touching only the PGs that node leads.

**A disk is its log.** Peering believes what a member says about the intervals it was live in: a member that neither holds nor lists a version proves the version was never acknowledged, and a member that adopts a new interval stops the old ones. A disk formatted again, or replaced by a new drive, that came back under the disk ID the table still names would answer from an empty log: it would drop acknowledged writes, and stop an interval whose writes it never saw. So the control plane records, beside each disk ID, the disk's log ID, the random number drawn when it was formatted ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)), and every node reports the log ID of each disk it opens. A log ID the control plane does not know is a new disk: it gets a new disk ID from a counter that never reuses one and joins as an empty disk. The disk ID it replaced stays down like a failed disk, and since its log is gone its members refill elsewhere at once, without waiting for `refill_after`; a peering that needs it waits, as for any unreachable member, until an operator declares it lost. A log ID already up on another node or device, as from a cloned drive, is refused. The shard replicas a lost log held are lost with it, and the disk's new replicas join their shards under new replica IDs, as etcd re-adds a member that lost its data directory under a new ID. Ceph's monitors likewise ignore an OSD's boot when its fsid differs from the one recorded for its ID (`OSDMonitor::preprocess_boot`). A drive that loses flushed writes but keeps its superblock keeps its log ID, so this rule does not catch it; whether consumer drives honour Flush is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

**Reads: the old primary's read lease runs out first.** Reads need no acknowledgment from other members, so the write rule does not stop an old primary from serving stale reads after the new one has accepted writes. A primary therefore serves reads for a PG only while it holds an unexpired read lease from each of the PG's other live members. A member that granted a lease does not take part in peering a new interval of a PG that moves its primary away from that node until the lease expires, or until the old primary has adopted that interval itself, as it does in every planned change. Nor does it renew that node's grant until the node has reported a table at least as new as its own, which every heartbeat carries, so an old primary that still reaches its members but not the control plane loses its leases within one `primary_lease`, and one that can fetch the table pays nothing. Only the last activated interval's primary can have served reads, and peering reaches a member live in that interval, which granted it a lease, so peering across several intervals adds no wait. A new interval that keeps the primary, such as a replaced replica or a counter bump, needs no wait at all: no other node can commit to the PG, so the primary keeps serving reads of committed versions and holds only writes until it has re-peered. A grant carries a *support epoch*, a number of the node pair's own, unrelated to table epochs. Once a grant lapses on the grantor's clocks, the grantor bumps the support epoch and never renews the old one, so a lease taken under it never comes back after a partition heals. A node that restarts has forgotten what it granted, so until both its clocks show one `primary_lease` since it started it grants no vote and joins no peering. Leases are granted per node pair, covering every PG the pair shares, and are renewed by the heartbeats that already flow between them, so their cost does not grow with the PG count. Raft shards use the same leases for their [leader leases](#metadata-layer-metastore), and client [sessions](#sessions-and-fencing) count theirs by the same rules. Ceph added PG read leases for the same reason. `primary_lease` defaults to 4 s, shorter than failure detection below, so a crash adds no wait for the lease unless a time daemon is slowing a grantor's clock at the time.

**Lease time: two clocks, each side taking the safe one.** The holder counts a lease from when it asked for it and the grantor from when it granted it, each on its own clocks, so leases need clocks that run at nearly the same rate, not synchronized ones. No single Linux clock promises that. `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` run at whatever rate the time daemon sets: `adjtimex` lets it move the tick by ±10%, the frequency by ±500 ppm (all that `MAXFREQ` in `include/linux/timex.h` bounds) and slew up to 0.5 s of phase offset on top, 22.6% fast or slow in all, and chrony slews at up to 8.3% by default (`maxslewrate`). `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` ignores the daemon, but runs at the rate the kernel worked out for the CPU's timestamp counter at boot, which nothing bounds: on Intel CPUs that report their crystal frequency the kernel trusts the reported number unchecked (`arch/x86/kernel/tsc.c`), a wrong entry in its own crystal table ran Skylake-X workstations 4% slow until a fix in December 2017, every KVM, Hyper-V and VMware guest takes its host's figure, and only the daemon corrects it ([clock slew spike](#appendix-clock-slew-spike), [lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md)). So every lease is counted on both: the holder ends it when either clock shows the lease minus `lease_drift` (default 1% of the lease) since it asked, and the grantor keeps it until both show the whole lease since it granted. A slew or a bad calibration then only shortens a holder's lease or lengthens a grantor's wait; a slew does so by at most 22.6%, so a crash can wait up to 5.2 s for a lease, past `node_down_after`. That leaves `lease_drift` to cover the two oscillators, typically within ±100 ppm each. A node's two clocks can still err the same way: ntpd and systemd-timesyncd correct at most 500 ppm of frequency and step the clock for the rest, which leaves `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` at the raw clock's rate. So each holder bounds how fast each grantor's raw clock runs against its own. A grant carries the grantor's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` reading `g`, and the holder notes on its own `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` when it sent the request, `s`, and when the grant arrived, `r`. The grantor read `g` somewhere between the two, so between grants `a` and `b` at least `s_b − r_a` passed on the holder, and the grantor's clock ran at most `(g_b − g_a) / (s_b − r_a)` times as fast: a bound, not an estimate, which no delayed or asymmetric path can make too low. Any two grants give a bound, however close together; a short gap only loosens it. A holder keeps its rate window, its last grant and its bound per grantor node and boot: every grant and heartbeat carries the grantor's boot ID (`/proc/sys/kernel/random/boot_id`, read once at start), because `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` starts again near 0 at every boot, and a new boot may calibrate differently or run on another host. A window starts at first contact with a grantor, at a new boot ID, at a raw reading below the last one, after any detection of a pause (below), and when the last grant ages out of `rate_window` (default 10 s). When a window's first grant arrives, the holder asks again `rate_gap` (100 ms, a constant) later, and it takes no lease from a grantor until it has a bound for that grantor's boot. Over the window the holder pairs the grant with the shortest round trip in its first third with the one in its last third, so a slow round trip loosens the bound only when every grant in a third was slow. The bound overshoots by about the two round trips over the third of the window between them: 600 ppm with 1 ms round trips, 60 ppm with the 100 µs of a quiet network, once the window is full, against the 5,000 ppm of `lease_drift / 2`. The first bound, from grants `rate_gap` apart, overshoots by about 2% at 1 ms round trips, so the first lease after a window starts is shorter: 3.90 s instead of 3.96 s, or 3.18 s with the 10 ms round trips of an event loop at 90% load, and it lengthens as the window fills. Since the holder counts on the faster of its clocks, so no slower than its raw one, and the grantor on the slower of its own, so no faster than its raw one, the holder's end of a lease from a grantor whose raw clock runs ρ times as fast as its own comes first exactly when the margin is at least `1 − 1/ρ`, whatever either daemon does, and with a bound `B ≥ ρ` a margin of `1 − 1/B` suffices. A holder whose bound for a grantor exceeds `1 + lease_drift / 2` widens its margin for that grantor by the excess, to `lease_drift + max(0, B − (1 + lease_drift / 2))`, and reports the pair. That is always at least `lease_drift / 2`, 20 ms of a 4 s lease, more than `1 − 1/B` needs (0.596% for a grantor 0.6% fast, given 1.1%), and that headroom is what covers a pair whose rate rises after it was measured: an oscillator warming up, the kernel's clocksource watchdog switching a grantor to another clocksource, which installs a new raw rate, or a VM migrated onto a host with another calibration. A margin of `max_margin` (50%) or more, a bound of about 1.5, gives no lease, and the holder reports the pair. A holder so pays about 100 ms without reads on a grantor's leases when it starts, when that grantor reboots and after every detection; a grantor's daemon restart within one boot costs nothing, since its raw clock and boot ID carry on. Neither clock tells a holder how long it slept: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` stops in suspend, and the kernel may measure the sleep in whole seconds of the real-time clock (`arch/x86/kernel/rtc.c`). A hypervisor can hide a pause altogether: QEMU and Firecracker bring back a snapshot or a migrated VM with its clocks as they were saved, so every clock in the guest stands still across the pause, and QEMU sets kvmclock back to its value at a `stop` when the VM continues, which freezes every clock of a guest whose clocksource is kvm-clock. A guest on the TSC clocksource (QEMU with `+invtsc`, Firecracker by default) sees a stop in place in every clock, and so does any Firecracker guest after a pause ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)). A pause matters once it outlasts `primary_lease` less a heartbeat, 3 s; a shorter one ends before the grantor's lease, renewed within the last heartbeat, runs out. So a holder ends every lease it holds on `Resumed`, which the [event loop](#runtime-in-go) delivers when `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` gains on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, after a suspend, or when the disruption marker of the hypervisor's VMClock device changes. QEMU (10.0 and later, with `-device vmclock`) and Firecracker (1.15 and later) change the marker on every snapshot restore and live migration before the guest runs again, and the loop reads it from a mapped page, 3 ns per pass. A QEMU guest on kvm-clock stopped and continued in place, or snapshotted while it runs, changes neither, so every heartbeat a grantor sends carries its `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, not only its grants, and the holder checks each: if the grantor's clock moved further since the last grant `a` than the margin `m` of the lease taken from `a` allows, `g − g_a > (r − s_a) / (1 − m)` (1.0101 times at `lease_drift` alone), the holder lost time, or the grantor's rate rose past the headroom, and ends its leases as on `Resumed`. Tied to the margin, the check never trips on a grantor whose rate the margin already covers, which a fixed threshold at `1 + lease_drift / 2` would do on every heartbeat from a grantor more than 0.5% fast, restarting its window each time so that its bound never formed. A pause the check misses fits inside the lease's headroom as long as the last grant is less than a lease old. Messages sent to a stopped VM wait in the hypervisor's network backend and arrive when it continues, ahead of any client request sent after them, and the holder handles a pass's peer messages before its reads, so it ends the old leases before it serves a request that waited behind them. After either, the holder asks each grantor again at once and restarts its rate window, so a pause costs about `rate_gap` of reads instead of also counting as a fast grantor. A grantor needs none of these rules, because a clock that stopped only makes it wait longer. Go's own monotonic clock is `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (`runtime/sys_linux_amd64.s`), neither of the two leases need, so the loop reads its clocks itself, together, after each pass's wait: `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` with raw syscalls, about 140 ns each, between two reads of `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). It sees a suspend as `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` gaining on `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`: the kernel changes the difference between the two only when it adds sleep time (`kernel/time/timekeeping.c`), and a slew moves both alike. The gain that counts as a suspend lies above the read bracket's ε, so that a stall between the reads is not one, and at most 2 s, since a pause that matters outlasts 3 s and the kernel may round a suspend down to whole seconds. Steps of the wall clock move none of these clocks. A stop whose queued messages the backend dropped or delayed can still leave a holder serving on an old lease for up to a heartbeat, and so can a grantor whose rate rises past the headroom while its heartbeats are lost ([open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).

**Failure detection.** Only the control plane changes membership. Every node sends it a heartbeat each second, and it marks a node down after `node_down_after` (default 5 s) without one. Marking a node down starts a new interval of every PG with a member on it. Until that table, writes to those PGs wait, so a crash pauses them for up to `node_down_after`. The count runs on the control-plane leader, and a new leader starts it from its own leader lease, so when the crashed node was the control-plane leader, its PGs wait for the control-plane [failover](#metadata-layer-metastore) plus `node_down_after`.

**Down is not gone.** A member marked down stays in its PG, marked down, and is refilled onto another disk only once it has been down for `refill_after` (default 10 minutes). Until then its PGs write to their live members, degraded, and a member that returns is marked up in a new interval, peers, and receives only the versions on its missing list, so a reboot or a network hiccup copies what was written meanwhile, never the member's whole data. Refilling at once would copy every member of a rebooting node onto other disks, and the balance step would move them back once it returned. RADOS keeps a down OSD *in* the mapping until it is marked *out* for the same reason (Weil et al., §2.3), and Ceph marks it out after 10 minutes (`mon_osd_down_out_interval`). A lower `refill_after` buys earlier re-protection with copies a reboot then wastes. On 3 nodes under `rep3` no other node can take a member, so `refill_after` delays only refills within a node after a disk fails.

Two cases refill sooner. A disk whose log is gone, replaced or declared lost ([A disk is its log](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), leaves nothing to wait for, so its members refill at once. A PG left with fewer than `min_live` live members accepts no writes, so its down members refill once they have been down for `degraded_refill_after` (default 1 minute): long enough that a 5 s network hiccup or a 1 s disk stall, which end well inside it, move nothing, and short enough that a PG a crash stopped takes writes again within about a minute rather than ten. A reboot outlasts it, so a `rep2` pool, whose `min_live` of 2 leaves every PG with a member down below it, copies a rebooting node's PGs onto other disks and back once it returns, unless the operator marks the node `maintenance` first; a member in `maintenance` is never refilled while it is marked. Neither rule refills a member that peering waits for anyway, the only member live in some interval since the PG's last activated one: the PG cannot activate without its writes, so a new member would only wait beside it.

**A refill member counts at once.** A member a refill places is live, and counts toward `min_live`, from the table that adds it, while it still recovers. Every write from then on lands on it and on the PG's other live members, so on at least `min_live` copies, and the chunks written before are on its missing list, which it persists before the primary activates and never serves until recovery copies them. So a PG stopped below `min_live` takes writes again once the refill's interval activates, not once the copy ends, while its older data keeps the redundancy it had until recovery finishes, and `Stuck` reports a copy that stops ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)).

**Flap damping.** A node or disk marked down `flap_count` times (default 5) within `flap_window` (default 10 minutes) stays down until its heartbeats, and for a disk the ages of its I/O, have been normal for a whole `flap_window`, as a fail-slow disk rejoins (below) and as Ceph stops an OSD marked down 5 times in 10 minutes (`osd_max_markdown_count`). Undamped, a node whose heartbeats stop for 5 s at a time would start two intervals of each of its PGs per flap; damped, at most `flap_count` per window. A damped member is down from the mark that damped it, and `refill_after` counts from there. Interval numbers would outlast even the undamped flap for centuries, and the damped one for millennia ([PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)); damping is for the PGs whose writes and reads each flap pauses. A damped node or disk is released as soon as its heartbeats are normal when a PG it belongs to would otherwise stay below `min_live` with no eligible disk to refill onto, as fail-slow never marks a disk down below `min_live`: on `rep2` with two storage nodes, holding it would stop those PGs' writes for a whole `flap_window` after it is healthy, where undamped they take writes between flaps.

**Hung disks.** A node's heartbeats say nothing about its disks: a drive whose I/O never completes leaves its node and the disk's event loop running, since an io_uring submission does not wait for the drive, while every PG the disk belongs to waits on it. So each disk's owner loop keeps the age of the disk's *oldest unfinished I/O*, a read, a write or a flush, on its own clocks; discards are left out, since some drives take long over a burst of them and a stall they cause shows in the I/O queued behind. A disk *stalls* when that age passes `member_timeout` (default 1 s), or when its open log has failed, which is final until recovery runs again ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)). Every heartbeat carries each disk's oldest age, so the control plane marks a stalled disk down within about a second even when no primary waits on it, such as a primary's own disk or a disk holding only shard replicas. A primary that waits more than `member_timeout` for a member's acknowledgment reports it, and the control plane then sends a *direct probe* to the member disk's owner loop, which answers from what it holds in memory, never with disk I/O, so a hung drive cannot delay the answer. The probe fails if the disk stalls or the loop does not answer within `member_timeout`, and the control plane then marks the disk down at once, so a hung disk stops its PGs for 1 to 2 s plus a table, not for `slow_disk_window`. A stalled disk counts as failed, not slow: it is marked down even when that leaves a PG below `min_live`, since a PG waiting on it accepts no writes either way. It rejoins as a fail-slow disk does, below. A probe that answers healthy leaves the member up, which is right when the member is only busy, but leaves the PG waiting when the link between the primary and that member alone is down ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)).

**Fail-slow.** A disk that slows down without failing would stall every write of every PG it belongs to, since writes wait for all live members. Primaries report each member's acknowledgment latency to the control plane. When a disk's 99th percentile stays above `slow_disk_factor` (default 10×) the median of its pool's disks for `slow_disk_window` (default 1 minute), the control plane marks it down as if it had failed. It never does this when a PG would drop below `min_live`; those PGs stay slow, and the pool reports them. A disk marked down this way or as stalled keeps being probed, and rejoins only after its latency has stayed normal, with no stall, for a whole `slow_disk_window`, so a flapping disk does not churn its PGs' intervals.

### On-disk format

Each disk holds one log for everything stored on it: chunk data, the Raft logs of the [MetaStore](#metadata-layer-metastore) shard replicas placed on it, and the indexes over both. The daemon embeds no key-value engine and a disk needs no filesystem. The log belongs to one event loop, the disk's *owner*, which also owns its chunk index and serves its chunk writes; shard replicas on other loops hand it their records through its [inbox](#runtime-in-go). The disk is a sequence of fixed-size segments, written only through the disk driver's actions, so SPDK can own the whole drive and the simulator sees every durable byte. A disk is a raw block device or a file; a file's segments are written in full once when it is created, because a write into never-written file extents makes `fdatasync` commit the filesystem journal as well (2.5 ms instead of 0.95 ms in the [NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike)). Every write is appended:

```
disk = segment 0:     superblock in its first and last block (log id, segment size and count)
       segments 1..n: segment header (log id, stream, lane, open sequence, newest manifest) + records

each round (what arrived from every producer during the last round, up to max_group_commit_bytes):
  record = header blocks: log id, segment sequence, offset, LSN, durable LSN,
                          entries [write | punch (pool, chunk_id, version, block range)
                                   | copy (pool, chunk_id, version, block range, source)
                                   | freeze (pool, chunk_id, version, snapshot epoch)
                                   | blob (kind, owner, length)],
                          crc32c per data block, header checksum
         + data blocks:   written chunk blocks, then blob payloads (Raft entries and state,
                          sorted runs, manifests)
  append it to one lane's open segment in one write (after the segment header if the round
  opens a segment), flush once, acknowledge every item
```

- **One flush per round.** A round's chunk writes and the commits of every shard on the disk share one flush, however many shards there are: one combined commit for 100 shards took 1.2 ms, and 100 separate ones 114 ms ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). Every extra flush costs a full flush time, so the header goes in the same write and the same flush as its data, and its own checksum plus the per-block CRCs make a torn record detectable on recovery. A round's write, segment header included, is capped at `max_group_commit_bytes` (default 1 MiB), which bounds how long a large chunk write delays a metadata commit: on the measured drive a commit costs the same 0.95 ms from 4 KiB to 64 KiB, and 2.3 ms at 1 MiB, beyond which latency grows faster than throughput. The cap is the only bound on a round: rounds under open load stay far below it (at most 574 KiB at 130,000 proposals/s on one loop), a smaller cap costs throughput because a round left behind waits a whole flush (256 KiB lost a third), and a bound on a round's collection time changed nothing ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). Each round writes one record to one lane of one stream (see [Three streams](#on-disk-format)), taking the lanes that have work in turn. At most `commit_depth` rounds are in flight at once, 1 by default, and a round's flush is submitted only once its own write and every earlier round's write have completed, so durability is a prefix of the log: when a record is durable, so is every record before it, and at a crash only the last `commit_depth` records can be torn or missing. A depth of 2 does not pay on a consumer drive: a flush drains the drive's whole write cache, so a hot 4K commit next to a stream of 1 MiB cold records took 2.3 ms with two rounds in flight, against 0.95 ms alone and 2.35 ms with both records in one round under one flush ([stream overlap spike](#appendix-stream-overlap-spike)). Whether a round should carry one record per stream under one flush is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
- **One owner loop per disk, not a log per loop.** A shard replica may run on any loop of its node. Once per pass, a loop hands each disk's owner one inbox message holding its shards' `Ready` records for that disk, and the owner hands back one message per round naming what became durable. That costs two inbox wakeups per loop per round, not per operation, and a shard on its disk's own loop skips them. On the cloud VM a wakeup cost 13.6 µs of CPU and a batched item 0.07 µs; an owner fed by 8 loops wrote 39 items per flush, where a log per loop wrote 8, at the same CPU per item ([log per core spike](#appendix-log-per-core-spike)). A log per loop on every disk would give a node's 4 to 16 loops as many flush streams per disk, past the two a consumer drive overlaps ([NVMe flush spike](#appendix-nvme-flush-spike), N7), split each round into smaller records with a header each, and give the format several LSN sequences. Recovery, checkpoints and the cleaner keep one owner: recovery runs on the owner and hands each shard's replayed entries to its loop, a checkpoint asks each index's loop for its runs, and the cleaner sends each move to the loop that owns the index it changes. TiKV works the same way: its raftstore threads hand every region's writes to one store writer by default, and raft-engine merges concurrent writers into one write and one sync. ScyllaDB keeps a commitlog per core, but by default acknowledges writes before a periodic sync, so it pays no flush per write. Pebble and RocksDB work the same way inside one process: every committing thread joins the group collected while the current sync runs, and CockroachDB runs every range of a store through one Pebble write-ahead log.
- **Write, then flush.** The driver makes a record durable with a write followed by a flush command (`IORING_FSYNC_DATASYNC` through io_uring, an NVMe Flush under SPDK), never with Force Unit Access (FUA) writes or `O_DSYNC`, which took 2.2 ms per 4K write on the measured drive. A flush makes durable every write that completed before it runs. When every earlier round's write has completed, the log emits a round's write and its flush together, and the io_uring driver submits them linked (`IOSQE_IO_LINK`), so a round costs one `io_uring_enter` and no wakeup between the two; otherwise the flush follows once the writes it must cover have completed. The write gathers the round's buffers in place: a header buffer the log fills, each producer's written blocks in the buffer it submitted, and one buffer into which the log packs the round's blobs, which are not block-aligned. Chunk blocks are never copied. The log takes each round slot's header and blob buffers from the pool once, when it starts, so producers that fill the pool cannot stop the round that would free their buffers. A disk error fails the log: queued items fail, and the items of rounds in flight are in doubt, since their writes may still land and be flushed, so recovery decides. Any flush error fails the log, including one reported late for a round a later flush already made durable, because the kernel may report a failed writeback to only one caller. Drives with power-loss protection may do better with FUA, so `durability` is a per-disk setting, `flush` (default) or `fua`.
- **Three streams, in lanes.** Records go to one of three streams, so that a segment holds data of one kind, which tends to die together. The *hot* stream takes new chunk copies and the Raft entries of the same round; the *cold* stream takes blocks the cleaner moves and encoded shards; the *metadata* stream takes sorted runs, compaction output and manifests, which are written whole and die whole when a compaction or a newer manifest replaces them. A stream fills its segments in *lanes*: a lane is a sequence of segments that fill one after another, with at most one open at a time, so LSNs rise along it, which is what [recovery](#on-disk-format) reads it by. Lane 0 of each stream is its shared segment, and merge worker *w*, 1 to the disk's merge workers, writes its output to lane *w* of the metadata stream, so that a merge's pieces do not share segments with flush pieces (see [The LSM tree](#on-disk-format)). When a merge ends, its worker's segment stays open and the worker's next merge continues it, so no unused tail is left behind run pieces the cleaner cannot move; a segment may then hold the tail of one merge's output and the head of the next one's, possibly another index's, at most one such segment per merge boundary. A lane per merge instead would leave each ended merge's last segment as the head of a lane, read at every start until it is freed, with its tail stranded. Group commit keeps a queue, a cursor and the append rule (see Recovery) per lane, and turns go round the lanes with work, 3 plus the merge workers, in the order hot, cold, metadata lane 0, then merge lanes 1 to *w*, starting after the lane of the last round. Only the hot and cold lanes take ordinary items: metadata lane 0 is the checkpoint's queue and a merge lane its worker's output, so no record mixes ordinary items with a checkpoint's or a merge's. `merge_workers` is one setting that both the log and the merge scheduler read: 0 means the default of 1, and at most 255, since the lane is a byte. A lane past the configured workers, left by a restart with fewer, takes no more records; recovery still reads its last segment at every start until the cleaner frees it. Mixed with the cleaner's copies, runs raised block-volume write amplification by 25 to 54% at 80 to 90% full, and from 17 to 50 at 95%; in a stream of their own, by 3 to 15% at 80 to 90% full ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). Raft entries stay in the hot stream because a round writes one record to one stream: a Raft stream would split a commit's chunk write and its Raft entries into two rounds and two flushes, to save 6%. More streams by temperature are not worth it yet: the cleaner's copies split by age, as SepBIT does, cut write amplification by up to 30% on hot/cold overwrites but raised it by up to 63% on uniform ones, and perfect hot/cold separation is only 1.1 to 1.6× below two streams once headers count. The stream tag and the lane are a byte each, so a later stream needs only a new format version.
- **Chunk slots.** A chunk written whole, all 4 MiB in one write-back, goes to a chunk slot instead of the log. A slot segment holds as many slots of exactly one chunk's data as fit: 16 in a 64 MiB segment, one in the 4 MiB segments of a 16 GiB disk (segment size, below), and a disk whose segments are smaller than a chunk has no slot segments and writes whole chunks to the log. The round's record goes to the hot stream as usual, its write entry names the slot, and its per-block CRCs cover the slot's blocks, so recovery checks a tail record's slot data as it checks data in the log. A slot is free for reuse once the record that rewrote or deleted its chunk is durable; the segment is TRIMmed and freed with its last slot, and allocation fills the fullest slot segment first. A slot segment has no segment header, since chunk data fills it: the usage table lists it as a slot segment, and replay marks one opened after the manifest from the records that name its slots, never from its first block. Whole chunks deleted at random then cost no cleaning, a write amplification of 1.004 at any fill, against 4.9 at 90% and 9.0 at 95% full in 64 MiB segments, and a pool holding half its data in whole chunks cleans its small writes for 2.5 instead of 6.1 at 90%, because whole chunks no longer use their spare space ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). The record headers stay in the log, 0.4% of the chunk's size, live while the chunk is and moved by the cleaner like data; a slot carrying its own headers would fit only 15 per 64 MiB segment, which caps a pool of whole chunks near 93% full. Partial chunks, such as file tails and small files, stay in the log too. Other layouts do not help: placement groups spread every file over every disk, so a disk sees whole-file deletes as random chunk deletes; a stream of their own changes nothing (6.2 against 6.1), since whole chunks die as randomly as small writes; and smaller segments for the whole disk would still cost 1.5 at 90% full with 4 MiB segments and multiply the usage table's snapshots by 16.
- **Every header names its place.** Segment and record headers repeat the log id drawn at format time, the segment's open sequence number and their own position, and the log sequence number (LSN) orders records across the streams. A record header also names the newest LSN that was durable when its round was built, as Pebble writes the offset its write-ahead log (WAL) had synced into every chunk header, so recovery can tell a missing record that was durable from one still in flight. A block left from an earlier use of the segment or an earlier format of the disk, or written to the wrong address, therefore never decodes as a record; since the log id is random, neither does a user's data block crafted to look like a header. The stream tag names one of the three streams and the lane byte its lane, and a write entry that names a chunk slot carries the slot's segment and index.
- **Addresses belong to the log, not the device.** Every address the format stores (an extent's location, a record header's address, a copy's source, a chunk slot's segment) is a block of the log's own address space, and segments are numbered across that space; today it is one device, block for block. That is what lets hard disk drives (HDDs) come later without rewriting an index entry. A log on an HDD alone works against the drive: every flush waits on the platter, and every turn between the lanes' open segments is a seek, so every MetaStore commit on the disk runs at HDD speed. The fix is the one Ceph BlueStore's DB device and ZFS's special vdev make: one log over two devices, its segment numbers running on from a flash partition onto the HDD, the hot and metadata streams in flash segments and the cold stream and chunk slots in HDD segments, so small writes reach the HDD only as the cleaner's sequential copies, and the driver maps a log block to its device and offset as a linear device-mapper target does. Each device's superblock then names its place in the space, a new [format version](#on-disk-format); both devices carry the one log ID, so the pair is one disk to placement and to [disk identity](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), and losing either loses the disk. The tier needs more than the split: recovery reads every segment header, up to 262,144 on a 16 TiB disk, which at 200 random reads a second is about 20 minutes; an HDD under a deep queue holds I/Os past the 1 s `member_timeout`, so the threshold must be set per drive class; and drive-managed shingled (SMR) HDDs, which stall for seconds while they rewrite overlapping tracks, must be refused at format. Until then a disk is one solid-state drive, NVMe or SATA.
- **The chunk index maps extents, and the live map never overlaps.** A chunk's keys start with `(pool, h, chunk_id)`, big-endian, so a PG's chunks are one key range. That prefix alone holds the chunk's entry: its current version and the snapshot epoch of its last freeze. Then comes its *live map*, `(…, 0, first block) → (version, location, block count, record header)`, one entry per run of consecutive blocks written by one record; a punched range has location 0 and reads as zeros. Applying a write inserts its extent and trims or splits the live entries it covers, as BlueStore's extent map and Btrfs do, removing the keys of the extents it covers whole with one range removal when there are two or more, so a lookup never steps over them (a punch of a whole chunk keeps the chunk's entry, its tombstone, and removes the live map `(…, 0, 0) .. (…, 1)` with one range removal, the bound short of version 1's epoch key, and releasing a frozen version `v` removes `(…, v) .. (…, v + 1)` the same way, except the absent version 0, whose map is empty and whose key range is the live map's, so releasing it removes no keys; frozen maps sit outside the live map's key range, so a punch never touches what a snapshot still reads), and a read of the live version costs one lookup per block, the greatest key at or below its block's (a read takes one reader per chunk, which probes the run filters once with the chunk's prefix and serves ascending blocks by stepping forward from the last one, as Pebble's `TrySeekUsingNext` and RocksDB's reseek within the current block do), and the map holds at most one entry per block, however many versions wrote it; one entry per version resolved at read time would make a read of a fragmented chunk scan every version that touched it. A version a snapshot freezes keeps the map it had under its own key, `(…, frozen version, first block)`, next to the live map, behind `(…, frozen version)`, which holds its freeze epoch (see [Versions share blocks](#on-disk-format)). Under the reserved pool 2^32 − 1 and a record header's address, after every chunk, the index counts the blocks its maps still refer to in that record, a block the live map and a frozen map share counting twice, so a record is dead to the cleaner once its count reaches zero. A cleaner move changes every map, live or frozen, that still stores the copied blocks where the cleaner read them. Trimmed entries are gone, so a primary maps a write only once it [commits](#data-layer-chunkstore), and maps a chunk's writes in version order: out of order, an older write would overwrite a newer one and a freeze would copy writes made after it. A checkpoint's replay point never passes a pending write's record, so a restart applies again the committed records after it that its runs already hold. That changes nothing: every block ends at the last record in LSN order that wrote it, as when the records were first mapped, and a freeze applied twice changes nothing. A block the cleaner moved meanwhile maps back to the record it was copied from, which is still on disk because records after the replay point stay live ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike), R1, R2). Replay and rebuild apply every logged record, so a write that [peering](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) drops must be superseded by a later record in the log of every member that logged it (R3). A chunk written whole is one live entry per record it spans: at least four, since a record holds at most `max_group_commit_bytes` (1 MiB), or about a million entries for a disk holding 1 TB of such chunks. The checksums stay in the record headers, and a read fetches its extent's header block alongside the data; hot header blocks stay cached. A chunk overwritten at random 4K holds one extent per write, and the cleaner does not regroup it ([Cleaning](#on-disk-format)), so a block volume can push a 1 TB disk toward 270 million entries, more than 10 GB, and sequential reads of such a chunk are random 4K reads, which NVMe handles well and SATA SSDs and HDDs less so.
- **The log is the source of truth.** The chunk index and each MetaStore shard's state are log-structured merge (LSM) trees inside the log. An in-memory memtable holds what was applied since the last flush. A flush writes it to the log as an immutable sorted run, in pieces of at most one record each, the last of which lists the others, and compaction merges runs into new ones. A *checkpoint* flushes the memtable of every index on the disk that holds anything, then writes a manifest. A manifest is a *snapshot*, naming each index's runs, the LSN each index replays from and its merge bounds, the whole [segment usage table](#on-disk-format) and `NextSeq`, the open sequence number the next segment will take (see Recovery), or an *edit* holding only what changed since the manifest before it: for every index the manifest keeps, the runs added and removed, its replay LSN and merge bounds (an index the edit leaves out is dropped), the usage rows that changed and `NextSeq`. This is how Pebble and RocksDB keep their MANIFEST: version edits, with a snapshot of the whole version when the file rotates. A checkpoint writes a snapshot once the edits since the last one would exceed the smaller of that snapshot and the next, Pebble's rule (`record/rotation.go`), weighing every byte a checkpoint wrote for the manifest, its piece lists included, so recovery reads at most twice a snapshot; RocksDB's default (`max_manifest_space_amp_pct` 500) writes a third less but lets recovery read six. An edit is bounded by the blocks its checkpoint's records wrote, one row per segment they changed, while a snapshot grows with the disk: on a 1 TiB disk at 90% full under 4 KiB overwrites, a full table in every manifest costs 0.42% of the log and edits plus snapshots 0.13%, and on a 16 TiB disk 6.6% against about 0.16% ([durability study](#appendix-lsm-study-durability-spike)). A manifest larger than a record is written in pieces; its last piece lists the pieces of its snapshot and of every edit since, and the segment header points at that last piece. A checkpoint is due once `max_replay_bytes` (default 64 MiB) of records follow the last manifest, which bounds replay after a crash to the records since then, and bounds the Raft entries a shard keeps in memory. If a checkpoint falls behind and the records of other producers after the newest manifest, written or in flight, reach twice `max_replay_bytes`, group commit takes items only from the checkpoint, the merges and the cleaner until a manifest is durable, and the other producers' items wait in submit order with their buffers, as Pebble stops writes at `MemTableStopWritesThreshold` and RocksDB at `max_write_buffer_number`, both two memtables by default. That makes the bound on memtables and on the Raft entries a shard keeps hard: twice `max_replay_bytes` per disk, plus the one round that crossed it. The checkpoint's own records count toward replay and toward when the next checkpoint is due, but not toward this hold: a checkpoint larger than the bound, such as a snapshot of a large usage table or a merge step, would otherwise leave as many records after its own manifest and hold writers past every manifest. Checkpoints and merges write only to the metadata stream, so a record is all ordinary or all theirs; a record that carried both would count toward the hold its header and every block other producers' entries touch, and recovery counts replayed records by that rule. So a replay after a crash reads at most twice `max_replay_bytes`, plus a round, plus what the checkpoint wrote, and recovery restores the hold from the ordinary records it replays. Group commit keeps the checkpoint's pieces and manifest in a queue of their own, metadata lane 0, so they never wait behind held items and held items never wait behind them; that is safe because a checkpoint covers only records already durable when it began. A flush costs what changed since the last one, never the whole index: a full snapshot at every checkpoint would write a 300 MB shard five times over, and a 16 GiB one 256 times over, for each 64 MiB of entries.
