Replay time adds to restart time: fjall needed 2.8 s to replay a 64 MB journal ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). If the chunk index is lost, a scan of the record headers rebuilds it. A [punch](#data-layer-chunkstore) or a `freeze` is recorded in a record header like a write, so a rebuild cannot bring punched blocks or a deleted chunk back, and knows which versions a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) pinned.
- **Recovery.** The superblock gives the geometry. It is kept twice, in the first and the last block of segment 0, so one bad block cannot make the disk unreadable. A damaged copy is rewritten from block 0. When block 0 is unreadable, or holds this format's superblock with a bad checksum, recovery takes the copy, which the disk's size locates, and rewrites block 0; a disk therefore keeps the size it was formatted with. A block 0 of zeros or of another format may mean the disk was wiped or reused, since a partition table or a new filesystem leaves the end of segment 0 alone, so recovery writes nothing and reports the copy for an operator to restore ([superblock repair](#superblock-repair)). Recovery then reads every segment header, many at a time since a disk has 4,096 to 262,144 of them, together with the block after each, which holds the record written with the header. The newest manifest that a header of this log naming its own segment points at is the one to start from (which of those segments are used follows below): recovery reads its snapshot and edits, all listed by its last piece, and applies the edits in order, which gives each index's runs and the LSN each replays from. The index owners read their runs' pieces through recovery, which then replays the records after the oldest of those LSNs in LSN order, each index taking the entries it owns. Each lane is read from the last of its segments whose first record is at or before the first record to replay, and each lane resumes appending in its newest segment. Since LSNs rise along a lane, every segment after the one it starts from holds only records to replay, and every segment before it none. Per stream the rule would miss records: a merge's segment opens before, and keeps filling after, shared metadata segments opened later, so the last metadata segment starting at or before the replay point can be a shared one, and the merge's newer records are never read. In a model of the log that missed a record past the replay point in 74% of crashes with one merge worker and 99% with four, usually `Corrupt` at every start, and a silent loss when the merge's records were the newest durable ones, which the tail rule then took for rounds in flight; per lane it missed none ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)). Every other system the study read keeps one order per log it replays: F2FS's checkpoint records where each of its active logs stood and roll-forward follows each on its own, and Pebble and RocksDB never replay compaction output, which lives outside their WAL. Merge lanes are read for their records' LSNs, which the tail rule needs, and their entries go to no index: a step's pieces count once a manifest names them. A checkpoint writes those pieces after its base, so they lie past every replay point of its manifest, and skipping merge lanes missed records in 68 to 99% of the model's crashes; reading only their header blocks would accept a torn merge record near the tail as durable. A segment header names the manifest that was newest when the segment opened, so replay can run up to a segment per lane past `max_replay_bytes` (in the model, about two segments more than strictly needed on average with one merge worker, four at most), plus the records written while the checkpoint's runs streamed, plus, for the chunk index of a disk holding replicated primaries, everything logged while its oldest pending write waited: at most `node_down_after` of writes when a member fails. Rebuild, which replays every record still in the log for an index that was lost, still reads the manifest, for its segment usage table and the size of what is left to replay. Once the cleaner has freed segments, the LSNs they held are gone for good, so a rebuild cannot check that LSNs follow one another from LSN 1 as replay does past the manifest; how it tells those gaps from damage is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

  Replay merges the lanes by LSN and stops at the first LSN it does not find. Durability is a prefix of the log and at most `commit_depth` rounds are in flight, so by the *tail rule* that missing LSN ends the log only if no record header recovery found past it names a durable LSN at or past it, every record header found past it, whole or torn, lies within the next `commit_depth` LSNs, and no segment header names a manifest at or past it: the missing record and those after it were rounds in flight, and are dropped. Otherwise the log is corrupt at that LSN, and recovery reports it instead of cutting the log short, as [Protocol-Aware Recovery](https://www.usenix.org/conference/fast18/presentation/alagappan) argues. The durable LSN proves corruption whenever a round began after the missing one was durable, which at queue depth 1 is every round, whatever `commit_depth` was; Pebble confirms WAL corruption the same way, and otherwise tolerates an unclean end only in its newest WAL. RocksDB's default, point-in-time recovery, instead opens with the prefix before any corruption; its own guard, refusing to open when a flushed table holds data past the corruption, is the manifest condition here. A replica that silently lost acknowledged writes must not rejoin as intact, so recovery never truncates on its own. A scan that finds no valid record at a block reads the rest of its segment for later headers, so a damaged header cannot hide the records behind it; this reads the unused tail of each open segment at every start. A prelude that claims more header blocks than `max_group_commit_bytes` holds is not taken for a header, so one flipped bit cannot make recovery read a whole segment at once. Two valid records with one LSN are corruption. A dropped round's record may have landed whole, and the log reuses its LSN, so a later start could find it behind the new tail and replay it. Recovery therefore zeroes the first block of every record header it found past the end of the log, then flushes, before it reports the log recovered: the flush also makes durable what a restarted process left in a volatile cache, which recovery read and replayed. A read error, or a failed write or flush, fails recovery.

  Which segments are free comes from the [segment usage table](#on-disk-format) of the manifest recovery loads, which lists every segment that was not free at the manifest's base LSN; before the first manifest, every segment is free, as at format. A segment the table quarantines stays quarantined and is not read again, whatever its header now says. A segment the cleaner frees keeps its header of this log until it is reused, TRIMmed or not, so a header of this log that names its own segment does not by itself say whether the segment was opened after the manifest or freed before it. Every manifest therefore records `NextSeq`, the open sequence number the first segment opened after its base LSN takes, and recovery judges each such header by the loaded manifest's. At `NextSeq` or more, the segment was opened after the base and is used; a row the table holds for it describes an earlier use and is dropped, and replay rebuilds its live blocks and newest LSN. Below `NextSeq`, it was opened before the base and the table decides: listed, it keeps its row's state; left out, it is free and its header stale. That holds in every crash order of freeing, reusing and TRIM, which the [log segments study](../studies/log-segments/#2-nextseq-in-the-manifest) lists. `NextSeq` is one past the sequence of the newest segment a durable round had opened at the base LSN, kept as rounds retire, and not the log's next sequence number when the checkpoint begins: a round in flight then may already have opened a segment, and a crash that keeps the round would leave durable, possibly acknowledged records in a segment below `NextSeq` that the table does not list, which recovery would free. Before the first manifest `NextSeq` is 1, so every header of this log is used. Pebble and RocksDB judge their files the same way, by the next file number each manifest records, as F2FS judges node blocks by checkpoint version and NILFS2 and BSD LFS segment summaries by a serial that must continue the checkpoint's.

  A segment the table leaves out whose header reads as zeros, or as another log's (the disk was formatted again), was never written by this log, or was trimmed, and is free. Block 1 of a segment under such a header, or under a stale one, can still hold the record of a round in flight that opened the segment again and whose segment header never landed: a record whose sequence is `NextSeq` or more. Recovery erases that record and opens later segments with a higher sequence number than it carries, so no record left in the segment can match the place of a new one; the segment stays free, or used while the table still lists it. An older record at block 1 is left from the segment's earlier use and is ignored, so a freed segment, which on a disk that is a file keeps its old records, is not read in full at every start. A header damaged to zeros over a segment opened after the base LSN looks the same, so a segment whose block 1 holds a record of this log at `NextSeq` or more that its header does not match is read in full, and every record header found there counts for the tail rule: a lone round in flight is dropped, and more records are corruption. A round appends to a segment only once the round that opened it is durable, which keeps block 1 the only place such a record can be; with `commit_depth` 1 the rule never waits. A segment is damaged if its header is otherwise unreadable or names another segment, or if the table lists it and its header reads as zeros or another log's, and recovery reads each damaged segment in full. A segment header fits in one sector, so a torn write leaves it whole or as it was, and such a header is damage, not a crash. If a damaged segment holds a record header of this log past the replay point, which may have been acknowledged, the log is corrupt at that record, and recovery writes nothing. Otherwise it is quarantined if the table lists it or it holds any record header of this log: never reused, and reported for repair from a replica, as NILFS2 never allocates a segment marked erroneous; the next manifest's table keeps it quarantined. A damaged segment the table leaves out that holds no record of this log is free. Every record the table counts lies before the manifest, so if replay cannot read one, replay stops short of the manifest's LSN and the tail rule reports the gap as corruption, in a rebuild too. Recovery hands group commit the table it loaded plus the records it replayed past the base LSN. Format writes only the superblock, so a raw device that held other data must be discarded or zeroed first, or every start reads its unused segments in full. Nothing on disk records `commit_depth`, so the window uses the configured one: lowering it across a crash can report a torn tail as corruption; raising it lets a torn header one LSN further pass, but no whole record that a later header names durable.
- **The LSM tree** is a memtable, immutable sorted runs and compaction, from the first commit, behind one interface (`internal/lsm`). The memtable is a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages: a page holds a slot array and its entries, pages refer to each other by `uint32` page number, and the pages sit in slabs outside the Go heap, so the garbage collector neither scans them nor counts them toward its heap goal ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go); until the event loops' slabs exist they are pointer-free Go slices, a [tech debt](tech-debts.md)). A tree of one heap allocation per entry cost a quarter to a third of the CPU in collection at 10 to 20 million files; the page tree costs under 3 ms of CPU per collection at 10 and 20 million files, with pauses under 1 ms, and answers gets fastest ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike)). A removal is an entry that hides the key in older runs, so a memtable never drops a key, and every inner key stays equal to the smallest key below it. A *range removal* hides every key from one key up to another, as RocksDB's and Pebble's `DeleteRange` do. A memtable keeps its range removals as non-overlapping fragments, `start → end`, in a second copy-on-write page tree in the same page store, as both engines keep theirs in a second skiplist, so a view reaches them through its root and each operation is one search. Removing a range turns the memtable's own entries in it into removals and merges the range with the fragments it overlaps or abuts; a later write cuts its key out of its fragment. Removing an empty range (`lo ≥ hi`) is an error and stores nothing. A fragment so hides the memtable's older entries as well as older runs'. Without sequence numbers a fragment's age is its layer's: it hides every older layer and none of its own layer's entries, which is Pebble's rule for an ingested table, whose points and range deletions share one sequence number. A get stops at the first layer holding the key or a fragment over it. A floor keeps each layer's candidate and, when a newer layer's fragment covers the best one, moves every older layer below the fragment's start in one search. A scan moves older layers past the fragment's end, keeping each layer's current fragment until the scan passes its end, as Pebble's merging iterator does. Without range removals, a chunk rewritten whole over 1,024 one-block extents cost 200 to 730 µs per lookup until a merge reached the old extents' run, which size tiering had not done after 192 GiB of further writes; with them, about two searches ([LSM runs spike](#appendix-lsm-runs-spike)). A full leaf hands entries to a neighbour before it splits, since pages filled in create order are 63% full against 98% in key order; with the neighbour, leaves filled in random order are 80% full, and the MetaStore spike's file model takes 199 B per file in the memtable. A split needs each entry, with its 2-byte slot, within half of a page's 4,080 bytes past the header, so key plus value is at most 2,033 bytes: a key is at most 1,032 bytes (`MaxKey`, an S3 key's 1,024 bytes plus its 5-byte prefix, rounded to 8) and a value 1,000 (`MaxValue`). A range removal's fragment stores both its bounds, so the MetaStore refuses a range bound over 1,000 bytes before proposing, and stores a value over 1,000 bytes as [pieces](#metadata-layer-metastore). A write that cuts its key out of a fragment can leave a bound one byte longer than its key, too long for a fragment; the memtable then keeps the fragment whole and lets its own entries win over its fragments from then on, as a run's do, which reads the same at the cost of the shortcut. An entry that would not fit a run piece on its own is refused when it is written.

  A flush freezes the memtable, starts an empty one and streams the frozen one into the log as a sorted run, one piece at a time, each built at its final size and handed over once the one before is durable, so its peak is one piece above the frozen tree and the run it becomes; the frozen pages are freed once the run is durable and no view holds them. A piece is the format version, one block in LevelDB's format, which Pebble keeps (entries prefix-compressed against the one before, a restart every 16 entries storing its key whole, the restart offsets at the block's end), then a trailer: the run's last piece, its *root*, lists the earlier pieces, as a manifest's last piece does, so the manifest names a run by its root alone; every piece ends with a kind byte, the run's sequence number and the count of references before it. A run's range removals follow its entries as blocks of their own, `start → end` sorted by start, which the trailer marks; a run's own entries win over its fragments, since a merge unites its inputs'. Lookups search those blocks in place, as they do entry blocks. With the trailer read from the end, a piece is filled before the writer knows it is the last; when the root's references no longer fit beside the last block, an empty root follows. A root in a 1 MiB record lists about 58,000 pieces, so a run holds up to about 57 GiB, a piece falling short of full by at most one entry. A checkpoint names again the runs already durable and writes only the frozen memtable and runs a compaction made since; one that fails keeps the frozen memtable, and the next writes it again from its first piece. A flush checks that the run and what it dropped hold as many entries and fragments as the frozen memtable, and a merge that its output and what it dropped add up to its inputs, as RocksDB does by default (`flush_verify_memtable_count`, `compaction_verify_record_count`); to count them, a flush or merge steps through the entries a range removal hides instead of seeking past them, as RocksDB's and Pebble's compactions do, so a merge under a wide range removal costs as much as one of the same bytes without it. Each piece in memory keeps the CRC32C of its bytes, since runs are not read back from disk, where Pebble and RocksDB verify every compaction input: a merge verifies its inputs before reading them and each block of an older run it reads to decide whether to keep a removal, and a flush each block of a merge's output it writes, so a flipped bit never reaches the log under a fresh checksum. It costs 4 bytes per piece and two CRC32C passes over the merged bytes, one over the inputs and one over the output, 12% of a merge's CPU on arm64, where Go's CRC32C runs at 359 ns per 4 KiB. A failed check fails the flush or merge, and the index is rebuilt from the log, a merge's installed steps with it; the disk's other indexes keep serving meanwhile. A MetaStore shard replica's log is truncated behind its flushes, so it rebuilds instead by dropping its state and catching up from a Raft snapshot, as a new replica does.

  Runs stay in memory, pointer-free, as the blocks of their pieces, each run with its last key and a Bloom filter. Each block's first key and restart keys are its fence pointers, so searching a run binary-searches its blocks and one block's restarts, and never the disk, where a cold lookup costs 0.15 to 1.4 ms ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). A search costs about 0.3 µs for a key the run holds and 0.6 µs for a miss or a floor ([LSM runs spike](#appendix-lsm-runs-spike)), and a 16 GiB shard keeps up to about 16 runs, so a lookup must not search them all. As every RocksDB and Pebble table does, each run therefore carries a filter that rules out most runs that cannot hold a key. A lookup checks the memtable, then probes each run's filter, newest first, and searches only the runs the filter lets through and whose first and last keys bracket the key. A filter holds a run's entries, never its fragments, and a run's first and last keys include its fragments' bounds, so a lookup checks each run's fragments for the key whatever its filter says, and stops at a covering one: RocksDB twice returned keys a `DeleteRange` had removed because a prefix filter skipped the table holding the tombstone.

  The filter is a Bloom filter whose probes all fall in one 64-byte cache line, in RocksDB's full-filter format, which Pebble writes too; its code is ported from Pebble's `bloom` package with its tests. At 10 bits per key it gives about 1.2% false positives. It is filled as the run streams out and sized from what the flush or merge knows in advance, the frozen memtable's entry count or the sum of its inputs' filter key counts, so no key's hash is held, and it is written after the run's entries and range removals, in pieces of its own that the trailer marks, so a restore loads it rather than hashing every key again. Each index names what its filter holds, as RocksDB's prefix extractor does; a prefix enters a filter once however many keys share it, and a filter's bytes start with what it holds and how many keys, which a merge sizes its output from. A restore refuses a run whose filter holds other keys than its tree's configuration names, since probing it would rule out keys the run holds, so changing what an index's filters hold means rebuilding the index from the log. An index's prefix must be closed, as RocksDB's prefix extractor must: every key that starts with a prefix has that prefix, and a key too short for one has none; otherwise a prefix lookup would skip runs holding keys it should return. The chunk index's prefix is a fixed 28 bytes, and the MetaStore's inode prefix is fixed-width because its integers are. A lookup hashes its key or prefix once and probes every run's filter with that hash. Each filter's checksum sits with its run's piece checksums, so a flipped bit fails the next merge that reads the run or flush that rewrites it, as a block's does. The chunk index's reads are floors inside one chunk's keys, which a whole-key filter cannot serve, so its filter holds each chunk's prefix `(pool, h, chunk_id)`, one key per chunk rather than per extent, and a floor inside a prefix probes it, as RocksDB's `SeekForPrev` does in prefix mode. The MetaStore's filter holds whole keys and each key's inode prefix `fs/{volume}/{inode}`, which serve gets and scans of one inode's keys such as `readdir`. Over 24 runs, a MetaStore lookup of an absent name then costs 1.9 µs instead of 13 µs, and a chunk-index read of a chunk written whole 5 µs instead of 32 µs, searching 1.1 runs; the filters take 0.8% of the chunk index's run bytes and 2.9% of the MetaStore's ([LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike)). Fewer, larger runs would not do as well: six unfiltered runs already cost 4.3 µs for the miss, and a cap on runs needs leveled merges into the largest run, which rewrite entries several times as often. A get that finds its key gains little, since searching a run that lacks the key is cheap when keys arrive in order, as inode numbers do. A block volume's chunk index under random overwrites still searches about 7 of 24 runs, since every large run holds every hot chunk. Filters stay on the oldest run: RocksDB's `optimize_filters_for_hits` and Pebble's default reads leave that one out to avoid reading large filter blocks from disk, but these filters are in memory, and misses (a `create`'s name check, a read of unwritten blocks) are common. A scan merges its layers with a min-heap ordered by key and then age, as Pebble's and RocksDB's merging iterators do, so a step costs a sift through log₂ N sources rather than a comparison with each: over 240,000 keys, a step takes 37 ns over 6 runs and 54 ns over 24 on an Apple M4 Pro, and 44 and 65 ns on an Intel Core Ultra 5 235U, where comparing every source at every step took 56 and 165 ns (`BenchmarkIterNextRuns`; the [LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike) measured that linear merge at 95 and 360 ns).

  A run takes 133 B per file of the spike's file model, against 199 B in the memtable, as the [MetaStore memory spike](#appendix-metastore-memory-spike)'s blocks of 16 entries did (135 B, F4), plus about 3.5 B of filter. Most of an index sits in runs, so a 16 GiB budget holds roughly 120 million file replicas, 40 million distinct files per node at three replicas, plus about 125 B per xattr.

  Compaction follows RocksDB's universal compaction, whose sorted runs are this tree's: each spans the whole key range, they are ordered newest first, and a merge takes only runs adjacent in age and puts its output in their place. Entries therefore carry no sequence number; the run's sequence number, in every piece, orders the runs. Once an index holds four runs, counting those a running merge holds as RocksDB counts runs being compacted, the picker tries three rules in order, as RocksDB's `UniversalCompactionBuilder::PickCompaction` does:

  - **Size amplification** merges every run once the runs newer than the oldest hold 200% of its bytes.
  - **Size ratio** walks from the newest run, takes the next one while it is at most 1% larger than the runs taken so far, and merges the first span of two or more.
  - **Run limit.** If neither rule applies and the index holds more runs than its limit, the newest runs beyond the limit merge whatever their sizes.

  Each rule considers only runs no merge holds, and a rule whose span would make a run larger than a root can list picks nothing, so the picker tries the next rule rather than cutting the span short.

  The limit is RocksDB's auto-tuned `max_read_amp`: the number of runs a size-ratio tree needs from 1 MiB up to the largest run, counted as RocksDB counts it (one run of 1 MiB, then each next run 1% larger than all before it, until one reaches the largest run), about 2 + log2(largest run ÷ 1 MiB): 8 for a 44 MB chunk index, 13 for a 1.6 GB shard and 16 for 16 GiB. An index may set a fixed limit instead, at the cost of bytes rewritten: four runs cost 100 times the flushed bytes on a 1.6 GB index under steady flushes, because the second-oldest run absorbs every flush, and 9 to 33 times under the other flush sequences; eight cost 4 to 7 times. Cassandra's size-tiered buckets need runs of similar size side by side: over runs adjacent in age they merged only while flushes were steady, and left one run per flush when checkpoint flushes alternated between user writes and a cleaner pass's moves, came in bursts, or spread over a range of sizes. Universal compaction kept 2.3 to 6.7 runs on average under all of these, rewrote 2.3 to 6.3 times the flushed bytes, and its runs took 1.3 to 1.7 times the live index, in memory as well as on disk ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)). A size-amplification merge writes the whole index, which is why merges install their output in steps.

  A flush or a merge drops a removal, or a fragment, unless a run older than what it writes holds a key it hides, since there is nothing else for it to hide, and drops the removals a fragment it keeps covers. Removals arrive in key order, so each older run is checked with a cursor that only moves forward, as RocksDB's compaction walks deeper levels' files; a flush builds its pieces on the owner's loop, where a fresh search of every older run per removal would cost about 0.6 µs per run. RocksDB keeps every removal through a flush and Pebble drops one only where no deeper file's key bounds overlap it; these runs span the whole key space, so only an exact check drops anything before the oldest run is merged. A range removal over a large key range, such as a dropped filesystem volume's `fs/{volume}/` keys, frees nothing until a merge reaches the runs holding them, which under universal compaction can wait for a size-amplification merge. Pebble's delete-only compactions drop the tables a range deletion covers with a manifest edit; the equivalent here, each older run dropping the blocks a fragment covers whole after a checkpoint and keeping its place, freed 22 of a dropped volume's 28 MB at the first checkpoint ([LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)), but pays only once a run's root can name pieces it shares with the run it replaces, which is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions): written again whole, the kept blocks cost 85.6 MB, as much as the merge it saved. The merge reads only its immutable inputs, so it runs on a worker goroutine like the [cleaner](#on-disk-format). The picker skips runs a running merge holds, so a small merge can run beside a large one, and one scheduler per disk runs its indexes' merges, `merge_workers` of them at once (default 1), the index whose runs most exceed its limit in proportion first: its score is its runs no merge holds divided by its limit, as RocksDB scores universal compaction (`version_set.cc`), leaving out runs being compacted. A merge installs its output in *steps*, as ScyllaDB's incremental compaction does: a step merges up to `compaction_step_bytes` (default 64 MiB) of output, the index's owner appends its pieces to the output run and raises each input's *bound* to the step's last key, and the next checkpoint, a manifest alone if none is due, writes the pieces, the output's root and a manifest edit holding the bounds. A run holds only its keys above its bound, its fragments clipped to it, so the output and its inputs never hold the same key and need no sequence number to say which is newer, and an input piece wholly below the bound is dead once that manifest is durable; the piece across it stays whole, as a Pebble virtual sstable keeps its backing file. A merge so holds at most one step beyond what the manifests name. Installed whole, it held all its output until the next manifest: 65 to 109 segments of 64 MiB for a MetaStore index of 2 to 6.25% of a 256 GiB disk, up to the whole index, 258 segments, for a size-amplification merge, and the 69 segments that ran the [cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)'s disk out of its 8-segment reserve; in steps, 2 segments, for at most 0.6% more manifest bytes per flushed byte ([LSM space spike](#appendix-lsm-space-spike)). Pebble and RocksDB free a compaction's inputs only when it ends and bound its size instead, at 25 target files, which suits levels cut into files but would leave whole-key-space runs unmerged; RocksDB's experimental incremental universal compaction slices a size-amplification merge by key range for the same reason. Input blocks below the bound leave memory too, unless a view holds them, so a merge's memory peak is also a step. A restart finds the bounded inputs and the partial output in the manifest and resumes the merge from the bound. Merge output fills its worker's lane of the metadata stream (see [Three streams](#on-disk-format)), so the steps of a later merge free whole segments: in the shared open segment, consumed input pieces died among live flush pieces and 30 to 57 segments stayed stranded instead of 8 to 10 in a segment per merge. A lane per worker also puts one merge's tail and the next merge's head in one segment, which that spike did not measure (an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). A reader or a Raft snapshot takes a point-in-time view, the memtable's root page plus the current runs: while a view is open, a write copies each page it changes that a view can reach, and a page copied away is freed when the last view that could reach it closes. Views are read on the owner's loop, since the page store grows under writes. A restart loads the runs and replays the records after them.

  A block cache that reads runs from the log instead of memory comes when an index outgrows memory. The fragmented index of a block volume does, so the cache lands before the block frontend at the latest; a MetaStore shard does once a node holds more files than its memory, about 40 million distinct files per 16 GiB at three replicas (above), and brings it sooner if file workloads get there first. Until the cache lands, every index lives in memory whole, so each node reports its indexes' memory against its memory limit, and `rock-solid node status` warns from 80%. Those runs cut each piece into data blocks of 4 to 32 KiB, so a miss reads one block rather than a 1 MiB piece (RocksDB and Pebble default to 4 KiB), and each piece's filter is a partition of its own. Fence pointers and filters stay in memory outside the cache, as RocksDB keeps them by default (`cache_index_and_filter_blocks = false`). The cache is one CLOCK cache over off-heap buffers and belongs to the index's owner loop, so it needs neither the per-CPU shards of Pebble's Clock-PRO cache nor the lock-free table of RocksDB's HyperClockCache, which exist to serve many threads.
- **Versions share blocks.** An overwrite appends only the blocks it writes. When a write carries a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) epoch newer than that of the chunk's newest write the primary has accepted, pending writes included, the primary puts a `freeze` entry for the chunk's current version in the same record. Every member copies the live map under that version's key before it applies the write. The frozen version so keeps every block the write did not touch and shares it with the live version, without a reference count per block; the copy costs one entry per live extent of the chunk, once per snapshot per written chunk. A chunk with no version yet freezes version 0, the *absent* chunk: its frozen map is empty, so a snapshot reads the chunk as never written, and its snapshot epoch tells later writes in that snapshot epoch not to freeze again. Record entries carry no epoch, so without this a chunk first written after a cut would look written before it: its next write would freeze a version the cut never held, and that snapshot and every older one would read it ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike), E1 to E3). Ceph keeps the same fact in each object's `SnapSet`, which every write updates. The absent freeze costs one 37-byte entry per chunk created in a volume that has had a snapshot, where a snapshot epoch in every write entry would cost 8 bytes on each, and the record format already encodes it. A read at a snapshot picks the frozen version whose freeze epoch is the smallest at or above the snapshot's, since the writes after a snapshot's cut carry its epoch, or the live map when there is none.
- **Cleaning.** A block is live while the live version, a snapshot or an unflattened clone still maps to it. A Raft entry is dead once the memtable that applied it has been flushed, and a sorted run once compaction has replaced it. A manifest, the snapshot and edits its last piece lists, the runs it names and every record after its replay points stay live until a segment header naming a newer manifest is durable. A freed segment keeps its header until it is reused, which recovery tells from a new one by `NextSeq` (see Recovery), and may be reused at once: a reused segment's new header is at or past the loaded manifest's `NextSeq`, so recovery takes its row from replay, and the cleaner's copy records move whatever the older manifest's runs still map into it. That holds under two conditions: the cleaner frees no segment holding what the newest manifest named by a durable segment header still needs (its pieces, the runs it names and the records after its replay points, which recovery reads before replay starts), and no index's replay LSN passes a copy record whose moves its runs do not hold, as for a pending write. Judged against the newest durable manifest or the index in memory instead, the first is not enough. F2FS keeps a freed segment *prefree*, not reusable, until its next checkpoint is written; here that would remove only the crash order of a segment reused before any header names the freeing manifest, at the price of a state and a wait in allocation, and would still need `NextSeq`. The cleaner never copies a manifest piece: a segment holding one waits for the next snapshot, which a checkpoint writes early when the cleaner asks for that segment. The cleaner picks segments by cost-benefit (free space × age ÷ copy cost, as in the original log-structured file system), copies their live contents grouped by chunk, and queues the freed segments for discard (TRIM). Grouping keeps the blocks of a chunk that die together side by side, which wrote 6 to 7% less than copying in log order under hot/cold overwrites. It does not regroup a chunk overwritten at random: a pass copies about 0.2 blocks of any one chunk on a 1 TB disk, and merging moved extents with their neighbours removed at most 0.7% of the index's entries under uniform overwrites ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). Two guards keep it from livelocking, which the [cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike) saw without them: a victim must free more space than copying it costs, header blocks included, and age counts up to one disk capacity of writes at most, so old, nearly full segments such as snapshot data stop looking attractive. On skewed overwrites, cost-benefit with two streams writes 36 to 45% less than greedy cleaning; on uniform ones it writes 3 to 14% more. With headers counted, perfect hot/cold separation is only 1.1 to 1.6× below two streams, no separation policy simulated beats two streams on every workload, and none of WiscKey, RocksDB's BlobDB, Titan or Pebble separates its cleaner's output by temperature. The cleaner's moves cause most of the chunk index's writes near full, 55, 76 and 92% of its entries at 80, 90 and 95% full under uniform overwrites, yet the index's runs add only 0.2 to 1.0 blocks per user block, about 6% of the total at 90% ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). TRIMs are paced, one per segment at `trim_rate` (default 128 MiB/s, CockroachDB's file deletion rate), and run faster only to clear a backlog older than 5 minutes within 5 minutes, as Pebble's delete pacer does, because some SSDs stall on large bursts of discards; RocksDB paces file deletion the same way and truncates large files 64 MiB at a time. Unlike deleting a file, a TRIM frees no space in the log, since the segment is already free in the usage table, so low space never speeds it up, and a TRIM lost at a crash costs only the discard. A freed segment's TRIM is queued only once a segment header naming the freeing manifest is durable: until then the manifest recovery loads still lists the segment, and a listed segment whose header reads as zeros is damage. A segment allocated again drops its TRIM if it is still queued and waits for one already issued, as F2FS does, so a late discard cannot erase new records. A discard that does not read back as zeros would leave an undecodable header, which recovery reads in full at every start, so on such a drive the freed segment's header is zeroed instead. The io_uring driver discards with `BLOCK_URING_CMD_DISCARD` (Linux 6.12) or `BLKDISCARD` on a worker goroutine on older kernels, and SPDK with a Dataset Management deallocate. A disk that is a file ignores TRIM, since punching holes would undo writing the file in full.
- **The cleaner runs beside the writer, not on it.** Its copying runs on a worker goroutine, never on the event loop that owns the disk's chunk index and serves its writes. Inline, cleaning cost that loop 5.9 to 11 µs per write at 90 to 95% full and gave its writes a p99 of 250 to 540 µs; with a worker, 1.6 to 1.8 µs and about 6 µs ([parallel cleaner spike](#appendix-parallel-cleaner-spike)). The worker checks liveness, verifies each block's CRC32C and copies the block into a record of `copy` entries, each naming the location it copied from, then sends its moves to the index's owner. Its reads skip the header-block cache, as Pebble's blob file rewrites skip its block cache, since readers move to the copies. The owner, replay and a rebuild all apply it only where a map, live or frozen, still points at that location, so a write that lands during cleaning wins in every order the records are applied; Titan's garbage collector guards its write-back the same way, comparing the key's current blob index with the one it copied. The owner frees a victim only after all its moves are applied. A segment holding moves not yet applied is not a victim candidate. Applying costs the owner 42 to 72 ns per move, about one index entry per moved block, and the index stays single-writer. WiscKey and Titan relocate values the same way. Pebble instead rewrites a blob file behind a stable file ID and a remap inside the new file, so its on-disk sstables never change, and RocksDB moves values only inside compactions, which rewrite their index entries anyway. Neither pays here, because the index is in memory: a remap per original record wrote 19 to 33× fewer index entries than moves, but sent 63 to 96% of reads through an extra lookup and forced copies into log order, which costs hot/cold overwrites 6 to 7% more write amplification, and relocating in compaction would rewrite the whole index, since the entries of the oldest data sit in its largest run ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). A disk gets one worker, and a second once free segments fall to 14, before writes slow, as RocksDB and Pebble add compaction threads under a backlog: an idle extra worker costs CPU and speeds nothing up.
- **Writers slow down before the log runs out.** Free segments outside the reserve play the part of RocksDB's and Pebble's level 0: the cleaner keeps `clean_free` of them (default 16), and the disk's owner reads how many are left every round, with time from `ctx.Now`. At RocksDB's level-0 proportions (compactions at 4 files, more compaction threads at 8, slower writes at 20, stopped writes at 36), a second cleaner worker joins at 14 free segments, chunk writes slow at 8, and at none every ordinary item waits. A slowed disk admits chunk write blocks through a token bucket refilled at the rate the cleaner measurably frees space, net of what it copies, averaged over one-second windows and scaled from 1 down to 0.5 as free segments fall from 8 to none, as CockroachDB's admission control hands out write tokens from the bytes compacted out of level 0 (and sets Pebble's own level-0 stop to 1,000 sublevels). RocksDB instead slows to a fixed rate it multiplies by 0.8 or 1.25 as the backlog grows or shrinks, and Pebble only stops. Raft entries, punches and freezes are not charged: they are small, and punches free space. A chunk write the bucket refuses stays with its connection, which the loop stops reading until the bucket refills, so clients see TCP backpressure, as from the capped replication queues ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)). Waiting items keep their buffers and are written in submit order once a segment frees; a disk the cleaner cannot free gets `NoSpace`. The same wait applies once the records after the newest manifest reach twice `max_replay_bytes` (see [The log is the source of truth](#on-disk-format)). An index over its run limit only costs lookups, so run count never stops writes, unlike Pebble's and RocksDB's level 0.
- **Free space is recorded, and some of it is reserved.** The manifests carry a segment usage table as of their base LSN: one row per segment that is not free, with its state (used, slot, for a [chunk slot](#on-disk-format) segment, or quarantined), its live blocks and the LSN of its newest record, 17 bytes a row: under 136 KiB on disks under 256 GiB, which have 4,096 to 8,191 segments, and 272 KiB per TiB above. A snapshot holds every row; an edit holds the rows that changed since the manifest before it, a freed segment as a row in state free, and group commit marks the changed segments in a bitmap that a failed checkpoint keeps. The log counts every data block of a durable record as live, since header blocks are dead to the cleaner, and a used segment that rounds recovery dropped left empty counts none. A segment is free because the table says so, never because its header is unreadable, and an index rebuild reads the table to tell a segment the cleaner freed from a damaged one, as F2FS and the original log-structured file system do; its rule for the LSN gaps that freed segments leave is still open (see Recovery). A few segments stay in reserve for the work that frees space: checkpoints, the cleaner and merge steps, each of which frees at least what it writes once its manifest is durable. A merge worker therefore hands the log its next step only once the manifest naming its previous one is durable, so the reserve never holds more than one step per worker. The reserve holds a checkpoint at its largest, twice `max_replay_bytes` since producers wait beyond that, one step per merge worker and one cleaner pass: ⌈(2 × `max_replay_bytes` + workers × `compaction_step_bytes`) ÷ segment⌉ + victims per pass, and at least 8, the reserve the cleaner spikes ran with. With 4 victims per pass, as in those spikes, the formula gives 7 segments on a disk of 64 MiB segments with one merge worker, so 8 it is; 10 on a 255 GiB disk of 32 MiB segments; and 772 of 4,096 on a 1 GiB disk of 256 KiB segments. The log works the reserve out itself, at format and at every open, from its settings and the segment size its superblock records, since whoever opens a disk learns its geometry only once recovery has read that superblock; a setting can name a reserve directly, and only tests run with none. A disk whose reserve would take every segment, such as a 64 MiB disk of 16 KiB segments at the default settings (12,292 of 4,096), is refused at format and at open with a bad-settings error rather than run with a smaller reserve; a lower `max_replay_bytes` makes it fit. An ordinary item never opens a segment while free segments number the reserve or fewer, and checkpoints and merges write to the metadata stream, whose segments an ordinary item never shares, so the reserve is spent in whole segments only on work that frees space. RocksDB's `SstFileManager` reserves the same three things, the largest write buffer, each running compaction's inputs and `compaction_buffer_size`, and ZFS's slop space and Btrfs's global reserve keep room for freeing space the same way. Ordinary writes wait while only the reserve is free ([writers slow down](#on-disk-format)), and get `NoSpace` only when the disk's owner reports that the cleaner has no victim that passes its net-gain guard. A checkpoint that finds no segment free at all, the reserve included, waits the same way, and the same report fails it (`CheckpointFailed` with `NoSpace`) together with the items held behind it, which bounds that wait: a reserve can run out when quarantined segments or settings below the formula shrink it, so this is an event the owner handles, not a broken invariant.
- **Segment count matters, segment size does not.** At a fixed number of segments, write amplification is the same from 1 to 256 MiB per segment. What drives it near full is the share of the disk taken by the segments the cleaner keeps free: a reserve of 8 segments costs nothing on 4,096 segments, but on 256 it raised write amplification at 95% full from 17 to 70. So a segment is min(64 MiB, disk size ÷ 4096), rounded down to a power of two: 64 MiB on disks of 256 GiB and up, with 0.5 MiB of segment metadata per TiB. Large segments, written sequentially and TRIMmed whole, also suit the drive's own garbage collection.
- **Headers are capacity.** Header blocks are dead to the cleaner, so near full they cost far more than their bytes: at 95% full, the headers of 64 KiB records with 32-byte entries raise write amplification from 11.4 to 17.3, and at 90% full, queue-depth-1 4K writes, one header block per data block, double it. The header encoding is part of the format and must stay compact: a write or punch entry takes 33 bytes, 8 of them the [version](#data-layer-chunkstore) (its PG's 32-bit interval number over a 32-bit counter per chunk), a freeze 37, the 8 after the version being the snapshot epoch, a write to a chunk slot 6 more for its slot, and a cleaner's copy 8 more for the location it copied from, plus 4 bytes of CRC per data block. How full a disk may get is the pool's [`write_limit`](#volume-size-and-capacity).
- **Whole blocks are the floor.** A record's chunk data is whole blocks, written by the primary's [merge](#data-layer-chunkstore) where a write covers a block in part, so a file of 1 to 4 KiB takes one block per copy, as in XFS, BlueStore since Pacific and Vitastor: 12,399 raw bytes in `rep3` with its entry and CRC, 8.1 times a 1.5 KiB file, and as much in an erasure-coded pool, where such a file [stays as copies](#when-chunks-are-encoded). Per copy it also costs about 108 bytes of chunk index (a chunk entry and one extent) beside the inode's 133 bytes in the MetaStore. A fsync'd append of 100 bytes rewrites its tail block: alone on the disk, a header block and a data block, 8.3 KiB per copy (83 times the bytes); with 8 appenders sharing each round, about 2 blocks per append; one appender among 15 overwriters pays about the log's write amplification instead, 4.9 to 8.4 blocks per append at 80 to 90% full, since its short-lived block dies among live ones ([sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)). That is about what a local filesystem writes per fsync. No layout below a block pays for itself yet: inline data in the inode would put file bytes in the MetaStore's runs, which live in memory, 12.5 times the per-file budget for a 1.5 KiB file; byte-granular patch entries help only when their bytes ride in header blocks, which would then hold live data; and storing a file's last block short, only its bytes up to the end of file, saves 2.6 times on a 1.5 KiB file for a stored length in every write entry and extent and liveness counted in bytes. The last two are [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions); old write entries stay valid under either, so neither needs a migration.
- **Upgrades.** Segment headers, record headers, sorted runs and manifests carry a format version. Until the first release no disk holds data worth keeping, so the format is version 1 and changes in place, its golden fixtures rewritten; from the first release on, every change takes a new version. A daemon reads the current and the previous format, but writes the new one only after the operator raises the cluster's format level (a `ctl/` key) once every node runs the new release. A node on an older release that heartbeats after the level was raised is not marked up, and a condition names it, as Ceph's monitor ignores the boot of an OSD below its minimum release. Until the level is raised, rolling back is safe. The cleaner rewrites old segments over time.

**Why one log.** For chunk data the alternative is a journal plus in-place data (Vitastor, BlueStore's deferred writes): a small write is journaled, acknowledged and copied into place later, big writes must skip the journal or media bandwidth doubles, and sharing blocks with snapshots needs a per-chunk extent map with reference counts, BlueStore's most complex part. For indexes, shards and Raft logs the usual choice is an embedded key-value engine, which opens and flushes files through the kernel itself: it cannot run on a drive SPDK owns, the simulator cannot crash it, and its flushes, one per Raft group if each keeps its own log, come on top of the chunk log's. One log gives one sequential write and one flush per durable write, shared by every shard on the disk, and snapshot block sharing, checksums and torn-write detection come with the format. This is value separation as WiscKey introduced it and RocksDB's BlobDB, TiKV's Titan and Pebble ship it: the index holds locations, the data stays in a log, and a cleaner copies live data and updates the index ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)). It also rules out Raft libraries that do their own storage I/O. The price is the cleaner and the LSM tree, and the cleaner's write amplification, which grows as a disk fills.

#### Superblock repair

A block 0 that does not decode is not always damage. A disk can be reused without the copy at the end of segment 0 being cleared: a partition table (`parted`, `sgdisk`), a new filesystem, an LVM or BlueStore label, or a partial wipe with `wipefs` or `dd` all write at the start of the disk, and the copy sits up to 64 MiB in. Device names such as `/dev/nvme1n1` can also change across reboots, so a node can be pointed at another disk that once held a log. Writing the copy back over such a block 0 destroys the new owner's data. The failures the copy is for are a block 0 the drive cannot read (NVMe reports uncorrectable media as an error, not as flipped bits) and a block 0 written torn or rotted in place, which keeps this format's magic and fails its checksum. So what block 0 holds decides:

| Block 0 | Recovery |
| --- | --- |
| A valid superblock | Uses it, and rewrites the copy if the copy differs |
| Unreadable, or this format's magic and version with a bad checksum or inconsistent contents | Uses the copy if it is valid and sits where its own geometry puts it, and rewrites block 0 |
| Zeros, another magic or another format version | Writes nothing. Reports `CopyOnly` when the copy is valid, else `NotFormatted` |

Restoring the copy over a block 0 of the last row, or over a bit flip inside the magic or version, is an operator's decision, through a repair command. Other systems split the same way. ext4, XFS and Btrfs mount from the primary superblock only and leave their copies to offline tools (`e2fsck -b`, `xfs_repair`, `btrfs rescue super-recover`, which asks before it writes). The Linux kernel reads the backup GPT header only when a protective MBR in block 0 still marks the disk as GPT, and never writes it ([`block/partitions/efi.c`](https://android.googlesource.com/kernel/common/+/e6cfc1098ef0/block/partitions/efi.c)). Ceph BlueStore refuses to start an OSD whose label copies disagree and repairs them only through `ceph-bluestore-tool repair`; stale copies left by `sgdisk --zap-all` and `wipefs` have kept reused disks from starting ([IBM note](https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/node/7242545)). ZFS rewrites its labels automatically, and has rewritten them over a disk reformatted with UFS ([FreeBSD bug 204622](https://bugs.freebsd.org/bugzilla/show_bug.cgi?id=204622)). mdadm's superblocks near the end of the device are called dangerous with auto-detection by the [kernel RAID wiki](https://raid.wiki.kernel.org/index.php/RAID_superblock_formats).

### Pools and redundancy

Redundancy is set per **pool**: a named set of placement groups that share one redundancy scheme, one failure-domain rule and, optionally, one class of disk. Every block volume and RWX filesystem belongs to one pool, named by its StorageClass:

```yaml
kind: StorageClass
metadata: { name: footage }
parameters: { pool: ec4-2 }
```

Pools are cluster objects (a `Pool` custom resource, stored under `ctl/pool/` in the control-plane group). Installation creates `rep3` as the default pool. Every pool setting is the user's choice; the defaults suit a small team that wants its data protected but watches cost. They never lower how many failures data survives, and otherwise pick the option that uses less space.

| Setting | Default | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `replicas` | 3; `parity + 1` in an erasure-coded pool | Copies of a mutable chunk, 1 to 3 |
| `sealed` | `replicated` | Layout of a chunk that is no longer being written: copies, or `ec` with `data` and `parity` shards |
| `encode_on_write` | On | Encode whole new chunks as they are written, skipping the copies stage |
| `seal_after` | 1 hour for files, 24 hours for block | Idle time before a chunk held as copies is encoded |
| `min_live` | `min(replicas, 2)` | Live PG members needed to accept writes, 1 to `replicas` |
| `failure_domain` | `node` | `node` or `disk` |
| `max_per_domain` | 1 | Shards of one PG allowed in one failure domain |
| `locality` | `node` with one copy, `spread` otherwise | Keep each volume on one node, or spread it over every disk |
| `overcommit` | On | Let volume sizes add up to more than the pool holds |
| `write_limit` | 90% | Disk fill past which writes that add data are refused; see [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity) |
| `disk_class` | Any | Only disks of one class, such as NVMe |
| `pg_count` | Sized from the disk count | Power of two; see [placement groups](#data-layer-chunkstore) |

**Overwrites only ever hit copies.** A 4K overwrite in a 4 data + 2 parity stripe costs 3 reads plus 3 cross-node writes, and risks the write hole on crash:

```
stripe = [d0 d1 d2 d3 | p0 p1], overwrite d1:
  read old_d1, p0, p1
  delta = old_d1 ^ new_d1
  p0' = p0 ^ gf_mul(coef[0][1], delta)
  p1' = p1 ^ gf_mul(coef[1][1], delta)
  write new_d1, p0', p1'      // must land together on 3 nodes
```

Three-way replication costs 3 writes and 0 reads. So a chunk in an erasure-coded pool takes one of two forms, `replicas` copies or `data + parity` shards, and is only overwritten as copies. It is encoded when it is written whole or once it goes idle, as [Windows Azure Storage](https://www.usenix.org/system/files/conference/atc12/atc12-final181_0.pdf) does with its extents (see [When chunks are encoded](#when-chunks-are-encoded)).

**Presets.** Names follow `rep<copies>` and `ec<data>-<parity>`; any combination within the rules below is allowed.

| Pool | Copies | Shards | Survives | Failure domains needed | Usable / raw, encoded | Suits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `none` | 1 | None | Nothing | 1 | 1 | Caches, CI scratch, apps that replicate themselves |
| `rep2` | 2 | None | 1 failure | 2 | 1/2 | Two storage nodes plus a [metadata-only node](#one-copy-pools-and-locality); re-creatable data |
| `rep3` (default) | 3 | None | 2 failures | 3 | 1/3 | Block volumes, databases |
| `ec2-1` | 2 | 2 + 1 | 1 failure | 3 | 2/3 | Media, photos and backups on a 3-node cluster |
| `ec4-2` | 3 | 4 + 2 | 2 failures | 6, or 3 at 2 per node (then 1 node) | 2/3 | Media, backups and S3 |
| `ec16-2` | 3 | 16 + 2 | 2 failures | 18, or 9 at 2 per node (then 1 node) | 8/9 | Archives on a large cluster |

An erasure-coded pool keeps `parity + 1` copies of a chunk by default, so a chunk survives the same number of failures in either form. "Usable / raw" counts encoded data; data held as copies (being edited, or idle for less than `seal_after`) costs `replicas` times its size.

**Rules checked when a pool is created:**

- **Width fits.** Copies always sit in distinct failure domains. Shards may share one up to `max_per_domain`, but never a disk, so a domain takes at most min(`max_per_domain`, its disks) shards of a PG, and the sum of that over the domains must reach `data + parity`, counting only disks of the pool's class.
- **Node tolerance is printed.** With `max_per_domain` above 1, one node failure takes out several shards of a PG, and an encoded chunk survives `⌊parity ÷ max_per_domain⌋` node failures. `ec4-2` at 2 per node fits on 3 nodes and survives one node failure (Ceph's `crush-osds-per-failure-domain` does the same); `ec16-2` at 3 per node fits on 6 nodes and survives none. The CLI states, for every pool, how many node failures its copies and its shards survive, and names each single loss after which the survivors cannot hold a PG's width, such as any node of `rep3` on three nodes: "losing node A leaves its PGs degraded until it returns". `rock-solid pool status` repeats it. Such a loss is a fact of the layout, not a [condition](#volume-size-and-capacity).
- **`write_limit` above 10%**, so that `NearFull` has a range below it.
- **Shape is fixed, the rest is not.** `data` and `parity` are fixed at creation, as in Ceph; to change them, [move the volumes](#moving-a-volume-to-another-pool) to another pool. Every other setting can change later; a change to `replicas` adds or drops copies per PG and starts a new [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) of each.

**Writes and `min_live`.** A write is acknowledged once every live member of the chunk's PG holds it. A PG with fewer than `min_live` live members rejects writes, rather than accept writes onto too few copies, until a member returns or a refill adds one, which counts as live while it still recovers; its down members are refilled after `degraded_refill_after` rather than `refill_after` ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). For `rep2` the default of 2 means a PG stops writing while either of its disks is down. A planned reboot avoids that with `maintenance <node>`: the mark takes the node's members out of the live set, so writes stop waiting for them, but they still count toward `min_live`, so until the node returns its PGs accept writes on one copy, and they resync first when it is back. The operator chose that exposure for a bounded time, and [peering](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) counts members in `maintenance` the same way, so it never skips an interval that accepted writes under the mark. Setting `min_live: 1` permanently does the same for unplanned failures, the setting Ceph users most often report losing data with.

**Encoded chunks need no write quorum.** An encoding commits only once all `data + parity` shards are durable, and only then are any copies dropped; a PG with a member down writes copies and defers encoding until recovery. An encoded chunk stays readable while any `data` of its shards survive. Ceph recommends `min_size = k + 1` for erasure-coded pools because it accepts writes to a stripe while only `min_size` shards are live; an encoding waits for every shard, so the rule does not arise.

#### When chunks are encoded

In an erasure-coded pool, a chunk's copies sit on the first `replicas` members of its PG and its shards on all `data + parity` members, so changing form moves no data between PGs. Shard `i` is the chunk's `i`-th contiguous byte range, so a 4K read of an encoded chunk touches one disk; a read whose shard is lost fetches `data` other shards and decodes. A chunk is encoded only if its shards, each rounded up to a 4 KiB block, take less space than its copies, so a small file's only chunk stays as copies: in `ec4-2` every file up to 8 KiB, so a 1.5 KiB file costs 8.1 times its size, 5.4 times what the pool promises, and in `ec16-2` every file up to 24 KiB.

1. **On write** (`encode_on_write`). The daemon's [write-back buffer](#client-design) collects a file's writes chunk by chunk. When a chunk that held no data when the file was opened fills up, the daemon encodes it with Reed-Solomon (`klauspost/reedsolomon`, pure Go with SIMD assembly) and sends each shard straight to its member; the primary commits the encoded version once every member holds its shard. Chunk 0 waits in the buffer until `close`, because many writers patch the file's header last (the `mdat` size in MP4 and MOV, WAV sizes, zip local headers), and a patch to an encoded chunk would send it back to copies. At `close`, chunk 0 and the file's last partial chunk are encoded the same way, and S3 objects take this path too. If buffer pressure flushes chunk 0 early, it is written as copies and encoded once idle. In `ec4-2` the data crosses the network once, at 1.5× its size, where copies-then-encode would cost 3× and then 1.5× more. This is the path for write-once data such as a studio's footage and photos.
2. **When idle** (`seal_after`). Everything else is written as copies: overwrites, files several nodes are writing, chunks flushed early by `fsync` or a full buffer, and block volumes. Each primary encodes chunks that have not been written for `seal_after`, every [frozen version](#snapshots-and-clones) of a chunk along with its live one:

   ```
   seal(chunk), PG members m[0 .. data+parity]:
     s[0 .. data]   = chunk split into contiguous byte ranges
     p[0 .. parity] = reed_solomon_encode(s)
     send each shard to its member in parallel, wait until all are durable
     commit the encoded version; each member replaces its copy, if any, with its shard
   ```

3. **Back to copies on overwrite.** A write to an encoded chunk first turns it back into copies: the primary reads `data` shards, writes the copies, then applies the write, merging a partial block against the decoded chunk it already read, and the chunk is encoded again once idle. That costs a full chunk read and a replicated chunk write. Editors write new files rather than patch footage, so media rarely pays it. A database on a block volume pays it on the first write to each idle chunk, which is why block chunks wait 24 hours before encoding.

#### One-copy pools and locality

`none` keeps one copy of everything. With `locality: node`, its default, each node has its own PGs, and a volume is placed on one node (the node of its first pod, through CSI's `WaitForFirstConsumer`) with its chunks hashed over that node's PGs only. Losing a disk then loses part of that node's volumes only; reads of the lost chunks return `EIO`, and the pool lists the volumes affected. Pods on other nodes still reach the volume over the network. With `locality: spread`, a volume uses every disk, and any disk loss takes part of every volume in the pool.

**Metadata is not pooled.** Pools set the redundancy of data only. MetaStore shards and the control plane are always 3-voter Raft groups, whatever pool their files use: metadata is small, and losing a directory loses every file under it. A cluster therefore needs three nodes that hold metadata. A node can hold metadata without data disks (`role: meta`), so a two-node cluster adds a small third machine, such as a single-board computer with an SSD, and runs `rep2` or `none` pools on the other two.

#### Moving a volume to another pool

A pool's `data` and `parity` are fixed, so changing them means moving each volume to another pool. Snapshot and restore could do that, but the result is a new PVC, and Kubernetes does not let a PVC change its StorageClass, so the application would have to stop and rebind its claim. Instead a volume moves to another pool in place, keeping its `volume_id`, PVC and inode numbers. The move reuses the [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) machinery:

```
move(volume, to):
  cut at snapshot epoch E: pause every node using the volume, as for a snapshot
  txn: volume record pool = to, source = (old pool, E)
  resume: writes go to the new pool's PGs; a read of a block not yet
          written there falls through to the old pool at snapshot epoch E
  copy the remaining chunks in the background, as a flatten does
  drop the source pointer; sweep the volume's chunks out of the old pool
```

Chunk IDs are the same in both pools; only the PG changes, so a chunk's [versions](#data-layer-chunkstore) in the new pool follow its new PG's intervals, and nothing compares them with the old pool's: reads fall through by snapshot epoch, and an `expect_ver` from before the move fails. Snapshots taken before the move stay in the old pool, since each snapshot record names its pool, and keep their space there until they are deleted. Moves reuse the cut, read-through and flatten code and its simulation coverage, so they ship with snapshots. Until the copy finishes, the volume uses space in both pools, and reads of uncopied blocks take an extra hop.

In Kubernetes, a move starts when a `VolumeAttributesClass` with a different `pool` parameter is applied to the PVC: the driver advertises the CSI `MODIFY_VOLUME` capability and starts the move in `ControllerModifyVolume`. The CLI can start one too.

### Volume size and capacity

A volume, block or file, can be larger than any single drive. Its chunks are spread across every disk of its pool through placement groups, so the limit is the pool's capacity, and a pool is full when its first disk is: every volume hashes over every PG, so once one disk refuses writes, some of every volume's writes are refused.

**What a pool reports.** A disk's *fill* is its live blocks, from its segment usage table, over its size. Its *capacity* for a pool is what `write_limit` leaves of it after other pools' data, and its *room* for the pool is that capacity less the pool's own data on it, never below 0. The placement table gives each disk's *share* of the pool's new data, its member slots over the PG count, a shard counting `1 ÷ data`. A pool's *held* data is what applications have stored in it: each chunk's raw bytes divided by the overhead of its form. From the fills and per-pool data that heartbeats report, the control plane computes, per pool, and per node over the node's own PGs for `locality: node`:

| Figure | Definition | Used for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Available | The smallest room ÷ share over the pool's disks, less the [space grants](#client-design) outstanding on the pool | `statfs`, CSI capacity, the CLI |
| Balanced | The most the pool could hold in all under its failure-domain rule: min over j of S₍>ⱼ₎ ÷ (b − j·a) | Admitting volumes with `overcommit` off, `Unbalanced` |
| Stranded fraction | Σᵢ max(0, cᵢ − a × balanced) ÷ Σᵢ cᵢ, and 0 when Σᵢ cᵢ is 0 | `Stranded`, and the confirmation when a pool or its disks change |
| Reprotectable | The least balanced over the survivors of any single loss listed below | `CannotReprotect` |

Here cᵢ is failure domain i's capacity, summed over the pool's disks in it; b is the pool's overhead (`replicas`, or `(data + parity) ÷ data` for encoded data); a is the most of each byte one domain may hold: 1 for copies, which always sit in distinct domains, and `max_per_domain ÷ data` for shards. Domains are sorted by capacity, largest first, S₍>ⱼ₎ is the capacity of all but the j largest, and j runs while j·a < b. Balanced is the largest D for which Σᵢ min(cᵢ, a·D) ≥ b·D: no domain holds more than its capacity, nor more than `max_per_domain` members of any chunk. Laying the domains' loads end to end around a circle of length D (McNaughton's wrap-around rule) reaches it, so placement can approach it to within PG granularity. Capacity ÷ b is its j = 0 term, which is what Ceph's `MAX AVAIL` and MinIO's usable capacity amount to; it holds only while every domain can take a share proportional to its capacity. An empty `rep2` pool on two nodes with 0.7 and 2 GB disks has 2.43 GB of capacity, 1.215 GB ÷ 2, but every chunk needs a copy on the small disk, so balanced and available are 0.63 GB; `ec4-2` at 2 per node on nodes of 4, 4 and 1 TB has 8.1 TB, 5.4 TB ÷ 1.5, but holds 1.8 TB, since each node takes a third of every chunk. With shards and `max_per_domain` above 1 (phase 10), a domain also holds at most one member of a PG per disk, so balanced is the largest D with Σ over domains of min(a·D, Σ over its disks k of min(cₖ, D ÷ data)) ≥ b·D, found by bisection, which is the closed form when `max_per_domain` is 1: `ec4-2` at 2 per node on three nodes of 2 × 2 TB and one of a single 4 TB disk holds 8.64 TB, not the closed form's 9.6 TB. Available is what Vitastor reports: in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike), uniformly hashed fills reached it within 0.3% in every layout ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). Available and balanced assume new data is encoded, as encoding on write lands most file data; data held as copies counts as used at its size. Pools that share disks draw on the same capacity, so their figures overlap. The CLI also lists each disk's fill, capacity, room, share and room ÷ share, which names the disk that sets available.

Everything but available is measured against capacity, not room, because room shrinks with every byte the pool writes even when nothing is wrong. Data follows the table, so a disk holds its share of what the pool holds, and available + held is the smallest capacity ÷ share, which does not move as the pool fills; nor do balanced and the stranded fraction. `rep3` on nodes of 4, 4, 2 and 1 TB (the spike's C2) strands 18.2% of its capacity at any fill, but measured against room 26% at 1 TB held and 75% at 2.5 TB, so a condition on room would rise on every healthy pool near full, and reprotectable and admission would shrink ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). At balance, domain i holds min(cᵢ, a × balanced), and these loads sum to b × balanced. A domain's *stranded room*, max(0, cᵢ − a × balanced), is therefore what it leaves unused, and the domains' stranded rooms sum to the pool's, Σᵢ cᵢ − b × balanced. The fraction is the same in raw bytes as in data bytes: C2's nodes A and B strand 0.9 TB each, 25% of their capacity and 18.2% of the pool's.

Reprotectable says whether the survivors of a likely loss could restore full redundancy. The losses it considers:

- **With a spare domain**, one whose loss still leaves enough domains for a PG's width, for copies or one shard per domain: the domain with the largest capacity. Balanced never falls when a domain grows, so losing that domain is the worst single loss, and losing a domain is worse than losing any one of its disks. By capacity, not size: under `rep2` on three nodes of one 2 TB disk each, one disk holding 1.2 TB of another pool, losing either of the other two leaves room to re-protect 0.6 TB, and losing the fuller one 1.8 TB.
- **Without a spare domain**, as `rep3` on three nodes: each disk that shares its domain with another, with its capacity taken out of the domain. The disk is the likely failure there, and recovery refills within the node; three nodes of 2 × 2 TB under `rep3` can re-protect 1.8 TB.
- **Shards with `max_per_domain` above 1** (phase 10): each domain in turn, then each disk, since with a member per disk the largest domain was not the worst loss in 367 of 3,000 random layouts.

A loss whose survivors cannot hold a PG's width is a fact of the layout, stated when the pool is created and in `rock-solid pool status` ([Pools and redundancy](#pools-and-redundancy)), and not counted here. A one-copy pool has nothing to re-protect.

The figures cover every disk with a share in the current table, a down disk at its last reported fill, so a node reboot changes none of them; a disk leaves them only when it is removed or marked out. Fill comparisons are in whole 4 KiB blocks: a disk's limit is ⌊its size in blocks × `write_limit`⌋, and balanced is rounded down to whole blocks.

**Conditions.** A pool raises conditions on its `Pool` resource, as events and as metrics:

| Condition | Raised when | Cleared when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `Full` | A member disk's live blocks plus ⌈grants × its share⌉ reach its limit: the primary's admission predicate ("Write limit", below), so `Full`, an available figure of 0 and the first refused write coincide | Every member disk is 1 point below its limit, grants counted |
| `NearFull` | Not `Full`, and a member disk's live blocks plus ⌈grants × its share⌉ reach `write_limit` less 10 points | Every member disk is 1 point below that |
| `Stranded` | The stranded fraction exceeds 10%, and the confirmed fraction (below) by more than 5 points. It names each domain with stranded room, and how much | The fraction falls 1 point below the level that raised it, or the operator confirms the current layout |
| `Unbalanced` | Evaluated only when placement proposes no move for the pool and no PG of it lists past members, and keeps its value otherwise. Raised when available + held < 0.9 × balanced. It names the disk that sets available, with its share and target | The same evaluation finds available + held ≥ 0.91 × balanced |
| `CannotReprotect` | Pools of 2 or more copies only: held > reprotectable. It names the domain or disk whose loss it is | Held ≤ reprotectable |
| `Stuck` | A PG of the pool has past or missing members and has copied nothing for `stuck_after` (60 s) on `ctx.Now`. It names the PGs | The PG copies again, or its past members are trimmed |

What sets these boundaries:

- **`Full` and `NearFull` count grants and use `≥`**, where Ceph's per-disk states compare strictly and ignore pending writes, because the [block frontend](#block-frontend) reports a pool holding writes as `Full`, and a window in which admission refuses but the pool is not `Full` would hide it. The 1-point clearing gap keeps a full pool holding block writes, which sits at its limit, from emitting an event pair at every discard.
- **`Stranded` waits for stranding nobody accepted.** On a mismatched layout stranding is permanent: `rep3` on nodes of 2, 2 and 4 TB strands 25% of its capacity from the first day, and only new hardware clears it, so a standing condition would only teach operators to ignore conditions. The prompt at creation is where it is seen and accepted; the condition reports what changes later, such as C2, confirmed at 18.2%, rising to 40% when node D's 1 TB disk is marked out after a failure, or another pool filling the small disks.
- **`Unbalanced` has no timer.** A move lasts as long as its data takes to copy, and the leader knows when placement is done, so the condition is a state predicate, as Ceph's balancer gates on states. Its 10% is twice the fill margin's 5%: in the study's model, after 400 random disk additions to converged `rep2` and `rep3` tables, none raised it at any fill, the worst leaving 9.4%. So it reports placement stopped well short of what it could reach: pinned PGs, a drain, a `pg_count` too small for a disk.
- **`Stuck` is the one timer.** A copy blocked by a full disk keeps its PG's past members listed, so `Unbalanced` is never evaluated and no state shows that copying stopped. `stuck_after` is Ceph's `mon_pg_stuck_threshold`.
- **`CannotReprotect` fires only when data is the problem.** Counting losses whose survivors cannot hold the width would raise it from the first byte on the default `rep3` with three nodes. One-copy pools have no redundancy to restore, which Ceph reports as a separate warning, `POOL_NO_REDUNDANCY`.

No system read warns about stranded space or predicts whether losing a domain leaves room to recover: Vitastor reports its space efficiency, stranding and imbalance together, with no threshold, and Ceph leaves both to an operator's planning and reports the failure when recovery runs out of room. The 10%, 5 points, 1 point and 60 s are judgments checked against the [capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)'s model, not against operators.

**Confirmation.** Placement never refuses a layout, because another pool on the same disks, such as `none` with `locality: node`, can use the room one pool strands. `rock-solid pool create` and disk changes print, per domain, its capacity, its load at balance and its stranded room, ask for confirmation when a domain would strand more than half its capacity, and record the stranded fraction as the pool's *confirmed fraction*. `rock-solid pool confirm` does the same for the current layout, for a `Pool` created without the CLI among others, whose confirmed fraction is 0 until then. `rock-solid pool status` always shows the stranded fraction, its domains and the confirmed fraction, and the losses the layout cannot recover from.

- **Thin provisioning.** A chunk exists only after its first write, so creating a volume allocates nothing. With `overcommit` on, volume sizes may add up to more than the pool holds. With it off, the pool keeps a reservation, the sum of its volumes' sizes, at the copies' overhead for block volumes in an erasure-coded pool, since they stay copies for at least `seal_after`; `CreateVolume` and `ControllerExpandVolume` succeed only while reservations plus what snapshots hold stay within balanced, and otherwise fail with `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`. Balanced, not available, because a reservation outlives every rebalance, and balanced over capacity, because reservations count data already written: C2 holding 2 TB of its own data with 2.5 TB reserved admits a 0.1 TB volume within the 2.7 TB it can hold, where balanced over room, 0.7 TB, would refuse it.
- **Write limit.** A write that adds live data is refused once a disk holding one of its PG's members has reached the pool's `write_limit` (default 90%): its live blocks plus the grants outstanding on the pool times the disk's share, rounded up (the control-plane leader returns each pool's total of outstanding grants on every heartbeat reply, so every primary holds a total at most a heartbeat old), reach its limit, ⌊size in blocks × `write_limit`⌋, the predicate `Full` uses. The primary decides from the fill each member reports with every acknowledgment and heartbeat.
