Three kinds of write pass: a flush of buffered data under a space grant, which was promised when the daemon acknowledged it; a write that only replaces blocks the chunk holds live, written after its latest freeze and held as copies, and that needs no new freeze, which kills in the log as many blocks as it writes, as dm-thin keeps writing provisioned, unshared blocks when its pool is out of space; and punches, which free space, as Ceph lets every operation that adds no bytes through a full pool. So a filesystem's journal and preallocated database files keep working on a full pool. A granted flush needs only its grant; the overwrite condition is checked only while a member is at its limit, one index lookup per run of blocks. The limit is checked per disk, not as a pool average, because one full disk stalls every PG on it; CockroachDB likewise stops placing replicas on a store over 95% full. The log's reserve and the cleaner's free segments sit below the limit, so a disk at its limit still cleans, checkpoints and merges ([Free space](#on-disk-format)). Pools that share a disk each check their own limit against the disk's total fill, so a pool with a higher limit can fill the disk past a lower one, and the lower one's writes stop there. The headroom serves recovery, granted flushes and the [cleaner](#on-disk-format): with random 4K overwrites, cleaning write amplification is about 3 at 80% full, 7 at 90% and 17 at 95%, where 94% of the disk's writes would be cleaning ([cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike)). Chunks written whole go to [chunk slots](#on-disk-format) and cost no cleaning whatever order they are deleted in, so a pool of large files, such as backups or footage archives, can raise its limit to 95%. Its small files and file tails still clean like block writes.
- **Fill sets drive wear.** A block volume's random overwrites cost each disk its cleaner's write amplification, which climbs with the disk's fill: under uniform overwrites 2 at 66% full, 3 at 77%, 5 at 86% and 7.2 at 90%, the default `write_limit`; skewed overwrites cost less (4.2 at 90% for Zipf), and file data deleted in write order 1.0 at any fill ([cleaner spike](#appendix-cleaner-write-amplification-spike)). The chunk index's runs raise 3.4 to 3.9 at 80% and 7.2 to 8.0 at 90% ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)), and the drive's own garbage collection adds what it adds, which is not measured yet ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). A drive's rated endurance, the terabytes written (TBW) its warranty covers, is 600 TB for a 1 TB TLC consumer drive (Samsung 990 PRO), 220 TB for a 1 TB QLC drive (Crucial P3) and about 1,700 TB for a 960 GB data-center drive rated at one drive write per day (Micron 7450 PRO). Spread over five years, each rating allows this many bytes a day of overwrites landing on one disk held at each fill:

  | Fill (write amplification) | 1 TB TLC, 600 TBW | 1 TB QLC, 220 TBW | 960 GB, 1,700 TBW |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 66% (2) | 164 GB | 60 GB | 465 GB |
  | 77% (3) | 110 GB | 40 GB | 310 GB |
  | 86% (5) | 66 GB | 24 GB | 186 GB |
  | 90% (7.2) | 46 GB | 17 GB | 129 GB |

  Each overwrite lands on `replicas` disks, so `rep3` on three nodes of one disk each puts every byte on every disk: a database overwriting 50 GB a day, held at 90% with the index's runs counted (8.0), reaches the TLC drives' rating in 4.1 years and the QLC drives' in 1.5. The fill that matters is the one a disk sits at, not the limit; a pool at 60% wears at under 2. `write_limit` still defaults to 90%, since pool defaults pick the option that uses less space ([Pools and redundancy](#pools-and-redundancy)), and holding disks to 80%, which more than halves the disk's writes (3.9 against 8.0), gives up a ninth of the pool. A pool of block volumes with random overwrites on consumer drives should set 80%, and one on QLC drives lower still. So that an operator sees this coming, each disk exports its cleaner's write amplification and, on NVMe drives, the SMART log's Percentage Used and Data Units Written as metrics, and `rock-solid pool status` shows each disk's Percentage Used and its writes per day over the last week, to set against the drive's rating.
- **Snapshots use space.** Versions pinned by [snapshots](#snapshots-and-clones) count toward the pool, and an overwrite of a block a snapshot shares needs new space, so past `write_limit` it is refused like a new write.
- **Wider blast radius.** A volume spread over every disk is hit by any failure, anywhere in its pool, that takes out more members of one PG than the pool survives. Recovery must restore a degraded PG before a second failure arrives, so it starts with the PGs that have lost the most members.
- **A filesystem volume's size is a quota.** The PVC size caps the sum of its files' sizes. Each inode slot keeps a byte counter (`u`, in the [key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)), changed by an `Update(u, Add, delta)` in the transaction that changes a file's size, which never conflicts with other writers of the slot. `statfs` reports the PVC size as the total, the sum of the slot counters as used, the rest as free, and as available the smaller of the rest and the pool's available figure, so `df` shows what can still be written. The daemon caches the sum and the pool's figure for `statfs_cache` (default 5 s) and fails writes that would extend files past the PVC size with `ENOSPC`, before it looks at its space grant. Writers can overshoot by what they add in one cache period.
- **What Kubernetes sees.** `GetCapacity` answers for the class's pool and the node's topology segment, per node for `locality: node` pools and the same everywhere for others. With `overcommit` off, `available_capacity` and `maximum_volume_size` are balanced minus reservations. With it on, `available_capacity` is available and `maximum_volume_size` is 0 when available is 0 and otherwise the largest volume the pool accepts, so the scheduler keeps `WaitForFirstConsumer` pods off a node whose node-local pool is full but still places thin volumes larger than what is free. The check is advisory: a `CreateVolume` the pool refuses returns `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, and Kubernetes reschedules.
- **Largest file.** A file can't grow past the volume setting `max_file_size` (default 16 TiB, ext4's limit), which bounds a [purge](#freeing-space) to 4 million chunk IDs.

**How a full pool reaches a writer.** From the top:

| Check | Where | What the writer sees |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PVC size (file volumes) | Daemon, at `write()` | `ENOSPC` |
| Space grant (buffered file writes) | Daemon, at `write()` | The write goes through to the primary instead of the buffer |
| `write_limit`, for writes that add data | Primary | `ENOSPC` from `write()` on a file volume; on a block volume the write is held until the pool has room ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) |
| Cleaner behind | Disk log | Slower writes, then waiting ([Writers slow down](#on-disk-format)) |
| Only the reserve free, nothing to clean | Disk log | `NoSpace`, which reaches a file's writer late, at its next `write()`, `fsync` or `close` ([Client design](#client-design)) |

### Freeing space

Chunk IDs are computed, so freeing data never needs to find chunks: a purge lists the IDs and [punches](#data-layer-chunkstore) them, batched per PG. A punch to a chunk that doesn't exist succeeds, so a purge interrupted by a crash simply runs again. Punches carry the snapshot epoch like writes, so a purge never frees a block a [snapshot](#snapshots-and-clones) still holds.

- **Allocation mark.** Each inode records `alloc_end`, one past the highest chunk that may hold data. Before a daemon writes a chunk at or past it, it raises `alloc_end` in steps that double up to 16 chunks, and the same transaction commits the size of what it has already written. Other nodes therefore see a file grow while it is written, at most 64 MiB behind, and a purge never misses a chunk written before a crash.
- **Unlink.** Removing a file's last link removes the inode's whole key range (attributes, xattrs, locks, symlink target) and adds a purge entry (`p/{inode}` → `0 .. alloc_end`) to its slot, in one transaction. If the file is still open, the inode goes on the orphan list instead (see [Tier 1](#cache-coherence-tiers)) and moves to the purge list at its last close. The slot's shard leader works through the purge list in the background.
- **Truncate.** Shrinking a file commits, in one transaction, the new size, `alloc_end` lowered to the new end, and a purge range from the new size's byte offset to the old `alloc_end`. The purge's first punch zeroes the rest of the block holding the new end, by a [merge](#data-layer-chunkstore), and the rest of its chunk; the daemon runs that punch before `truncate` returns, the purger runs it again if a crash came first, and the purger deletes the whole chunks after it. So bytes past a file's size are zero on disk whatever crashes, where zeroing only in memory before committing the size is the bug XFS fixed in 2015 for its extending truncates (commit `5885ebda`), and FreeBSD's fusefs kept a final partial block's stale tail in its cache until 2019 (`e312493b`, found by fsx). Reads never return data past the size, and anything that extends the file over a range with a pending purge, a write raising `alloc_end` or a truncate upward, first finishes that purge. So a file extended later reads zeros where it was cut, without any write to the gap, and the purger never deletes newer data.
- **One purge entry per inode, merged.** The purge list is keyed by inode, so a truncate, unlink, rename-over or an orphan's last close that finds an entry pending for the inode replaces it with the union of the pending range and its own, with a `Check` that the entry still holds what it read. Every pending range lies past the file's current size, since an extension first finishes it (above), so the union never covers live data. Writing the new range over the old one would leak chunks: a truncate followed by an unlink before the purger runs would leave only `0 .. alloc_end` at the lowered `alloc_end`, and the truncated chunks above it would never be deleted ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md), finding 3).
- **The purger's writes are checked.** Its punches are idempotent; its MetaStore writes are not safe to repeat blindly, because the list is not a queue: an entry for any inode of the slot can be added or merged at any time, below the purger's position too. So the purger removes an entry it finished with a `Check` that the entry still holds what it read, records progress through a long entry the same way, and removes a consumed run of entries K₁ … Kₘ with one range removal carrying a `Check` on each Kⱼ and a `CheckEmpty` over each gap between them ([Lists consumed in key order](#metadata-layer-metastore)). A failed check means a writer got there first, and the purger reads the entry again. An unchecked range removal would delete an entry added inside the run between the scan and the removal, and a blind delete retried after an election could remove an entry written after its first attempt applied; either leaks chunks (finding 4).
- **Block discard.** A discard (TRIM) from the pod's filesystem, which the CSI driver mounts with `discard` ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)), reaches the daemon through ublk and becomes a punch of the range, so a thin-provisioned block volume gives space back as soon as files in it are deleted.
- **Volume deletion.** Deleting a block volume purges chunks `0 .. size ÷ CHUNK_SIZE`. Deleting a filesystem volume removes its `fs/{volume}/` key range in one range removal, checked against the volume's record saying it is being deleted, and has every disk of the pool sweep its chunk index for the volume's ID. That background scan is cheaper than purging millions of small files one by one. A volume that still has snapshots or clones keeps the chunks they need (see [Deletion](#snapshots-and-clones)).

## Metadata layer: MetaStore

The MetaStore is an ordered, transactional key-value store, sharded into many small Raft groups, each with its own leader. Consensus is needed per key, not globally.

```go
type MetaStore interface {
	Txn(t Txn) (Result, error)
	Scan(start, end []byte, limit int) (kvs []KV, more bool, err error)
}

type Txn struct {
	Ops    []Op
	Replay []byte // the replay record's key, or nil for an internal transaction
	Oldest uint64 // the mount's oldest outstanding request; its records below it are dropped
}

// The op kinds, the whole set:
//   Put(key, value)
//   Delete(key)                          succeeds if the key is absent
//   DeleteRange(start, end)              removes every key in [start, end)
//   Update(key, off, Set|Add|Bump, n)    changes the 8-byte big-endian field at off; an absent key
//                                        or short value reads as zeros; Add wraps; Bump stores
//                                        max(n, field + 1), so the field always moves forward
//   Check(key, value | absent, final)    the key holds exactly value, or is absent
//   CheckEmpty(start, end, final)        no key in [start, end)

type Result struct {
	FailedCheck int      // index of the first failed check, or -1
	Actual      []byte   // that check's key's current value; nil if absent or a CheckEmpty
	Updated     []uint64 // each Update's new field value, in op order
}
```

**Why consensus is unavoidable.** Leases, atomic rename and S3 `if-not-exists` are compare-and-set (CAS) operations, and agreeing on one CAS winner among replicas that can crash is consensus. Dynamo-style quorums and conflict-free replicated data types (CRDTs) scale without a leader but cannot do CAS: two racing lock grabs would both succeed.

**How a `Txn` applies.** The shard evaluates a `Txn` when it applies the log entry, on every replica, in log order: first every `Check` and `CheckEmpty` against the state before the `Txn`, then, if all pass, every write in list order, each seeing the ones before it. So `put a=1, delete a` leaves `a` absent, two updates of one key's fields compose, and nothing is ever undone, since no write can fail once the checks pass. A failed check applies nothing and returns its index and its key's current value, so the caller can retry from it without another read. This is etcd's rule (compares before ops, ops in order) and CockroachDB's order within a batch; etcd refuses two writes to one key in one `Txn`, but here they are common (`mtime` and `ctime`, size and `alloc_end`). Apply-time evaluation costs each replica the checks' reads, about 1 µs each from the memtable, and makes the ops' meaning disk format, since every release must apply an old entry the same way: a new op kind needs a raised format level. CockroachDB evaluates at the proposer instead, for that reason, but then a write that depends on a key's value must wait until the write before it on that key has applied, or the leader must track every proposal in flight.

**Why `Update`: keys many writers share.** A round's transactions were all built from reads taken before the round, so of the concurrent writers that compare one key, only one commits per round and the rest retry. Three kinds of key are written by many clients at once: a slot's counters (`n`, `u`), and a directory's attributes, since every `create`, `mkdir`, `unlink` and `rename` in a directory moves its `mtime` and `ctime` (POSIX, checked by `pjdfstest`), and `mkdir` and `rmdir` its link count. With compare-and-set only, 64 parallel creators in one directory (many pods writing into one upload directory, `make -j`, a parallel copy) would get one create per commit, 333 to 1,667 per second; with an `Update` that cannot fail, the shard's loop is the bound, about 60,000 proposals per second at 90% busy. A single creator such as `tar x` is unaffected. So keys many clients write change only through `Update`, and a file's own attributes, which one node usually writes, by `Check`-and-`Put`, which keeps its size, `alloc_end` and the `u` delta consistent. A parent's times move by `Bump`, never `Max`, so a create from a node whose clock is behind still moves the directory's `mtime` by at least 1 ns, as [Modification time](#client-design) requires. FoundationDB's atomic `add` and `max` on "a known offset in the value" are the precedent, and CockroachDB's `Increment` the single-key form. `Update` needs the attribute value to have a fixed layout, which its hand-written encoding gives it. `CheckEmpty` is etcd's `Compare` over a `range_end`: `rmdir` and rename over a directory need it for `ENOTEMPTY`, flock for "no other holder", and the purger for the gaps in what it consumes ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)).

**Limits, checked before proposing, as errors, never panics.** A `Txn` has at least one op and encodes to at most `max_txn_bytes` (256 KiB, a quarter of `max_group_commit_bytes` and three times the largest designed transaction, a 64 KiB xattr in pieces); its keys are at most `MaxKey` (1,032 bytes) and its values `MaxValue` (1,000), and each `DeleteRange` and `CheckEmpty` bound at most 1,000 bytes, so a range removal's fragment fits the [LSM tree](#on-disk-format)'s page; every designed range bound is under 40 bytes. A `Txn` is never split, since splitting breaks all-or-nothing.

**`Scan`** reads `[start, end)` from one point-in-time view, at most `limit` keys and at most `max_scan_bytes` (256 KiB) of keys and values, and sets `more` when it stopped at either bound. A `limit` below 1 is an error, since an unbounded reply would allocate without bound. The caller resumes with `start` = last key + `0x00`, which is also S3's `start-after` and a `readdir` cookie, so a scan bound may be `MaxKey` + 1 bytes; it is never stored. A `Scan` whose range spans two Raft groups is refused with `ErrCrossShard` as a `Txn` is (see below), since one view covers one group; a caller scans each group's range in turn. A prefix scan is `Scan(p, PrefixEnd(p), n)`, and a point read such as `getattr` or `lookup` is `Scan(k, k + 0x00, 1)`. CockroachDB bounds a reply by keys and bytes the same way and returns a resume span; etcd returns `more`.

**Internal transactions are guarded.** A client request is safe to resend because of its replay record ([Daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). A `Txn` without one, from the control-plane leader or the purger, carries a `Check` or `CheckEmpty` on every key or range it read, so a repeat after an election, or a write that raced it, fails instead of doing harm; an `Add` whose repetition would matter, such as one to `u`, rides only in a `Txn` with a replay key or with a check a repeat would fail. Being idempotent is not enough: a blind delete retried after an election can remove an entry written after its first attempt applied. The exceptions remove keys nobody writes again: replay records below a mount's oldest outstanding request, and a session's records once it has left the session table.

**Sharding (multi-Raft).** The key space is split into ranges, each its own 3-replica Raft group, with leaders spread across nodes:

```go
func (m *metaStore) Txn(t Txn) (Result, error) {
	shards := m.shardMap.Route(t.Ops) // sorted and deduplicated
	if len(shards) == 1 {
		return m.shard(shards[0]).Propose(t) // fast path: one Raft round
	}
	return twoPhaseCommit(t, shards) // slow path, rare by design; ErrCrossShard until phase 13
}
```

**Routing.** A `Txn` goes to its shard's leader, from the daemon's route cache. A follower or candidate never forwards it: the shard sets etcd-raft's `DisableProposalForwarding` and answers `NotLeader{shard, leader node, term}`, and the daemon updates its cache and resends, as CockroachDB's `NotLeaseHolderError` and TiKV's `NotLeader` do. Forwarding would add a hop, leave the proposer learning the result only by applying it, and bypass the [inode owner](#metadata-layer-metastore)'s open registry, which lives at the leader. During an election a `Txn` fails retryably (etcd-raft's `ErrProposalDropped` underneath). The leader never proposes again on its own, so a proposal from an old term is either in the new leader's log or gone, and the only ambiguity is the caller's: a client resends under the same replay key, which returns the first final result, and an internal caller reads again, its guards making a repeat fail harmlessly. CockroachDB needs its `max_lease_index` because its leaseholder re-proposes; this shard does not.

**`ctl/` uses the same API.** The control-plane leader's logic (placement, session renewals in memory, fencing, space grants) is code beside the control-plane group that proposes `Txn`s on `ctl/` keys like any client, so the shard state machine, its tests and its invariants are one piece of code. The shard map pins `[ctl/, PrefixEnd(ctl/))` to the control-plane group, which never splits, as CockroachDB's first-level addressing range never splits.

**Key layout: the design goal is single-shard for almost everything.** A `create` writes a directory entry under the parent and a record for the new inode, so the two keys must fall into one shard without a global transaction service. Other systems run one global transaction across shards (3FS on FoundationDB, JuiceFS on TiKV), store inodes inside directory entries with a separate path from inode number back to the entry (CephFS, HopsFS), or order writes and garbage-collect what a crash leaves (Tectonic).

Keys are written below as paths. On disk, each `word/` is a one-byte tag, and integers are fixed-width and big-endian, so numeric order is byte order. Only the last component may have variable length (a file name, xattr name or S3 key), so keys need no escaping and a prefix scan is a byte-prefix scan. CockroachDB escapes every byte string in a key because a SQL key holds several variable-length columns; with one variable part last, escaping is unnecessary, and fixed widths keep decoding trivial and make slot boundaries plain prefixes, at about 8 bytes per key over varints, which the runs' prefix compression mostly recovers.

| Level | Tags |
| --- | --- |
| First byte | `0x01` ctl, `0x02` volume, `0x03` fs, `0x04` s3 |
| `ctl/` | `0x01` pool, `0x02` placement table, `0x03` membership, `0x04` session, `0x05` format level, `0x06` shard map |
| `volume/{id}/` | `0x01` attach, `0x02` snap |
| An inode's keys (sequence ≥ 1) | `0x01` a, `0x02` x, `0x03` t (symlink target), `0x04` l, `0x05` d, `0x06` c, `0x07` P (a directory's parent) |
| A slot's keys (sequence 0) | `0x10` n, `0x11` u, `0x12` o, `0x13` p, `0x14` r, `0x15` f (next free slot, at the shard's lowest slot only) |
| Pieces of a split value | The value's tag with its high bit set (`0x82` for x, `0x83` for t), a 1-byte piece number, then the last component |

Volume IDs are 32 bits, inode IDs 64, session IDs 64, mount IDs 32, request IDs 64 (FUSE's `unique`), table and snapshot epochs 64, and a lock mode 8. So `fs/5/2:1/a` is `03 00000005 0000020000000001 01`, and a replay key is 34 bytes. `a` and `x` are adjacent, so a file's attributes and xattrs are one range; an inode's tags sort before a slot's, which appear only at sequence 0, so a decoder can check that; piece tags sort after everything an inode scan reads; the gaps leave room. A snapshot's metadata copy at `fs/{snapshot}/` puts a snapshot ID where a volume ID goes, so snapshot IDs come from the volume ID counter.

**Large values are split into pieces.** A value over 1,000 bytes (`MaxValue`) is stored with the first piece and the piece count under its own key and pieces 1 … m under the piece tag, all in one `Txn`, as FoundationDB advises for values over 10 KB. A 64 KiB xattr, which Samba's `vfs_streams_xattr` writes for macOS resource forks, is about 66 values, about 82 KiB in one `Txn`; a 4,095-byte symlink target, which `pjdfstest` creates, is 5; a read is 1 + m point lookups, about 1 µs each. The frontends check names before they reach the MetaStore: 255 bytes for names and xattr names, 4,095 for symlink targets, 64 KiB for xattr values (Linux's `XATTR_SIZE_MAX`), 1,024 for S3 keys, whose index key, at 1,029 bytes, sets `MaxKey`.

**Inode IDs** are 64 bits: a 24-bit *slot* and a 40-bit sequence number. Every key of an inode starts with `fs/{volume}/{inode}`, so a slot is one contiguous key range, and shards split only at slot boundaries.

- A new file takes a sequence number in its parent's slot. Its entry and its inode are in one shard.
- A daemon reserves sequence numbers 64 at a time, with a `Txn` of its own, `Update(n, Add, 64)`, before its first create in a slot, hands them out from memory, and reserves the next batch in the background when 10% are left, as JuiceFS prefetches its batches of 1,024. The reservation cannot ride in the create's `Txn`, since the create's keys contain the new number and no op can use an earlier op's result. It costs one round before a node's first create in a slot; a repeat after an election only skips numbers.
- A new directory takes a free slot from the same shard's range, so `mkdir` is single-shard too, and a later split can move whole subtrees. Daemons reserve slots 16 at a time from the shard's `f` key in the same way. The directory takes sequence 1 of its fresh slot, as the root is inode 1, and its `mkdir` sets the slot's `n` to 66, so the creating daemon owns sequences 2 … 65 and "`mkdir`, then create in it" needs no reservation round. When the shard has no free slot left, the directory shares its parent's slot, and when a slot's 2^40 IDs run out, new files go to a fresh slot.
- IDs are never reused. The root directory is inode 1, and sequence number 0 of each slot holds the slot's own keys.

| Data | Key | Shard rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pools, placement table, membership, sessions, format level, shard map | `ctl/...` | The control-plane Raft group: the shard map pins the `ctl/` range to it and it never splits; small because it maps placement groups, not chunks |
| Inode attributes, including size and `alloc_end`, as fixed 8-byte fields | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/a` | The inode's slot. The volume prefix makes a filesystem one contiguous key range, which a snapshot copies and a deletion drops |
| Directory entries | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/d/{name}` → inode, type | The directory's slot, usually shared with its files, so `readdirplus` is one request to one shard |
| A directory's parent | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/P` → parent | Its own key, so creates in an ancestor never fail a rename's loop check on it |
| Extended attributes | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/x/{name}` | Next to the attributes, so one request returns both; the daemon caches small ones with the attributes |
| Symlink target | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/t` | Next to the attributes |
| Case-folded names | `fs/{volume}/{dir}/c/{folded name}` → name | Only with the volume setting `case_index` (off by default) for SMB-shared volumes; turning it on later builds it in the background |
| flock and whole-file locks | `fs/{volume}/{inode}/l/{mode}/{session}` | The inode's slot; the mode in the key lets one `CheckEmpty` find an exclusive holder |
| Slot counters (next sequence number, bytes used), orphans (unlinked while open), purge list | `fs/{volume}/{slot · 0}/n`, `…/u`, `…/o/{inode}`, `…/p/{inode}` | The slot itself; see [Freeing space](#freeing-space) |
| Replay records of mutating client requests | `fs/{volume}/{slot · 0}/r/{session}/{mount}/{request}` → result | The slot the request changed; see [Daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) |
| Volume attach and mounts, snapshot records | `volume/{id}/attach`, `volume/{id}/snap/{epoch}` | Any shard; touched only at attach, detach, snapshot and move operations |
| S3 index | `s3/{volume}/{key}` → object ID, size, version | Range sharding, never hash, so `LIST prefix` is a sequential scan. A bucket is a volume, and object IDs come from a slot that each shard reserves, reserved as inode numbers are, so a `PUT` stays single-shard |

**The transactions.** Each is one `Txn` in one shard, except a cross-directory rename after phase 13. "The parent's times" is `Update(parent a.mtime, Bump, t)` and `Update(parent a.ctime, Bump, t)`; a check marked final is one whose failure the daemon returns as an errno.

| Operation | Ops |
| --- | --- |
| `create` | `Check(d/name absent, final)`, `Put(d/name → inode)`, `Put(child a)`, the parent's times; with `case_index`, `Put(c/folded)` |
| `mkdir` | As `create`, plus `Update(parent a.nlink, Add, 1)`; in a fresh slot s, `Put(s:1 a)`, `Put(s:1 P → parent)` and `Put(s:0 n = 66)` |
| `link` | `Check(d/name absent, final)`, `Put(d/name)`, `Check(child a)` and `Put(child a)` with `nlink` + 1 and `ctime`, the parent's times |
| `symlink` | As `create`, plus `Put(child t)`, in pieces past 1,000 bytes |
| `unlink`, last link, not open | `Check(d/name = inode, final)`, `Delete(d/name)`, `DeleteRange` over the child's keys, its purge entry merged ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)), `Update(u, Add, −size)`, the parent's times |
| `unlink`, other links or still open | `Check(d/name, final)`, `Delete(d/name)`, `Check(child a)` and `Put(child a)` with `nlink` − 1 and `ctime`, the parent's times; if open, `Put(o/inode)` |
| `rmdir` | `Check(d/name = inode, final)`, `CheckEmpty(child's d/, final)`, `Delete(d/name)`, `DeleteRange` over the child's keys, `Update(parent a.nlink, Add, −1)`, the parent's times |
| `rename` in one directory | `Check(src entry, final)`, `Check(dst entry absent, or = what was read)`, `Delete(src)`, `Put(dst)`; over a directory also `CheckEmpty(its d/, final)`; a replaced inode as in `unlink` |
| `rename` across directories | As above, plus both parents' times; for a directory, `Update(a.nlink, Add, ±1)` on both parents, `Put(its P → new parent)`, and `Check(P = what was read)` on each ancestor of the new parent up to the root, so a directory never moves under itself |
| `setattr` on a directory | `Update(field, Set, v)` and `Update(a.ctime, Bump, now)`; never a `Check` on a directory's attributes, which every create in it changes |
| `alloc_end` raise, `close` or `fsync`, a size change, O_APPEND | `Check(a = what was read)`, `Put(a)` with size, `alloc_end` and `mtime`, `Update(u, Add, new size − old size)`; a failed `Check(a)` is not final, so the daemon reads again and retries |
| Truncate | `Check(a)`, `Put(a)`, `Update(u, Add, −delta)`, the purge entry merged |
| flock `LOCK_EX` | Scan `l/`; for each key of a session gone from the session table, `Check` it and `Delete` it; any live holder fails the request; then `CheckEmpty` over each gap between those keys, and `Put(l/x/{session})` |
| flock `LOCK_SH` | The same over `l/x/` only, then `Put(l/s/{session})` |
| xattr set | `Put(x/name)`, in pieces past 1,000 bytes |
| S3 `PUT` | `Check(s3/v/key absent, or = version, final)`, `Put(s3/v/key)` |
| `ctl/`: a table or session-table version | `Check(version = e)`, `Put(e + 1)` and its contents |
| `ctl/`: a session starts | `Check(counter = k)`, `Put(counter = k + 1)`, `Put(session/k)`; sessions start rarely, so compare-and-set is enough |

Clearing a dead holder's lock in the transaction that grants the next one, with a check per gap, saves the round a separate clean-up would cost after every fence. A cross-directory rename of a directory reads every ancestor.

**What the layout costs.** `create`, `mkdir`, `unlink` and `readdirplus` touch one shard, and a split never cuts a directory away from its files. In exchange:

- A cross-directory `rename` keeps the inode in its old slot, so locality decays slowly over time; correctness does not depend on it.
- A directory and all its files live in one slot, so one huge, busy directory is bounded by one shard, as in CephFS without directory fragmentation.
- A slot holds 2^40 IDs, about 3.5 years of creates at 10,000 per second.
- 64-bit `st_ino` values can overflow 32-bit programs built without large-file support.
- A volume shared over SMB needs `case_index` on, which costs one extra key in each create's transaction; small xattrs cached with the attributes answer a gateway's per-entry `user.DOSATTRIB` reads ([Samba spike](#appendix-samba-gateway-spike)).
- The hash function and bit layout of chunk IDs and PGs are disk format: changing either moves every chunk.

**Inode owner.** The leader of an inode's shard is that inode's *owner*. Besides serving its keys, it keeps in-memory state for open files: who has the file open, who has written, and the current cache mode (see [Cache coherence](#cache-coherence-tiers)). This state is soft. It is not written to the Raft log, so `open` costs one round trip and no disk write. A planned leader transfer (leader balancing, a rolling upgrade, draining a node) hands the open registry to the new leader with the transfer, so opens don't pause. After a leader crash, clients re-register their open files during a grace period of one session lease, as in NFS version 4. During it the new owner accepts only re-registrations: new opens and cache-mode changes for its inodes wait, while reads and writes on open handles carry on, since they go to chunk primaries.

**Cross-shard transactions (CockroachDB style, no global timestamp service):**

1. Coordinator writes a transaction record `{id: T, status: PENDING}` in shard X.
2. Each touched shard gets an "intent": a provisional value tagged T, written in parallel.
3. Flip T's record to COMMITTED. This single key is the atomic commit point.
4. Intents are resolved into real values asynchronously. A reader that meets an intent checks T's record, so anyone can finish or abort T after a coordinator crash.

Cross-directory `rename` is the main file operation that takes this path. Every operation stays in one shard until range splitting makes a second data shard in phase 13: the control-plane group is a Raft group of its own from phase 4, but no designed operation writes it and a data shard in one `Txn`. Every volume's keys, `volume/{id}/` included, sit on that one data shard, so the [snapshot cut](#snapshots-and-clones) and a volume's deletion are one-shard transactions until then, and the cut's markers in other shards arrive with the protocol. So the protocol is finished in phase 13, with a TLA+ model as its first precise form, before its code. The four steps above leave out what other systems' bugs came from: what a reader or writer that meets a PENDING intent does (wait, or abort T by changing its record, and how the coordinator learns that it lost), when a record may be removed (only once every intent is resolved), how a cross-directory rename refuses to make a directory its own ancestor when the ancestors are on other shards, and how the snapshot cut's markers commit with the snapshot record. CockroachDB's parallel-commit model reproduced two assertion failures its TPC-C runs had hit, and MongoDB's two-shard transaction model found a 27-step snapshot isolation violation in minutes ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)). Until then a `Txn` whose keys span two Raft groups, the control-plane group included, is refused with `ErrCrossShard{groups}` before anything is proposed, so nothing comes to depend on a path that is not modelled.

**Lists consumed in key order.** A list worked through in key order, such as a purge list, removes a consumed run of entries with one `DeleteRange` rather than one removal per entry. Removals of entries flushed before they were consumed otherwise pile up at the list's head: in the [LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study), a seek to a queue's head stepped over 10,820 removals, and over one with the prefix removed as a range. RocksDB documents the same pile-up for queues. The purge list is keyed by inode, not a queue, so entries can be added inside a run while it is consumed: the `DeleteRange(K₁, Kₘ + 0x00)` carries a `Check` on each entry Kⱼ it consumed and a `CheckEmpty` over each gap between them, about 1 µs per check ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)).

**Storage.** Each shard replica lives on one disk of its node. Its Raft log and its state, an LSM tree, are part of that disk's [log](#on-disk-format), so the daemon embeds no key-value engine and SPDK drives hold metadata too.

**Raft shard.** The Raft shard is etcd-raft itself (`go.etcd.io/raft/v3`, pinned to v3.6.0), which already has the sans-IO shape: the shard wraps its `RawNode` in `OnEvent`, the driver feeds it ticks and messages, and it returns a `Ready` of entries to persist, messages to send and entries to apply. It is v3.6.0, not the newer v3.7.0: v3.7.0 generates its messages with `google.golang.org/protobuf`, a pointer per field, so it makes 16 allocations per message against 2.7, spends 1.64× the CPU per proposal and 22% of the process's CPU in collection against 14%, and its `Storage` returns `[]*pb.Entry`, which would allocate on every read of entries kept in an off-heap slab ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). Ticks, messages and persistence all belong to the driver, so it shares flushes, batches messages per connection and quiesces shards across every group on the node. etcd-raft's `Node` wrapper and hashicorp/raft run their own goroutines or own their storage, which rules both out. A Raft written from scratch would have to find again the edge cases etcd-raft has fixed over years in etcd and CockroachDB. Even so, a mature library is no guarantee of safety, so etcd-raft's tests, the simulator and a TLA+ model all cover the shard.

- **Synchronous `Ready` and `Advance`.** etcd-raft's asynchronous storage writes stay off: with them a restored snapshot can overtake committed configuration changes still queued for apply, which panics upstream ([study](../studies/etcd-raft-async-snapshot-apply/README.md)). Turning them on needs two guards first: drop apply work that a restored snapshot covers, and never compact past the durable commit index.
- **A pinned fork.** Upstream draws election timeouts from `crypto/rand` behind a mutex (`raft.go`, `globalRand`). A `replace` to a pinned fork of v3.6.0 gives each replica a seeded source instead, so the simulator replays the shard exactly, until upstream takes a configurable source; the fork's only other change is v3.7.0's ReadIndex retry fix (etcd-io/raft#397). etcd-raft's own unit tests and datadriven testdata run on the fork at every tag update, and a simulator test replays a seed twice and compares histories, which catches any other source of nondeterminism.
- **Log entries.** Entries and hard state are written to the [disk log](#on-disk-format) as part of the round's record. A leader's appends to its followers go out in the same round as its own write, as etcd's server sends them, and nothing else does: every other message a `Ready` carries, a follower's acknowledgments and a vote included, waits until the round holding its entries and hard state is durable, since an acknowledged entry, a granted vote and a new term must survive a crash (Figure 2 of the Raft paper, Ongaro and Ousterhout, "In Search of an Understandable Consensus Algorithm"). A pre-vote changes no hard state and needs no wait. The group commit already reports an item only once its round's flush completes, so the wait costs the shard nothing of its own. etcd-raft reads entries synchronously through `raft.Storage` while it builds appends, so the shard keeps every entry after its last compaction in memory, in its loop's off-heap slab, and compacts once the memtable that applied them has been flushed. That bounds the retained entries by twice `max_replay_bytes` per disk ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)).
- **Cost.** A group costs 2.8 KiB of heap idle, 2.7 MiB per node for 1,000 groups, and 2 to 8% of a core per node with a 10 ms tick; under load its heap is the entries it retains, which live in the loop's slab. At 60,000 proposals/s a proposal costs 22.6 allocations, 8.4 messages and about 27 µs of loop CPU across its three replicas, and collection 14% of the process's CPU. Most allocations are etcd-raft's own message and state slices, so the driver neither recycles messages nor encodes from entries until a profile says otherwise ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). With the `Ready` shape, the [multi-raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike) kept 3,000 quiesced replicas to 0.07% of a core.

**Shard count.** A shard splits, at a slot boundary, once it holds more than `shard_split_size` (default 512 MiB, as CockroachDB's ranges), so a 3 to 10 node cluster runs tens of groups per node. Splitting by load comes with leader balancing. The Raft shard is still built to stay cheap at about 1,000 groups per node, so per-group cost never limits splitting:

- **Batching per connection.** The driver sends every group's messages for one peer loop as one batch over their [connection](#runtime-in-go). In the spike, 80,000 Raft messages per second fit in 240 batches.
- **Quiescence.** A group with no writes stops ticking and heartbeating. The [multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike) cut the idle cost of 3,000 replicas from 13% of a core at the design's timing, 36% at raft-rs's default 50 ms heartbeat, to 0.07%. Its leader keeps its [lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) and its followers stay loyal through the per-node-pair leases, so a quiescent group sends nothing until it writes again or a node-pair lease lapses, which wakes every group led from that node. TiKV's hibernate regions and CockroachDB's leader leases on store liveness work the same way.
- **Timing.** `raft_heartbeat` defaults to 100 ms and `raft_election_timeout` to a random 1 to 2 s. The election timer ticks every 10 ms, finer than the heartbeat: with only a few distinct timeout values, followers time out together and split the vote. A crashed leader's groups first wait for its node's grants to lapse, `primary_lease` (4 s, up to 5.2 s under a slewed clock), since their followers stay fortified until then (below). The lapse itself shows the follower that its leader's node is gone, so it campaigns, pre-vote first, after a random `raft_lapse_campaign` (150 to 300 ms, the range the Raft paper measured, §9.3) instead of a whole election timeout; a candidate whose pre-vote fails, because another voter's grant has not lapsed on its own clocks yet, tries again at its election timeout. Failover is set by the slowest of the groups that lost their leader, so the budget is about 3.2 to 4.3 s from the crash to a new leader (a grant lapses one `primary_lease` after its last renewal, which came up to a heartbeat before the crash, then `raft_lapse_campaign`), up to 5.5 s under a slewed clock, and 1 to 2 s more for a group whose campaigns collide. That budget is reasoned, not measured: the [multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike) measured elections in 1.4 to 5× the maximum election timeout with the timer ticking once per heartbeat, and a 10 ms tick halved that, but no run has measured campaigns on a lapse ([open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). The control-plane group fails over the same way, and meanwhile a client keeps the lease of its last answered renewal, which came up to a heartbeat before the crash. A client whose clock a time daemon runs 22.6% fast ends that lease at `session_lease` less `lease_drift` on the fast clock, about 8.1 s, so a control-plane failover longer than about 7.1 s pauses that client's writes until the new leader answers, and one longer than about 9 s pauses every client's ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). The budget stays below 7.1 s except where a slewed clock and colliding campaigns meet, up to 5.5 + 2 s. `session_lease` stays at 10 s: the pause fences nobody, and a longer lease would hold a dead client's locks longer. A crashed control-plane leader's own PGs also wait for the failover plus `node_down_after` before they are marked down ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)).

**Replica placement and replacement.** The control-plane leader places every group's three voters, one per node on nodes that hold metadata, each on the disk of its node with the fewest shard replicas, and records them in the shard map under `ctl/`. A group obeys its own configuration, in its Raft log; the shard map is the plan the control-plane leader drives each group toward, through the group's leader. A voter whose node or disk has been down for `refill_after` is replaced, as a PG member is refilled ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)), and one whose disk's log is gone is replaced at once.

A replacement never joins as an empty voter. In a group of three with one voter down, an empty fourth voter would raise the quorum to three, so every commit would wait for the newcomer to catch up from a snapshot of up to `shard_split_size`, the stall the Raft paper warns of (Ongaro and Ousterhout, §6). So the group's leader adds the new replica as an etcd-raft *learner*, which receives the log, or a snapshot taken from a [point-in-time view](#on-disk-format) once the log is compacted, but does not vote. Once the learner has caught up with the leader's log, the leader proposes one `ConfChangeV2` that removes the dead voter and promotes the learner, which etcd-raft carries through a joint configuration and leaves on its own. The two surviving voters are a majority of both halves, so the swap commits without the dead voter and without waiting on the newcomer, and the [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) needs a quorum in each half throughout. A replica whose node returns after the swap deletes its state only once the group's committed configuration shows it removed, never on the shard map's word, which runs ahead of the group: a voter that forgot its log and votes while still in the configuration could help elect a leader that lacks committed entries. The control-plane group changes its voters the same way, driven by its own leader. With only three nodes that hold metadata, a voter whose node is down has no other node to move to, so its group runs on two voters until the node returns and survives no further failure.

**Group commit.** Each leader batches whatever arrived during the last round into one log entry and one network round. The entry joins the next record of its disk's log, so it shares one flush with every other shard and every chunk write on that disk. The batching, not a bound on the round, is what keeps rounds short: with one entry per proposal, 32,000 proposals in flight on 1,000 groups per node ran at 164,000 proposals/s with a p50 of 178 ms; with one entry per group per round, at 1.6 to 2.0 million/s in rounds of 5 ms on one loop per node ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). The loop collects `Ready` only from the shards it stepped, proposed to or ticked since the last round, never by scanning all of them: a scan of `HasReady` over 250 shards per round took 54% of a loop's CPU once 0.1 ms flushes made rounds frequent.

```go
for {
	batch := inbox.DrainUpTo(1024)
	entry := encode(batch)
	diskLog.Append(entry) // the append and the sends go out in the same round
	replicateToFollowers(entry)
	waitForMajority()
	for _, t := range batch {
		memtable.Apply(t)
		t.ReplyOK()
	}
}
```

**Event loop per core.** Each core's [event loop](#runtime-in-go) owns a set of shards outright, their Raft state and LSM trees, with no locks between them. Their records go into the log of the shard's disk through that disk's [owner loop](#on-disk-format). A commit waits for about three rounds (the leader's append, the follower's append and flush, the leader's apply), each behind its loop's round in flight, so commit latency follows loop utilization, not round size: with each loop under half busy, p50 3 ms and p99 5 ms on a 0.95 ms flush, and 0.6 ms and 1 to 3.6 ms on a 0.1 ms flush, while one loop holding 1,000 shards is 90% busy at 60,000 proposals/s and its p99 reaches 10 to 50 ms ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). Shards are spread over the loops, and a node plans its shard leaders so that no loop runs above about half busy.

**Reads and leader leases.** The leader serves reads under a lease, with no round trip. The lease rides on the per-node-pair leases that already back [primary read leases](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection), as CockroachDB's leader leases ride on its store liveness. Once elected, a leader *fortifies* its term by asking each follower for support. A follower that agrees records the leader's node and the support epoch of its own node's grant to that node. While that grant holds in that epoch, the follower neither campaigns nor grants a vote or pre-vote in the group, except to a transfer target. The leader's lease ends at the quorum-th latest expiry among the voters that fortified it, itself included, and both halves of a joint configuration must each give a quorum. Each expiry is the holder's end of a node-pair lease, counted from when the leader's node asked for the grant, by the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection); a voter whose node's grant the leader's node has no rate bound for yet gives no expiry, though its support, and so its refusal to vote, stands. Groups led from a restarted node therefore read through ReadIndex for their first 100 ms or so, and a failover is unaffected. Node-pair heartbeats renew every group's lease at once, so a quiescent group keeps its lease with no Raft heartbeat, and the cost does not grow with the group count.

When a grant lapses on the grantor's clocks, every fortification under it ends. The groups led from that node then wake up and campaign after `raft_lapse_campaign` (above), so a crashed leader's groups fail over after `primary_lease` (4 s) plus an election. Campaigning early is safe: a challenger needs the votes of a quorum, a follower whose own grant to the leader's node still holds drops them, and by the lease-time rule the leader's expiry from each follower passes before that follower's grant lapses, so once a quorum can vote, the leader's lease, which needs a quorum of unexpired expiries, has ended; a live leader that votes for a challenger has stepped down to the challenger's higher term first. Until a new leader is fortified and has committed an entry of its own term, it answers reads through etcd-raft's ReadIndex round instead. Followers can also read with ReadIndex later, if leaders get hot. Readers never wait for writers, except a reader that meets a cross-shard intent and must check its transaction record (above). A reader gets a point-in-time view of the shard's LSM tree without blocking the writer. The view pins the memtable and sorted runs it reads, so a view held open holds back the cleaner, and anything long-lived, such as a [snapshot's metadata](#snapshots-and-clones), is copied out instead.

etcd-raft has no such lease. Its own lease (`CheckQuorum` with `ReadOnlyLeaseBased`) counts ticks, which a quiescent group does not take. It also checks the quorum only once per election timeout, so a leader that lost its quorum just after a check believes it leads for up to two election timeouts while followers refuse votes for one (`raft.go` at v3.6.0, `tickHeartbeat` and the `inLease` check in `Step`). So `CheckQuorum` stays off and `PreVote` is on, and the shard implements fortification around `RawNode`: it drops the (pre-)votes of a supported leader's challengers before they reach etcd-raft, keeps supporting followers' election timers stopped, and computes the lease itself. CockroachDB's fork of etcd-raft has the same mechanism, but its fortification and store liveness code is under the CockroachDB Software License, so we take the design from it, not the code.

**Leader transfer.** A planned transfer ends the old leader's lease the moment its `Ready` carries `MsgTimeoutNow` to the target. etcd-raft keeps the old leader in office until it sees the new term, but it serves no lease read after that point. The target campaigns with etcd-raft's transfer context, which followers let through even while they support the old leader. The new leader fortifies its own term before it serves lease reads, and the open registry moves with the transfer, as above.

**Deferred:** leaderless consensus (Egalitarian Paxos, Accord). Leader-per-shard already removes the single-leader bottleneck.

## File frontend: RWX filesystem

This is the first product. Many pods on many nodes mount one POSIX directory: shared media, uploads, CI caches, datasets, app data. The data path is the shared ChunkStore path. The hard part is keeping each node's cache of pages, attributes and directory entries correct.

### Client design

Every node runs the daemon, which mounts RWX volumes through FUSE. Three rules come straight from the [FUSE spike](#appendix-fuse-coherence-spike):

1. **The kernel page cache is used for reads only.** We never enable FUSE's `writeback_cache`. It is a mount-wide switch, it hides writes from the daemon until the kernel flushes them, and it disables the kernel's automatic invalidation on attribute changes. With it on, the daemon could not stop a node from caching a shared file.
2. **Write-back buffering lives in the daemon.** Every `write()` reaches the daemon as a FUSE request (about 5 µs). Without `writeback_cache` the kernel sends each `write()` with exactly its offset and length (`fs/fuse/file.c`, `fuse_perform_write`), and direct I/O unaligned too, so the daemon takes any byte range. While the file is not shared, the daemon buffers writes in its own memory and acknowledges at once, as long as its [space grant](#client-design) covers them. It flushes a chunk as soon as it is full, and the rest on `fsync`, `close` or a recall. A flush covers whole blocks wherever the daemon holds a block's other bytes or they lie past the end of file, which no other node can change while the file is not shared, and bytes past the end are zero on disk ([Freeing space](#freeing-space)): a new small file goes out as one block padded with zeros, and the daemon keeps a file's last partial block after flushing it, until a recall, so the next append's flush is a whole block too. The chunk primary [merges](#data-layer-chunkstore) every other partial block, which is what write-shared files pay. The buffer holds up to 256 MiB per node (`writeback_buffer`); when it is full, the oldest partial chunk is flushed. The daemon therefore always knows when a file has been written, and can flush it on demand.
3. **Whether a handle is cached is decided at `open` and cannot change.** The kernel fixes a handle's direct-I/O flag when the file is opened. So the daemon chooses it per open, from the file's cache mode at that moment. Handles that are already open and cached are made coherent by other means (see Tier 2).

**Write path**

```
FUSE write(inode, off, data)
  past the PVC size (cached)  → ENOSPC
  file not shared and the grant covers it:
                   take it from the grant, buffer, acknowledge   // flushed later, in order
  otherwise:       flush the chunk's buffered writes first
                   chunk_id = (volume, inode, off / CHUNK_SIZE)
                   → ChunkStore.write via the chunk primary, which merges partial blocks
                   → acknowledge, or ENOSPC if the pool refuses it
                   → if the write extends the file: MetaStore size = max(size, end)
any chunk write    → first, if its index >= alloc_end: MetaStore.txn([raise alloc_end, set size written so far])
close() / fsync()  → flush buffered chunks, widened to whole blocks where the daemon holds the bytes
                   → MetaStore.txn([set size, mtime = max(last write's time, mtime + 1 ns)])
                   → an error this handle has not seen yet, if any
```

`alloc_end` bounds the chunks a later [purge](#freeing-space) must delete, and its transaction also lets other nodes watch a large file grow while it is written.

**Modification time.** A file's `mtime` comes from the wall clock (`CLOCK_REALTIME`) of the node that wrote it: the daemon sends the time of the file's last write with its `close` or `fsync` transaction. Wall clocks on different nodes disagree and can step back, so the transaction stores the latest of that time, the stored `mtime` plus 1 ns and, for a file that was write-shared, the largest `mtime` the node reported for it ([Leaving WriteShared](#cache-coherence-tiers)), and a file's `mtime` never goes backwards when another node writes it next, which `make` and `rsync` rely on. An explicit `utimensat` stores the time it is given. CockroachDB's hybrid logical clocks keep their timestamps monotonic by the same rule ([clock skew study](../studies/clock-skew/README.md)).

**Space grants.** A buffered write is acknowledged before any disk takes it, so it must not fail later for lack of space: ext4, XFS and Btrfs give that guarantee by reserving blocks at `write()` (`ext4_da_reserve_space`, `xfs_dec_freecounter`, `btrfs_delalloc_reserve_space`), while the Linux NFS client, CephFS and JuiceFS report a full server only at a later `write()`, `fsync` or `close` ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). So the daemon buffers only against a *space grant*: bytes of a pool its node may hold unflushed, counted in physical blocks written, so a 100-byte append is charged a whole block per copy. Grants ride on the heartbeat each node sends the control-plane leader every second, which renews its [session](#sessions-and-fencing):

- **Ask.** Each heartbeat asks, per pool, for min(`writeback_buffer`, what the node holds buffered + what it flushed since its last heartbeat): what it would buffer in the next interval at its current pace. An idle node asks for 0, so its grant comes back within a heartbeat, which is Lustre's shrink protocol for free. The heartbeat also carries, per pool, a cumulative count of blocks flushed, which the leader differences, so a lost heartbeat loses no report, and the grant the node holds.
- **Divide.** The leader keeps a pool's grants at most half its [available](#volume-size-and-capacity) figure counted before them, so grants shrink as the pool fills and daemons turn to writing through before it is full, as CephFS's client syncs every write once its pool is nearly full. When the asks exceed that cap or the disk cap below, it divides max-min fairly (water-filling), with what each node holds buffered as a floor: grantᵢ = min(askᵢ, max(bufferedᵢ, L)), the water level L set so the grants fill the cap. Four nodes asking 256 MiB against 512 MiB get 128 each; asking 256, 256, 64 and 16 they get 216, 216, 64 and 16. The part of a grant above L is a promise for data already buffered, not room to reuse: a node above the level buffers nothing new until its buffered bytes fall below L, and the leader counts it at its buffered bytes plus k × L (below). Otherwise a node held at its floor on a full disk, where L is 0, would flush and refill its whole grant every window. If the floors alone exceed the cap, as after a sharp drop in available, every grant is its floor, nothing new is buffered, and the excess drains as the nodes flush.
- **Reuse budget.** The daemon reuses its grant as each flush completes, so a node streaming faster than one buffer per second keeps buffering, but for at most k = 2 grants' worth of flushes, counted from when it sent the last heartbeat whose reply it received; past that it writes through until the next reply, and data it already buffered still flushes. Once it has used half the budget it sends its heartbeat at once rather than waiting for the next second, as CephFS's client reports its size early once its unreported growth passes a fraction of what is left, so the budget bounds unseen bytes, not throughput: a streaming node sends about 4 heartbeats per second at 10 GbE and 41 at 100 GbE with full grants, each a memory update at the leader and no Raft entry, and a lost reply costs nothing unless the next early heartbeat's reply is lost too. Everything a node can land since its window began is then at most (1 + k) × its grant, whatever happens to heartbeats, clocks or the control plane. The daemon keeps one counter, and one snapshot of it per outstanding heartbeat.
- **The disk cap.** For every disk d of the pool, Σ (1 + k) × grantᵢ + F ≤ g_d, where F is what nodes reported flushed since the fills the leader holds, and g_d the disk's size less its log reserve less the larger of its fill and `write_limit`, so a disk a refill pushed past the limit counts from where it is. A granted flush passes `write_limit`, and all of a node's buffer can land on one disk (one full buffer put up to 3.4 times its share on one disk in the [capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike)), so this cap is what keeps a granted flush off the reserve: it is never refused for space. No disk write rate is needed. The leader counts (1 + k) × max(held, new) for a node until a heartbeat echoes the new grant, so a shrink whose reply is lost is never counted before the node has it; without that, three nodes shrunk from 256 to 50 MiB with their replies lost could land 2.42 GB while the leader counted 0.47 GB. Near full the cap is g_d ÷ 3: 3.29 GB on a 100 GB disk at 90%, more than ten full grants need, and 1.06 GB on a 32 GB NVMe disk.
- **Failover, crashes and lapsed sessions.** A new leader, which knows no held grants, counts every live session at (1 + k) × `writeback_buffer` per pool until it renews; meanwhile a node that flushes more than its budget writes through, about 1 ms per write instead of 5 µs, until the new leader answers. A lapsed session's grant counts, with its budget, until its [fence](#sessions-and-fencing) is acknowledged, the moment owners release its locks, since until then a primary can still apply its granted flushes; Lustre likewise discards an evicted client's grant only "once we're sure no more interaction with the client is possible". The cumulative flush count lives in the [handoff memfd](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade), so a successor after a daemon crash continues it; one that cannot read it buffers nothing until its first heartbeat is answered, and the leader keeps counting the old (1 + k) × grant until fill reports after that heartbeat arrive.

Why a budget: a grant reusable for as long as the renewal that carried it, `session_lease`, under a gap cap less a heartbeat of each disk's writes, would let a node whose heartbeats stopped reaching the leader while its writes still reached storage, in a control-plane failover or a partition from the leader alone, buffer and flush unseen for 10 s: on a 3-node `rep3` pool streaming at 1.1 GB/s, 11 GB on each disk against a gap of 9.87 GB. Reuse for one heartbeat interval with a peak rate per disk would close that too, but it depends on clocks, sends even a light writer to writing through after one late reply, and leaves a small, fast disk almost no grant (0.17 GB on the 32 GB disk). A larger k buys a heavy writer less than a second more of buffering through a failover and lowers the cap near full in proportion (k = 4 gives 1.97 GB on the 100 GB disk), so k is small ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)).

A write the grant does not cover goes through: the daemon flushes that chunk's buffered writes and sends the write to the primary, as for a shared file, so a full pool returns `ENOSPC` from `write()`. Near full, writes then cost a round trip and a flush each, about 1 ms on the measured NVMe drive, instead of 5 µs. The PVC size is checked first; it bounds a file's logical size, while the grant bounds new physical space, which an overwrite needs too. Mapped writes cannot be granted: the kernel sends them at writeback, so a refusal reaches `msync` or `fsync`.

**Errors after acknowledgment.** A flush can still fail: the disk log has no segment the cleaner can free, the session was fenced, or the daemon crashed ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)). The daemon drops the data, which a recall would otherwise wait on forever, as CephFS's client discards its cache when a pool fills, and reports the error once to each handle that was open on the file, at that handle's next `write()`, `fsync` or `close()`, which the kernel sends as `FUSE_FLUSH` (`fs/fuse/file.c`, `fuse_flush`): `ENOSPC` for space, `EIO` otherwise. That is the rule `errseq_t` gives local filesystems, where every open file description sees a writeback error once at `fsync`, extended to `close()` as NFS does. A FUSE file volume can fail a write this way because no filesystem sits between the daemon and the application; a [block volume](#block-frontend) holds its writes instead.

**Read path**

```
cached handle:   kernel page cache → on a miss, FUSE read → daemon → ChunkStore.read
uncached handle: FUSE read every time → daemon → ChunkStore.read via the chunk primary
both:            the daemon clamps a read to the file's size
```

**Namespace operations** (`create`, `mkdir`, `link`, `symlink`, `unlink`, `rmdir`, `rename`, `setattr`) are MetaStore [transactions](#metadata-layer-metastore), single-shard except for cross-directory rename. Each moves the parent directory's `mtime` and `ctime`, and `mkdir`, `rmdir` and a directory's rename its link count, through `Update`, so concurrent creators in one directory never conflict on it.

### Cache coherence tiers

Coherence ships in tiers. Each tier is correct for the workloads in its row, and each one is a usable release.

| Tier | What | Correct for | Effort |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | RWX block: multi-attach, no daemon-side cache, fencing | KubeVirt live migration, cluster-aware apps | Small |
| 1 | Close-to-open consistency, short attribute and directory-entry timeouts, flock, unlink and rename of open files, fencing | One writer per file at a time: media, configs, uploads, datasets, CI caches | Medium |
| 2 | Per-file cache mode switch: cache while one node holds the file or all are readers; uncached once two or more nodes share it and one has written | Concurrent writers on a file, automatically, with no tuning | Medium |
| 3 | Byte-range locks, directory caching with pushed invalidations | SQLite-style apps that lock byte ranges; large directory trees | Large |

Tier 1 already beats Longhorn RWX: there is no NFS pod per volume, so no single-server bottleneck or single point of failure.

**Tier 1: close-to-open**

- **On `open`:** the daemon registers the open with the inode's owner and gets current attributes. If the file changed since this node last cached it, the open is returned without `keep_cache`, and the kernel drops the stale pages.
- **On `close` and `fsync`:** the daemon flushes buffered writes and commits size and mtime. The next open on any node sees them.
- **Metadata:** attributes and directory entries are cached for 1 second by default. A `strict` mount option sets the timeouts to 0. No timeout the daemon hands the kernel reaches past its [session lease](#sessions-and-fencing).
- **flock and whole-file `fcntl` locks:** MetaStore keys owned by the client's session.
- **Unlink and rename-over of open files:** if anyone still has the inode open, unlink records it in its slot's orphan list instead of freeing it. The owner moves an orphan to the [purge list](#freeing-space) once no node has it open, and only after any failover grace period. This replaces NFS's `.nfsXXXX` silly rename. It is in Tier 1 because temp files, log rotation and "write a temp file, then rename it over the original" are common.

**Tier 2: the per-file cache mode switch**

Weka caches file data in the page cache by default. When several servers access a file and at least one writes, it bypasses the page cache and sends all I/O to the backend. We do the same, and the inode's owner tracks the mode:

```go
// Exclusive: one node; it caches reads and the daemon buffers its writes.
// ReadShared: several nodes, none has written; everyone caches reads.
// WriteShared: several nodes, one or more has written; nobody caches.
type CacheMode struct {
	Kind    ModeKind
	Members []ClientID
}
```

The mode is computed from the open registry. A node counts as writing after its first write to the file, whatever flags it passed to `open()`. The daemon sees every write, because the kernel does no write-back.

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
    direction LR
    Exclusive: Exclusive<br/>One node has the file open<br/>Cached reads, buffered writes
    ReadShared: ReadShared<br/>Several nodes, no writer<br/>Everyone caches reads
    WriteShared: WriteShared<br/>Several nodes, 1+ has written<br/>Nobody caches
    Exclusive --> ReadShared: 2nd node opens, no writes yet
    Exclusive --> WriteShared: 2nd node opens after a write
    ReadShared --> WriteShared: first write (recall)
    ReadShared --> Exclusive: all but one node close
    WriteShared --> Exclusive: all but one node close
```

*Caching turns off only while a file is write-shared, and turns back on when only one node still has it open.*

**Entering WriteShared.** The owner sends a recall to every other member and waits for every acknowledgment, or for that member's session to expire and be fenced. The write or open that caused the switch waits for this too: a first write in ReadShared, or a second node's open of a file another node has written, which the owner answers only once the recall completes. If the node that wrote is unreachable, the open can wait up to its `session_lease`, or `recall_timeout` if the node still renews its session but does not answer, plus the time to fence it ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)). A recall does four things on the receiving node:

1. **Flush:** write the daemon's buffered writes for the file to the ChunkStore.
2. **Drop cached pages:** `notify_inval_inode` (a few µs).
3. **Uncache new opens:** from now on, open the file with `direct_io`.
4. **Revalidate old handles:** a one-time drop is not enough, because an already-open cached handle refills the page cache on its next read (spike test T3). So while the file is WriteShared, the daemon answers every attribute request for it with a new change counter in the mtime field, with an attribute timeout of 0: the larger of the stored `mtime` and the last it reported for the file, plus 1 ns. With `auto_inval_data` on, the kernel then drops the cached pages before each `read()` on an old handle and fetches fresh data (spike T5c, about 15 µs per read locally). No extra network trip is needed.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant A as Node A (writer)
    participant O as Inode owner
    participant B as Node B (reader, cached)
    A->>O: first write to file (mode ReadShared)
    O->>B: recall(inode)
    B->>B: flush buffer, drop pages, direct_io for new opens, revalidate old handles
    B-->>O: ack
    O-->>A: mode = WriteShared
    A->>A: write goes straight to the chunk primary
```

**While WriteShared:**

- **Data:** every read and write goes to the chunk primary, which orders them. That costs one network round trip per I/O, tens of microseconds on 10 GbE with kernel TCP. Write-sharing is rare in homelab workloads, so this is acceptable.
- **File size:** a write that extends the file updates the size in the MetaStore before it is acknowledged, so readers on other nodes can read past the old end of file. Writes inside the file don't touch the MetaStore.
- **Appends** (`O_APPEND`) from several nodes use a MetaStore compare-and-set on the size.
- **mmap:** FUSE does not tell the daemon when a file is mapped, and mapped pages are not revalidated on access (spike T5b). So after each write it commits, the writing node tells the other members' daemons, and each calls `notify_inval_inode` on its own kernel. Mapped readers then see the write within one message delay, not instantly. The `mmap_sync` mount option (off by default) makes each write wait for those invalidations instead. Shared-writable `mmap` across nodes stays a non-goal.

**Leaving WriteShared.** When all but one node have closed the file, the owner returns it to Exclusive. No recall is needed, since no other node is caching. New opens on the remaining node are cached again. The change counters ran ahead of the stored `mtime`, so falling back to it would show `mtime` going backwards, which `make` and `rsync` read as an older file. So a daemon never reports a lower `mtime` for a file than it last did, and each closing node's `close` transaction carries the largest `mtime` it reported, which the transaction stores if it is later ([Modification time](#client-design)). A node that sees the file after it leaves WriteShared therefore sees an `mtime` at least as late as any the closed nodes showed.

### Sessions and fencing

Each client daemon holds one session, for its FUSE mounts and block devices alike, and every chunk write carries the session's ID. The session's locks, open registrations and cache rights all belong to it.

**The control plane owns every session.** A session is a key in the control-plane group, `ctl/session/{id}` → node and state, with IDs from a counter that never reuses one. Its lease (`session_lease`, 10 s by default) is renewed by the heartbeat each node already sends the control-plane leader every second for [failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection): the heartbeat names the session, and the reply grants the renewal. Renewals change only the leader's memory; the Raft log changes only when a session starts or is fenced. So renewal costs one message per node per second whatever the number of shards, a node that uses only block volumes renews like any other, and inode owners never decide that a session expired: they cannot disagree, and their failovers leave sessions alone. Chubby, ZooKeeper, etcd and HDFS keep sessions at their leader the same way. CephFS lets each metadata server expire its own sessions but turns the first expiry into a cluster-wide blocklist, and Lustre's servers evict clients on their own, which works only because each holds both the locks and the data they protect ([session expiry study](../studies/session-expiry/README.md)).

**Each side counts the lease on its own clocks**, by the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) every lease follows. The client is the holder and counts from when it sent its last answered renewal; the leader is the grantor and counts from when it processed that renewal. The client keeps its rate bound per leader node and boot, so a leader failover starts a new window; the client keeps its old lease, counted from its last answered renewal, while it sends its second renewal to the new leader `rate_gap` after its first, well inside `session_lease`; a client daemon's start holds its writes about 100 ms more. Only a leader holding its [leader lease](#metadata-layer-metastore) answers renewals. A new leader gives every live session a full `session_lease` from the start of its own lease before it expires any, and a leader whose loop fell behind restarts every session's timer rather than expire sessions it could not hear, as CephFS's metadata server skips expiries after it was itself slow. Both only delay an expiry, never bring it forward.

- **The client limits itself.** It issues no write after its lease ends, and no attribute or directory-entry timeout it gives the kernel reaches past that instant. A paused client's kernel therefore stops trusting its cached pages when the lease ends: the next `read()` asks the daemon for attributes, and the daemon, seeing its lease has lapsed, drops the file's pages first. Mapped pages are the exception, since the kernel does not revalidate them (spike T5b), so a paused node's `mmap` readers can see stale pages until its daemon runs again and invalidates every cached inode; whether attribute timeouts hold across a machine suspend is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions). A daemon whose lease lapsed without word of a fence holds its requests until a renewal is answered, so a control-plane failover shows as a pause, as in Chubby's grace period.
- **The control plane fences.** The leader fences a session when its lease runs out, when an owner asks because the session left a recall unanswered for `recall_timeout` (default 30 s), or when an operator evicts it. It commits every session due at that moment as one new version of the *session table*, which lists the live sessions and those being fenced and is published like the placement table. Every storage node applies the version, from then on rejects every write from a fenced or removed session, including writes already queued, and acknowledges; its heartbeats repeat the version it holds. A write from a session newer than a primary's table makes the primary fetch the table first.
- **Owners release last.** Once every storage node has acknowledged the version, or has been marked down in a committed table, the leader commits it as acknowledged and the fenced sessions leave the table. A down node is marked up again only once its heartbeat reports the current version. Owners act only on that: they reject requests from sessions not live in their table, a lock key whose session has left the table is free and is deleted in the transaction that grants the lock to the next holder, the session's open registrations go, and its recalls count as answered.
- **A fenced client starts over.** It learns from its next renewal or a rejected write, starts a new session and reopens its files. Its applications get errors, never silently stale data.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as Client daemon
    participant L as Control-plane leader
    participant P as Storage nodes
    participant O as Inode owner
    C->>L: heartbeat, session S
    L-->>C: renewed
    Note over C: paused, its lease ends lease_drift early
    Note over L: no renewal of S for session_lease
    L->>L: commit S fenced, table version v
    L->>P: session table v
    P-->>L: applied v, or the node is marked down
    L->>L: commit v acknowledged
    L->>O: session table, S gone
    O->>O: free S's locks, drop its opens, answer its recalls
    C->>P: late write, session S
    P-->>C: rejected
```

*The client's lease ends before the leader's, the fence commits after both, and owners release only once every storage node has applied it.*

This is safe because the client's lease ends first: it counts from a send that came before the leader processed the renewal, and the [lease-time rule](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) makes a holder's count end before its grantor's whatever a time daemon does to their clocks. A renewal answered by a deposed leader was answered under its leader lease, which ended before the new leader's began. So the client issues no write after the fence commits, every write it issued earlier is applied before its primary applies the fence or is rejected after, and the next holder writes only once every storage node has applied it or has been marked down. Marking a node down starts a new [interval](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) of every PG with a member on it, after which peering stops any write still tagged with an earlier interval, and in every other PG each member has applied the fence and checks it on every write it receives, replicated ones included. A write the fenced client got applied is therefore ordered before every write of the next holder.

A dead client's locks wait `session_lease` plus one commit and one acknowledgment round, milliseconds on a LAN, or until an unreachable storage node is marked down; when the client's own node crashed, that has already happened, since `node_down_after` is shorter than `session_lease`. Fences are batched, so a switch failure that strands many nodes costs one version and one round. The session table is versioned on its own, not as a change to the placement table, so its acknowledged version counts fences alone and a fence never waits behind a rebalance. A control-plane outage longer than about 7.1 s can pause the writes of a client whose clock a time daemon runs fast, one longer than about 9 s every client's, until a new leader answers, and it fences none of them ([Raft shard timing](#metadata-layer-metastore)).
