This is the same machinery for flock (Tier 1), recalls (Tier 2), snapshot cuts and RWX block (Tier 0).

### Kubernetes integration for RWX

- **CSI:** advertise `ReadWriteMany` for `volumeMode: Filesystem`. The node plugin asks the local daemon to mount the volume with `allow_other`, then bind-mounts it into each pod. The daemon's container mounts the kubelet directory with `mountPropagation: Bidirectional`, which requires a privileged container; without it the mount stays in the daemon's own mount namespace. Application pods need no propagation, since their volume is mounted before their containers start. The node plugin also holds the daemon's `/dev/fuse` descriptors ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)).
- **fsGroup:** advertise the CSI `VOLUME_MOUNT_GROUP` node capability. Kubelet then passes the pod's fsGroup to the driver instead of recursively changing ownership of every file, which would stall mounts of large volumes.
- **Compute-only nodes** run the daemon in client-only mode: no disks, no shards, just FUSE.

### Daemon restart and upgrade

When the last process holding a mount's `/dev/fuse` file descriptor exits, the kernel aborts the connection, and every pod using the mount gets "Transport endpoint is not connected". The [FUSE restart spike](#appendix-fuse-restart-spike) showed that any process holding the same descriptor can serve the mount without a new handshake, and files opened under the old process keep working. One process per volume would not avoid this, since that process can crash too; JuiceFS's mount pods pass their descriptors on for the same reason. So the daemon restarts in place:

- **The CSI node plugin holds every descriptor, from mount time.** The daemon passes each `/dev/fuse` descriptor, every clone of it, and a memfd holding the negotiated `INIT` parameters and the daemon's session ID to the CSI node plugin over a Unix socket in a hostPath directory that both pods mount, and a successor takes them back. The mount belongs to the connection, not to the process or mount namespace that made it: the [FUSE handoff spike](#appendix-fuse-handoff-spike) put the holder, the daemon, its successor and the application in four different mount namespaces, killed the daemon so that its namespace disappeared, and served an `allow_other` mount to another uid from the descriptor alone, with no application error. The holder hands descriptors only to a root peer it checks with `SO_PEERCRED`, and the daemon only to a root holder, since a descriptor is the whole filesystem. A successor sets the file flags it needs, because flags such as `O_NONBLOCK` belong to the open file that the holder and the dead process share. The kernel never renegotiates `INIT`, so the successor refuses to adopt a mount whose saved parameters differ from its own, and a release that changes them (a larger `max_write`, a request timeout) needs a remount.
- **Kernel-visible IDs come from durable state.** The FUSE node ID is the inode ID, and file handles name state the successor can rebuild on a miss. Lookup counts are lost in a crash, so the daemon tolerates `FORGET` for nodes it does not know.
- **An upgrade drains; a crash resends.** For an upgrade, the old process stops reading requests, finishes those in flight, flushes its write-back buffer and hands over; the spike measured stalls of 0.7 ms with the successor already running and 4.2 ms when it starts cold and takes the descriptors from a holder. After a crash, the successor writes `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` at once, and the kernel requeues every request the dead process had read but not answered. Without the resend, those callers would block forever.
- **Replayed requests are idempotent.** A resent `mkdir` that the dead process had already applied returned `EEXIST` to the application. So every mutating MetaStore request carries a replay key, `r/{session}/{mount}/{request}` in the slot it changes ([key layout](#metadata-layer-metastore)). If that record exists, the shard returns the stored `Result` and applies nothing; otherwise it applies the `Txn` and, in the same transaction, stores its `Result` there if the result is final: every check passed, or the failed one is marked final because the daemon returns it as an errno (`EEXIST`, `ENOENT`). So a `rename` that failed with `ENOENT` returns `ENOENT` when resent, even after the source appears, while a lost compare-and-set race, such as `close`'s `Check(a)`, stores nothing, and the daemon reads again and retries under the same key. The key holds the mount ID because FUSE numbers requests per connection, so two mounts of one volume by one session would otherwise collide. Each request also carries the oldest request ID its mount still has outstanding, and the shard removes the mount's records below it in that slot with one range removal; when a session leaves the session table, each shard's leader removes its `r/{session}/` records in every slot in the background. The successor resumes the dead process's session, which the memfd names.
- **A crash loses the write-back buffer, and says so.** Unflushed buffered writes die with the process, as dirty pages die in a kernel crash, but here the application keeps running. The memfd lists the handles holding unflushed data, and the successor fails the next `write()`, `fsync` or `close` on each with `EIO` ([Errors after acknowledgment](#client-design)), so the loss is never silent.
- **Mount settings.** The daemon negotiates `FUSE_PARALLEL_DIROPS`; without it, one unanswered `LOOKUP` blocks every lookup in its directory for the whole handoff. It never negotiates a request timeout shorter than the worst-case restart, since an expired request aborts the whole mount. It never unmounts on exit and does not rely on `auto_unmount`; unmounting belongs to CSI `NodeUnpublishVolume`.
- **No FUSE over io_uring.** Since Linux 6.14 a daemon can take requests from per-CPU io_uring queues instead of reading `/dev/fuse` (`FUSE_OVER_IO_URING`). That transport loses requests in a crash, on Linux 6.18.44 and mainline alike. `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` does not requeue requests the dead process had fetched from a queue, so they block until the mount is aborted. A request handed to the dying process fails in the application with `ECANCELED`, in 2 of 20 crashes under load. Requests queued while nothing serves wait for an unrelated request from the same CPU, and a connection whose ring was ready can never go back to `/dev/fuse`. So the daemon never negotiates it; whether to revisit that once the kernel requeues these requests is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).
- **The frontend speaks `/dev/fuse` itself.** A successor must adopt a descriptor with saved `INIT` parameters, write `FUSE_NOTIFY_RESEND` and echo the full 64-bit request ID. libfuse 3.18 does none of these in its default build. go-fuse v2.12.0 does only the echo: on an adopted descriptor it takes the first request for `INIT` and runs on with zero kernel settings, and it cannot send a resend. go-fuse also starts a goroutine per request, its filesystem API returns each reply from the call that received the request, and even its transport-free `ProtocolServer` allocates per request; all three break the [runtime rules](#runtime-in-go). So the FUSE protocol is a hand-written, fuzzed codec in a sans-IO package, about 15 opcodes, which took about 200 lines of C in the spike. The frontend's driver reads and writes non-blocking `/dev/fuse` descriptors in the event loops, as it does sockets; how one mount's requests spread across loops is an [open question](#roadmap-and-open-questions).

Block volumes have the same problem: a ublk device disappears when its server exits, unless it was created with ublk's user recovery. The block frontend creates every device with `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY` and `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY_REISSUE`, as SPDK's ublk target does, plus `UBLK_F_QUIESCE` on kernels that have it (6.16 and later):

- **The kernel keeps the device.** Once the server's `/dev/ublkcN` is closed, `/dev/ublkbN` stays, with its size, parameters and flags in the kernel, and new requests wait in the block layer, without a timeout, until a successor sends `START_USER_RECOVERY`, opens `/dev/ublkcN`, fetches on every queue and sends `END_USER_RECOVERY` ([`ublk_drv.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/drivers/block/ublk_drv.c)). `/dev/ublkcN` admits one opener, and recovery starts only after its last holder closes it, so unlike `/dev/fuse` no other process holds the descriptor. The daemon stores the volume ID in the device's `ublksrv_flags`, 64 bits the kernel keeps and returns with `GET_DEV_INFO`, so a successor finds its devices without a file of its own.
- **A crash reissues; an upgrade drains.** With `REISSUE`, every request the dead server had received is requeued for the successor. With user recovery alone they would fail with `EIO`, and with `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY_FAIL_IO` so would every request until recovery. A failed write makes ext4 abort its journal and XFS shut down, so the pod would see the restart; a reissue costs it a stall. For an upgrade, `QUIESCE_DEV` stops dispatch, the old server finishes what it holds and exits, and nothing is reissued. Without `QUIESCE`, the old server finishes what it has started and exits; what it received but never sent is reissued, which is safe since none of it left the node.
- **A reissued write is idempotent, and the dead server's copy is fenced.** A block write carries no compare-and-set, so the same bytes applied twice give the same blocks under a newer version, and the block layer completes the original only when the reissue completes, so no write the pod ordered after it can come between them. The dead server's own copy can: bytes already queued on a socket still reach the primary after the process dies ([`tcp.c` `__tcp_close`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/net/ipv4/tcp.c#L3123)), and could land after the reissue and the pod's next write. So each connection's hello names the client's session and *incarnation*, the daemon's start time on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, and so does every chunk write and punch, in 8 bytes. A primary that has seen a newer incarnation of a session closes the older one's connections and rejects their writes, and it checks when it orders a write, not only when it receives one, so a write it already holds is caught too, as storage nodes reject a fenced session's writes already queued. The incarnation rides in each write because with one [event loop](#runtime-in-go) per core the old connection, the new one and the chunk's loop can be three different loops; having every hello reach every loop before its connection's writes would be an ordering rule between loops that the runtime keeps forever. The reissue names the same chunk as the copy, so it reaches the same primary, and the copy is either versioned before it or rejected. The primary's table of newest incarnations need not survive a primary change: the copy is bound to its connection, so it reaches only the primary process it was sent to, and that connection dies if the process restarts; a reissue that completed on another primary means the copy's interval was stopped before that primary activated; and a primary orders a held write again only if no other primary led the PG since ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)). The phase 2 epoch change model is to confirm this ([open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). The same rule fences the chunk writes of a dead FUSE daemon.
- **RWX block.** Another node's write to the same block between the original and its reissue is overwritten by the reissue. If the original never reached a primary, the two writes overlapped in time and either order is allowed. If it reached one and committed, it takes effect twice, and a reader on a third node can see it, the other node's write, then it again ([Single order per chunk](#data-layer-chunkstore)). KubeVirt's live migration never writes one block from two nodes at once, so it never meets this.

The holder restarts and upgrades too, and its pod and the daemon's are rolled in an order that never leaves a FUSE descriptor with neither:

- **While the holder restarts, the daemon holds.** The daemon keeps its own copy of every descriptor, so its mounts keep serving while the node plugin is down. A new holder connects to the socket, the daemon sends it every descriptor, clone and memfd again, and the holder confirms once it holds them all. Until then the daemon refuses a planned handoff, so an upgrade waits, and a daemon crash in that window aborts the node's FUSE mounts: the one case in which a restart reaches the pods. ublk devices do not depend on the holder, since the kernel keeps them.
- **The holder stores, never parses.** It keeps descriptors and memfds as opaque objects behind a small protocol of its own (hold, list, give back), versioned apart from the daemon, so the memfd's contents can change in any release without a new holder. The node plugin's other link to the daemon, its mount and unmount calls, is the daemon's API, and a daemon accepts the calls of the previous release's plugin. So an upgrade rolls the daemon first.
- **One node, one pod at a time.** Kubernetes rolls each DaemonSet on its own, so under the default `RollingUpdate` a chart upgrade could replace a node's daemon and node plugin at overlapping times and drop every descriptor. Both DaemonSets therefore use `updateStrategy: OnDelete`: the chart changes their specs and restarts nothing, and `rock-solid upgrade` walks the nodes one at a time. On each it deletes the daemon pod and waits until the successor has adopted every mount and device and the holder holds every descriptor, then deletes the node plugin pod and waits until the new one holds every descriptor. Before the next node it waits until no PG with a member on this one lacks a member or still recovers, as Ceph's upgrades check `ok-to-stop`, so an upgrade never takes two members of a PG down at once.

### What is not free

- File metadata speed is bounded by MetaStore round trips, so small-file workloads never match a local disk. Every shared POSIX system pays this. Tier 3's pushed directory invalidations let caches live longer.
- While a file is write-shared, its reported mtime changes on every `stat` (a side effect of revalidation). Tools that compare mtimes on a file that another node is actively writing may notice.
- SQLite in WAL mode works from one node at a time only. Its WAL index is a `-shm` file that every connection maps and updates through shared memory, which no filesystem keeps coherent across nodes, and SQLite's own documentation says WAL does not work over a network filesystem. Tier 3's byte-range locks let several nodes share a database in rollback-journal mode, never in WAL mode.
- SMB for macOS and Windows clients would be a Samba gateway over the file frontend, and is out of scope for v1. The [Samba spike](#appendix-samba-gateway-spike) found that Samba's leases, share modes and lock state are per gateway, so a gateway on our filesystem needs a VFS module to see writes from other nodes.

## Block frontend

Block volumes use the same ChunkStore path and never touch the MetaStore on the hot path.

**Block 4K write**

```
pod write() → kernel block layer → userspace block device (ublk) in node daemon
  → chunk_id = (volume_id, 0, offset / CHUNK_SIZE) // computed
  → pg = (pool, top bits of xxh3_64(chunk_id))     // computed
  → placement_table[pg] → primary, interval        // in-memory, per-PG interval
  → primary: log append ‖ send to the other replicas → acks → done
```

**RWO:** a single-writer attach record is taken in the MetaStore at attach. It prevents two nodes from mounting one volume and corrupting it. Remote clients use NVMe over Fabrics over TCP (NVMe-oF/TCP) instead of ublk.

**Logical block size.** A block volume exposes 4 KiB logical and physical blocks (ublk's `logical_bs_shift` and `physical_bs_shift` 12), `io_min` 4 KiB and `io_opt` the chunk size, so the kernel never sends a write smaller than a block and the block path never merges. Vitastor exposes its 4 KiB `bitmap_granularity` as the sector size the same way, while krbd keeps the kernel's default 512 bytes and BlueStore merges each 512-byte write on every replica. KubeVirt guests, which see 512-byte sectors unless their disk sets a `blockSize`, are padded by QEMU's own read-modify-write (`bdrv_padding_rmw_read`). The StorageClass parameter `block_size: 512` exposes 512-byte logical blocks for guests and tools that need them; the primary [merges](#data-layer-chunkstore) them like a file's partial blocks, which keeps RWX block correct when two nodes write sectors of one block, at the cost of a base read and a 4 KiB block per write. The size is fixed when the volume is created and inherited by its clones and restores, since XFS refuses a device whose logical block is larger than the sector size it was formatted with (`bdev_validate_blocksize` in `fs/xfs/xfs_buf.c`).

**RWX block (Tier 0):** several nodes attach one raw device; the homelab user is KubeVirt live migration. The changes are a multi-writer attach record and no caching in the daemon, so every I/O goes to the chunk primary. The kernel's own cache for the block device is the application's choice: KubeVirt opens shared disks with `cache=none` (direct I/O), which is what makes live migration safe.

**A full pool pauses a volume's new writes; it never fails them.** A write the pool refuses at [`write_limit`](#volume-size-and-capacity) because it needs new space is held until the pool has room again: the daemon retries it whenever the control plane reports that the pool's available figure has grown, with no time limit. Failing it costs more than holding it. In the [block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike), a hold on dm-thin that ended when the pool grew completed every held write with no error, new filesystem metadata among them, and ext4 and XFS checked clean. Failing the writes instead lost their data, although `fsync` reported `ENOSPC`, and the refused writes included metadata in blocks the device had never stored: the leaf of a file's extent tree, the inode chunks and directory blocks of new files. ext4 aborted its journal at the first such write and refused every later write, overwrites included, although `errors=continue`; XFS retried its metadata for as long as it stayed mounted, but shut down at unmount. Either way the image on disk was inconsistent until a mount replayed the log, and the replay writes the same new blocks, so it needs the room the pool lacked: failing needs the same space as holding, plus a remount, and still loses data. dm-thin's default, a 60 s hold that then fails with `BLK_STS_NOSPC`, does the same a minute later, and a grow too small to finish the held writes restarts its timeout. ublk, unlike NVMe, never times a request out (`ublk_timeout` resets the timer for a privileged device), so nothing forces a limit. Reads, discards and overwrites of blocks the volume holds live and unshared carry on, since they pass `write_limit`, so the filesystem keeps its journal; but held writes keep their ublk tags, and ext4's journal commit waits for the data it orders (`journal_finish_inode_data_buffers`, `fs/jbd2/commit.c`), so a volume that keeps writing soon stops altogether, as a Ceph RBD image pauses while its cluster is full. Its tasks sleep uninterruptibly, the kernel warns about them after 120 s, and its pod cannot be killed until the writes complete. That is the trade: a hang that ends without loss once space appears, instead of an outage that needs the same space and a remount, and loses data. While writes are held, the node plugin posts a Warning event on the PVC naming the pool, the pool's condition is `Full`, and a metric counts held writes per volume. There are three ways out, in this order:

1. Free space outside the volume: delete snapshots, which are what make overwrites need new space, delete other volumes, or add a disk. The writes resume by themselves.
2. Raise the pool's `write_limit` into the room above it. The writes resume at once, and space grants and recovery have less room.
3. `rock-solid volume fail-held <volume>` completes the held writes with `ENOSPC`, for when the pod must go. Their data is lost; ext4 then refuses every write and XFS shuts down at unmount, and the filesystem is consistent again once it is mounted with room in the pool, which replays its log.

**A hold must not take the node with it.** The pod's processes wait on held writes in uninterruptible sleep, so the pod cannot be killed until they resume, and deleting it waits too. Nothing else on the node may wait with it: the node plugin answers `NodeGetVolumeStats` for a block volume from the daemon's figures, never from the filesystem on the device, and the daemon warns at start when `kernel.hung_task_panic` is set, since the kernel's hung-task detector then panics the node once a write is held past `hung_task_timeout_secs` (120 s by default). Whether kubelet keeps the node Ready while a pod's processes sleep on held writes for hours is for a real cluster to show ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)).

**Remote clients.** An NVMe over Fabrics host cannot be held forever: Linux times a command out after 30 s (`nvme_core.io_timeout`) and retries a status without Do Not Retry at most 5 times (`nvme_core.max_retries`, [`drivers/nvme/host/core.c`](https://github.com/torvalds/linux/blob/v6.18/drivers/nvme/host/core.c#L399)). So the target holds a write at most 20 s, then answers Capacity Exceeded without Do Not Retry and with a Command Retry Delay that names the longest delay its controller advertises, 6,553.5 s, the most the field holds; the host requeues the write after that delay. A stock host so pauses about 9 hours before it fails the write with `BLK_STS_NOSPC`, after which its filesystem stops as above; a client that must never fail raises `nvme_core.max_retries`, up to 255, about 19 days.

**Discard is on, and restart is invisible.** The device advertises discard, and the CSI node plugin formats a `volumeMode: Filesystem` block volume (ext4, or the StorageClass's `fsType`) and mounts it with `discard`, so files the pod deletes return their space to the pool at once as [punches](#freeing-space), instead of staying allocated in the pool until it is full. In the [block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike), without discard a write after deleting a 512 MiB file still failed, because the freed space never reached the device; with it, the write succeeded. A scheduled `fstrim`, weekly in util-linux's timer, would leave a full pool full until its next run, and a punch costs one log entry, so online discard is cheap here. A raw block volume's consumer sends its own discards, as a VM guest does when its disk passes them. Devices are created with ublk's user recovery and reissue, so they survive a [daemon restart](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade) with requests in flight.

## Snapshots and clones

Volume snapshots are an optional CSI capability. Once the driver has it, backup tools such as Velero and Kasten work with no further integration: Velero's data mover creates a temporary PVC from the snapshot, mounts it and uploads it with Kopia. Without snapshots, Velero falls back to file-system backup from the live mount, which is not point-in-time.

**CSI side.**

- Advertise the `CREATE_DELETE_SNAPSHOT` controller capability (`CreateSnapshot`, `DeleteSnapshot`, `ListSnapshots`) and `CLONE_VOLUME`, and support `CreateVolume` from a snapshot. Backup needs restore too, since the data mover reads through a restored PVC.
- Ship the `csi-snapshotter` sidecar in the controller pod and a `VolumeSnapshotClass`. The snapshot CRDs and `snapshot-controller` are installed once per cluster and are missing from many distributions, so the Helm chart installs them optionally.
- `csi-sanity` already has snapshot tests.

**A snapshot is a crash-consistent cut.** It holds what the volume would hold had every node using it crashed at one instant: every write acknowledged before the cut and none issued after. Each chunk has its own primary, so no single chunk can define that instant. The cut is taken at the writers instead, with the same fan-out and fencing as a [Tier 2 recall](#cache-coherence-tiers):

```
snapshot(volume):
  E = next snapshot epoch of the volume
  to every node with the volume attached or mounted, in parallel:
    pause new writes and namespace operations on the volume
    wait until in-flight ones are acknowledged, then ack    // or its session expires and is fenced
  txn: put volume/{id}/snap/{E}, plus a marker key in each shard holding fs/{volume}/   // one shard until phase 13
  resume every node; its writes now carry snapshot epoch E
```

**A paused node resumes only once it knows the cut's outcome.** The cut is a two-phase commit: pausing prepares it and `volume/{id}/snap/{E}` commits it. A node whose coordinator crashed cannot resume on a timeout alone: if E committed and the node resumed on the old epoch, its next writes would not freeze, and the snapshot would hold a post-cut write. So it asks the MetaStore whether E exists. How the coordinator's commit is made conditional, so that a cut a node gave up on can never commit later, and how a node that attaches or mounts the volume during the cut is paused, are for the phase 11 model of the cut to settle.

Writes pause for about one round trip to every node using the volume. Tagging writes without the pause would be cheaper but wrong: a node still on the old snapshot epoch could write data derived from a post-cut write it read from another node, and the snapshot would hold an effect without its cause. A block RWO volume has one node to pause; a volume nobody is using needs no pause.

The daemon's [write-back buffer](#client-design) is not flushed by default. Unflushed data is what a crash would lose too, and closed files are always included because `close` flushes. The `VolumeSnapshotClass` parameter `flush: true` makes each node flush its buffer for the volume before it acks. Application consistency (a database's own flush, `fsfreeze`) comes from backup-tool hooks.

**Data: copy-on-write per block.** A write carrying snapshot epoch E to a chunk last written before E freezes the chunk's current version for the snapshot with a `freeze` entry in the [log](#on-disk-format), or version 0, the absent chunk, if it has never been written, then applies. The frozen and live versions share every 4K block the write does not touch, so the first write after a snapshot writes only the 4K blocks it touches, merged whole, not a 4 MiB chunk copy; the [on-disk format](#on-disk-format) shares them without reference counts. In an erasure-coded pool a frozen version keeps the form its chunk has. While the chunk is held as copies, its frozen versions are copies too, sharing blocks with the live version as above; when the chunk has been idle for `seal_after`, the [seal](#when-chunks-are-encoded) encodes every version, live and frozen, each into shards of its own, since it reads the whole chunk anyway. Encoding a frozen version alone, as soon as it froze, would read and write the whole chunk on the first 4 KiB write after a snapshot, the cost per-block copy-on-write exists to avoid. Encoded versions share nothing, so each frozen version of a sealed chunk costs a full encoded chunk, 1.5× in `ec4-2`, where as copies it cost 3× only the blocks that differ. A write to an encoded chunk after a snapshot [returns](#when-chunks-are-encoded) only the live version to copies and keeps the encoded version as the frozen one. A backup reads the snapshot with `read(at_ver)`, which [any PG member](#data-layer-chunkstore) holding the version can serve, so backups don't load the primaries.

**Metadata.** On each shard, the marker key's Raft log index is the cut. When the shard applies the marker, it opens a point-in-time view of its LSM tree, the same kind of view a Raft snapshot is built from, and copies the `fs/{volume}/` range out of it into `fs/{snapshot}/` in the background. The view is closed once the copy is done, so it never holds back space reclamation for the life of the snapshot.

**Restore and clone.** A restored volume gets a new `volume_id` and a parent pointer to `(source volume, E)`:

- **Metadata** is copied from `fs/{snapshot}/` into the new volume's range: one pass over its inodes and directory entries, no data. Orphaned inodes are dropped, since nothing has them open.
- **Data** is read through: a read of a block that chunk `(new, inode, k)` has not written falls through to `(source, inode, k)` as it was at snapshot epoch E, at the cost of an extra hop. Writes land in the new volume, block by block. Inode numbers are unchanged, so `st_ino` survives a restore.
- **Clone from a PVC** (`CLONE_VOLUME`) is a snapshot followed by a restore.
- **Flatten.** With the StorageClass setting `flatten: true` (off by default), a background task copies every chunk the new volume has not written yet, then drops the parent pointer. Off saves space and suits Velero's temporary PVC, which is read once and deleted; a long-lived restored volume can turn it on. A restore into a different pool always flattens. To change a volume's pool without a new PVC, [move it](#moving-a-volume-to-another-pool).

**Deletion.** Deleting a snapshot removes its record at once. A sweep over the volume's PGs then drops each frozen version that no remaining snapshot and no unflattened clone needs. A deleted volume that still has snapshots or clones is hidden from Kubernetes and keeps those chunks until they are gone. Snapshot space counts toward the pool (see [Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)).

## S3 frontend (later)

```
stream body → fresh chunk ids → ChunkStore.write (once each; encoded on write in an erasure-coded pool) → seal
  → MetaStore.txn([put "bucket/key" → chunk list, if-not-exists or version check])
LIST → MetaStore.scan(prefix)
```

The object becomes visible atomically in that one transaction.

## Frontend comparison

|  | File (RWX) | Block | Object (S3) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Write pattern | Byte-range writes, appends | 4K overwrites in place | Whole object, written once |
| Writers | Many, coordinated per file by cache mode | One (RWO), or many with app coordination (RWX block) | Many, atomic per key |
| Metadata | Heavy: directories, rename, stat, opens | Almost none | Sorted key index |
| MetaStore on hot path | Open, close, fsync, namespace operations; extending writes while write-shared | Never (attach only) | Once per PUT commit, and on LIST |
| Redundancy, in an erasure-coded pool | Encoded as written; copies while shared or edited, encoded once idle | Copies; chunks idle for a day encoded | Encoded as written |
| Snapshot cut | Pause every node with the volume mounted | Pause the attached node (or nodes, for RWX block) | None |
| Hot cost | Metadata round trips | Latency at queue depth 1 | Throughput, listing |

## Modularity: sans-IO core, pluggable drivers

All protocol and replication logic, including the coherence protocol, is written as pure state machines that take events and return actions. They never touch real I/O, so the simulator can stand in for every driver, one seed replays a whole cluster run, and every input/output (I/O) backend is swappable.

```go
// OnEvent appends its actions to out and returns it; the caller reuses out, so a call allocates nothing.
func (r *ChunkReplica) OnEvent(ctx *Context, ev *Event, out []Action) []Action

// Context carries the current time on CLOCK_BOOTTIME and on CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW (1 tick = 1 µs) and the driver's buffer pool.
type Context struct {
	Now  Tick
	Raw  Tick
	Pool BufPool
}

// Event and Action are tagged structs, not interfaces, so passing one never allocates.
type Event struct {
	Kind  EventKind // NetRecv, PeerLost, DiskDone, Timer, Resumed
	Peer  NodeID
	Msg   Msg
	IO    IOID
	Err   DiskError
	Timer TimerID
}

type Action struct {
	Kind   ActionKind // NetSend, DiskRead, DiskWrite, DiskFsync, DiskTrim, SetTimer
	Peer   NodeID
	Msg    Msg
	IO     IOID
	LBA    LBA
	Buf    IOBuf
	Vec    []IOVec // a DiskWrite gathers these pool buffers, or writes Buf when Vec is empty
	Len    uint64
	Linked bool // a DiskFsync that runs only once the DiskWrite just before it completes
	Timer  TimerID
	At     Tick
}

// IOVec is the first Len bytes of a pool buffer, one element of a vectored write.
type IOVec struct {
	Buf IOBuf
	Len uint32
}
```

**Rules for state machines:**

- **No allocation per call.** `Event` and `Action` are tagged structs, not interfaces, and `OnEvent` appends to a slice the caller owns and reuses. Hot-path structs are filled in place, not built as temporaries and copied: a frame literal copied into a slice cost 534 ns, filled in place 177 ns ([codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)).
- **Time comes in, waiting goes out.** A machine reads time only from `ctx.Now` and `ctx.Raw`. It never blocks: it records what it waits for under an `IOID` or `TimerID`, and the matching event resumes it.
- **Deterministic.** No goroutine, channel, `select`, `sync` or `sync/atomic`; no clock, `os`, `net`, `syscall` or `unsafe`; no `range` over a map, since iteration order shapes the order of emitted actions (iterate an ordered structure or sorted keys). Go randomizes map iteration on purpose (`internal/runtime/maps/table.go`). Randomness comes only from a seeded `math/rand/v2` source (PCG or ChaCha8) the machine owns. In the simulator the run's seed gives a source that forks the scheduler's own child first, for its choices, then one child per node in node order, and each node's forks one per machine as the node creates it (`Rand.Fork`), so a draw added in one machine shifts no other machine's stream.
- **Errors are data.** A corrupt message or a failed disk is an event to handle. A panic is only for a broken invariant of the machine's own state, never for peer or disk input.
- **Protocol logic lives in the core**, even when only one frontend uses it today: who may cache, when to recall, what is fenced. A frontend only translates between FUSE, ublk or S3 requests and events and actions.
- **Tested by feeding events.** Unit tests feed events and assert on the returned actions, with no mocks. A new machine registers its invariants with the simulator's checker in the same change; a seed that finds a bug becomes a regression test before the fix; a new protocol is modeled in TLA+ before it is written.

A `go vet` analyzer (`tools/sansio`) enforces the deterministic rules in every sans-IO package: everything under `internal/` except `loop`, `driver/…`, `frontend/…` and `csi` ([package layout](#package-layout)). Test files may read their `testdata`. The loop and driver packages get a narrower rule set of their own ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)).

A driver executes `Action`s and feeds back `Event`s. Chunk data, Raft logs and indexes all live in the [disk log](#on-disk-format), so the disk driver is the only code that touches storage:

| Slot | Default driver | Opt-in fast driver | Test driver |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Disk | `io_uring` on a file or raw device | SPDK on a dedicated NVMe drive | Simulated disk with fault model |
| Network | Kernel TCP | RDMA (real ConnectX or Soft-RoCE); DPDK as an experiment | Simulated network |
| Clock and timers | `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, which counts through suspend, and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, which no time daemon slews, read together each pass for [lease time](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection) (1 tick = 1 µs), with the VMClock disruption marker from a mapped `/dev/vmclock0` where the VM has one; timers on `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` | Same | Simulated time: per-node oscillator drift, time-daemon slews, suspends and VM pauses; the event loop's own tests take the same simulated clocks through its [kernel seam](#runtime-in-go) |
| Frontend | FUSE client; ublk + CSI driver | NVMe-oF/TCP for remote block clients | Simulated clients with a model page cache that record histories |

**Driver contract.** The [bypass-drivers spike](#appendix-kernel-bypass-drivers-spike) showed that SPDK and RDMA fit this slot, but only if the first three rules hold from the first (default) driver; the [open-loop latency spike](#appendix-open-loop-latency-spike) added the fourth:

- **Data buffers come from the driver's pool, and the core frees them.** `IOBuf` is a handle from a buffer pool the driver provides, never an arbitrary heap allocation. NVMe under SPDK can only DMA from SPDK-registered memory, and RDMA can only send from registered memory; one stray buffer breaks the whole RDMA connection, and registering per I/O costs as much as the I/O. The default drivers use a slab mapped outside the Go heap ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)), the simulator a plain pool. The pool has an explicit size: without hugepages, vfio limits SPDK to just under 256 MB of DMA memory (65,535 mappings of 4 KB pages), so the SPDK pool defaults to 128 MB and needs hugepages above that. An exhausted pool is an error the machine handles, typically by retrying on a timer, never a panic or a fallback to the heap. Control messages up to 256 bytes go inline and need no pool buffer.

  The core allocates with `Alloc` for buffers it overwrites (like disk reads) or `AllocZeroed` when it needs clean memory, and it frees every buffer, including the data a `NetRecv` delivers. A buffer named in an action stays with the driver until the action is over, and the core must not free it before then:

  | Action | The driver is done with the buffer when |
  | --- | --- |
  | `DiskRead`, `DiskWrite` | its `DiskDone` arrives |
  | `NetSend` | the peer's reply arrives, or `PeerLost` for that peer |

  A send reports no completion of its own, so a peer answers every request that carries data, even if only to reject it. The driver releases a send's buffer before it delivers the reply (an RDMA driver drains send completions before receive completions) and releases all of a peer's buffers before it emits that peer's `PeerLost`. A timeout gives up on the result, not the buffer: the buffer stays allocated until the reply or `PeerLost`. One buffer can go to several peers in a fan-out and is freed once every one of them is done with it. `IOBuf` is a plain value (a slot number and a generation), so the type system doesn't track any of this; the simulator's pool checks a per-slot generation and fills `Alloc`ed memory with a poison byte, which catches stale handles and reads of unwritten memory.
- **Every driver exposes a readiness fd and defaults to event mode.** SPDK's thread interrupt fd and the RDMA completion-channel fd both sit in the loop's epoll set. Busy polling is a separate opt-in for dedicated cores. SPDK's NVMe driver supports event mode on PCIe drives bound to `vfio-pci`, with one MSI-X eventfd per queue pair ([SPDK NVMe interrupt spike](#appendix-spdk-nvme-interrupt-spike)). Elsewhere (`uio_pci_generic`, or interrupts that can't be enabled) it silently attaches in polling mode, and a loop that sleeps on its fd then hangs. So the driver reads the mode back after attach and fails the disk with a clear error, unless the operator gave it a busy-polled core.
- **Network connections fail as a unit.** A transport error on a connection becomes one `PeerLost` event, every outstanding request on it fails, and the driver reconnects. The core keeps its own deadlines and never relies on transport timeouts. `SetTimer` on an armed `TimerID` replaces its deadline, and there is no cancel: a `Timer` event can still arrive for a deadline that was replaced or no longer matters, so the core checks its own deadline against `ctx.Now` before acting on one.
- **Every pass of an event loop does bounded work.** A pass reads at most a fixed budget from each connection (256 KiB in the spike), the bytes queued for replication are capped and stop the loop reading clients while over the cap, and output buffers are rings or compacted in place rather than grown. A loop that reads each socket until it would block makes each pass slower the more is queued: under overload both the Rust and the Go replication harnesses ran away into multi-second stalls with up to 200 MB buffered and most cores idle, Go from 30,000 writes/s because growing a slice copies it, Rust above 90,000. With the budget, neither dropped a request up to 100,000 writes/s.

**Transport negotiation.** Every pair of [event loops](#runtime-in-go) that talk connects over TCP first, then upgrades to RDMA if both ends report a usable device. RDMA needs that TCP channel anyway, to exchange queue pair numbers and addresses. The daemon loads `libibverbs` at runtime, so one binary runs on nodes with and without rdma-core, and a missing library, device or memory-lock allowance means TCP, not an error. Mixed clusters work without configuration. The daemon pod always carries `IPC_LOCK`, which the io_uring driver's registered slabs already need ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)), so RDMA's buffer pool can be registered too.

### Runtime in Go

The daemon runs the state machines inside event loops tuned to what the Go spikes measured, on one 4-vCPU cloud VM with Go 1.27.0 against the same designs in Rust 1.99.1:

| Question | Measurement |
| --- | --- |
| CPU per replicated 4K write, same epoll design ([tuned Go spike](#appendix-tuned-go-spike)) | Go 1.21× Rust; 1.5 to 1.7× with a goroutine per connection, whatever its synchronization |
| Where that comes from ([codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)) | Not the compiler: the generated code costs about the same per write. About 61% is runtime scheduling (`sysmon` retaking the P of a loop blocked in `epoll_wait`), 16% syscall wrappers |
| After tuning the runtime (codegen spike) | **Go 1.07× Rust** with `GOMAXPROCS` above the blocking loops, raw syscalls for non-blocking I/O, and memory touched before use |
| Compute-bound code ([Go CPU spike](#appendix-go-cpu-per-operation-spike), tuned Go spike) | Go 1.1 to 1.25× Rust; PGO, `GOAMD64=v3` and bounds checks each under 3.5% |
| Rising open-loop load ([open-loop latency spike](#appendix-open-loop-latency-spike)) | Same throughput up to 100,000 writes/s; Go's p99 knee 1.2× earlier (measured before the runtime tuning above) |
| `GOMAXPROCS` under a CPU limit ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)) | Ps above the limit throttled in up to 6.5% of 100 ms periods; as many Ps as loops starves the collector and workers; loops = Go's default − 1 never throttled |
| Garbage collector ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike), tuned Go spike) | Pauses under 1 ms. A memtable with a pointer per entry costs 23 to 35% of CPU in collection; in a Go arena 9%; as pointer-free 4 KiB pages 0% (under 3 ms of CPU per collection at 20 million entries) |

The rules below are what keep the data path near 1.07×; without them it costs 1.5 to 1.7× the CPU, a quarter of it in collection. They apply to the data path: the sans-IO packages, the event loops, the drivers and the frontends' request handling. Code off the data path (the CSI gRPC server, metrics, the admin API, the FUSE descriptor holder) is ordinary Go with goroutines, `net/http` and `context`. Each serial owner (a hot PG primary, a disk's commit loop, a busy shard) still reaches its throughput ceiling and latency knee somewhat earlier than in Rust, so headroom is planned per owner, from latency against offered load.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph LOOP["Event loop goroutine (one per core)"]
        direction TB
        EP["epoll_wait"] --> SM["OnEvent of the owning state machine"]
        SM --> EX["execute actions: writev, io_uring submit, timer heap"]
        EX --> EP
    end
    SOCK["peer and client sockets"] --> EP
    URING["io_uring completion eventfd"] --> EP
    TFD["timerfd: earliest deadline"] --> EP
    INBOX["inbox: lock-free queue + eventfd"] --> EP
    WORK["worker goroutines: cleaner copies, erasure coding"] --> INBOX
    OTHER["other loops"] --> INBOX
```

*A loop sleeps only in `epoll_wait`; everything that can wake it is a descriptor in its set.*

**Event loops**

- **One loop per core** (one fewer under a CPU limit, see below). A goroutine runs `epoll` through `golang.org/x/sys/unix` on raw non-blocking descriptors, and owns its shards, the logs and indexes of its disks and its connections outright. Never `net.Conn` or a goroutine per connection on the data path: 1.21× Rust's CPU against 1.5 to 1.7×. A disk's log belongs to one loop ([on-disk format](#on-disk-format)). A node with fewer disks than loops spreads its shards over every loop by load, and each loop hands its records to the disk's loop through the inbox, batched once per pass, since every hop costs a wakeup. A node with more disks than loops gives a loop several disks, each with its own log and flush.
- **Connections are per loop pair, and a request goes to the loop that owns its target.** Each node publishes which loop owns each of its disks and shard replicas, in its membership record and whenever that changes, and a loop opens one connection to each peer loop it needs, on demand, as Seastar's shard-aware clients do. A chunk write from loop A on one node to a disk that loop B owns on another travels A → B and its reply B → A, with no other core in the path. One connection per node pair would instead hand nearly every request and reply through an inbox, two cross-core wakeups per operation, or pin a node pair to one core. A request that reaches the wrong loop, after a shard moved, is forwarded through the inbox and its reply names the new owner, so the owner map is a hint for performance, never a condition for correctness. A 10-node cluster of 8-loop nodes holds at most 576 sockets per node.
- **One spare P, and no more Ps than the CPU limit.** With as many busy loops as Ps, `sysmon` retakes a blocked loop's P (`runtime/proc.go`, `retake`), costing futex wakeups and CPU migrations (−2.9 µs per write with a spare P, futex calls from 0.21 to 0.02 per write), and the collector and worker goroutines wait for a P while the loops do (scheduler p99 7 to 25 ms). Under a Kubernetes CPU limit, Ps beyond the limit let the process run more threads than its CFS quota pays for, and the kernel stalls all of them until the next 100 ms period: 1.6 to 6.5% of periods at 2 CPUs ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)). So the daemon reads its cgroup's `cpu.max` (`cpu.cfs_quota_us` on cgroup v1) at start; Go does not export the limit, and comparing `runtime.GOMAXPROCS(0)` with `runtime.NumCPU()` misses a limit equal to the CPU count. Under a limit it runs one loop fewer than Go's default `GOMAXPROCS`, `max(2, ceil(limit))`, and leaves `GOMAXPROCS` to the runtime, which follows an in-place resize within a second. With no limit it runs one loop per CPU and sets `GOMAXPROCS` to the loop count plus one, since an extra P then costs nothing. A limit takes a core of loop capacity: a 2-CPU limit gets one loop. The chart therefore asks for an integer limit of at least 3 CPUs or none. If the limit is lowered while the daemon runs, it is left with no spare P, and the daemon reports that until it restarts.
- **Raw syscalls where nothing blocks.** Reads, writes and sends on non-blocking descriptors use `unix.RawSyscall`; `epoll_wait` and anything else that can block use `unix.Syscall`: −1.4 µs per write. The loop never waits inside `io_uring_enter`: the io_uring driver runs in event mode with its completions signalled on its eventfd, drains a full completion queue within the pass and defers what a full submission queue cannot take to the next pass.
- **Loops are not locked to OS threads** (35 against 29 µs per write), except where a C library requires its own thread, as SPDK's `spdk_thread` does.
- **A loop waits only on its epoll set.** A goroutine blocked in `epoll_wait` cannot wait on a channel too, so everything that can wake a loop is a descriptor: its sockets, its io_uring completion eventfd, one timerfd armed for the earliest deadline in the loop's timer heap, and one eventfd for its inbox. Other loops and worker goroutines hand it work through a lock-free multi-producer queue and write the eventfd only when the queue was empty, so a burst costs one wakeup. No channel, `select`, `time.Timer` or `time.AfterFunc` on the data path.
- **Time and waiting go through one seam.** Everything a loop asks of the kernel that involves time or waiting is one small interface in `internal/loop`, so its tests can put scripted clocks behind it ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)):

  ```go
  // Kernel is everything a loop asks of the kernel that involves time or waiting.
  type Kernel interface {
  	Wait(ready []Ready, block bool) []Ready // epoll_wait; block is the loop's own decision
  	Clocks() core.Clocks                    // CLOCK_BOOTTIME, CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW, CLOCK_MONOTONIC and the VMClock marker, read together
  	Arm(deadline core.Tick)                 // the loop's timerfd: CLOCK_BOOTTIME, absolute deadline
  }
  ```

  A pass (`Loop.Pass`, which `Run` repeats) waits first and reads the clocks after the wait returns. It then delivers `Resumed` if the detector reports it, fires every timer due by `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, handles events with peer messages before client reads, executes actions and arms the timerfd. So every event is handled at a time no earlier than when it became ready, as in FoundationDB's loop, which sleeps, collects what is ready and only then reads its clock and fires its timers (`flow/Net2.cpp`). Reading the clocks before the wait would be unsafe for [leases](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection): a suspend during the wait would surface a pass late, after it served the reads that woke it on the old lease; a lease-expiry timer and a read that became ready together would be handled at a `Now` from before the expiry; and a grant's arrival `r` would be recorded early by the length of the wait, which makes the grantor-rate bound too low, the unsafe direction. The seam's other rules:
  - **The loop decides when to block.** `Loop.Pending` reports what it still has to do (work, the next deadline, kernel I/O in flight), and the loop blocks in `Wait` only when it has no queued inbox item, deferred submission or unspent read budget.
  - **Timers do not wait on the timerfd's readiness.** `Wait` drains the timerfd's expiration count, and every pass fires the timers the heap holds due. `Arm` is called only when the heap's earliest deadline changed, so a pass makes at most one `timerfd_settime` and usually none. The timer heap and the `Resumed` detector are pure functions in `internal/core`, and the simulated network delivers `Resumed` through the same detector.
  - **Trace records take their tick from the pass's clock reading**, never from a clock read of their own.
  - **The real `Clocks` brackets its reads**: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, read again if the two `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` readings lie more than ε apart. Without the bracket, a stall between the reads, from descheduling or the CFS throttling the [GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike) measured, would look like a suspend and end every lease the node holds. The real kernel's constructor maps `/dev/vmclock0`; without one the marker reads 0.
  - **Nothing else in `internal/loop` or `internal/driver/…` reads a clock.** `tools/sansio` has a rule set for those packages: no `time.Now`, `time.Since`, `time.Sleep`, `time.Timer` or `time.AfterFunc`, no channel operation or `select`, and no `range` over a map.

  The seam costs three interface calls per pass, a few ns (estimated, not measured), and two `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` reads through the vDSO, the kernel's syscall-free fast path, beside the two raw clock reads the leases already need. Nothing allocates ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)).
- **Bounded passes** and **one `writev` per descriptor per pass**, gathering every frame ready for it, as the [driver contract](#modularity-sans-io-core-pluggable-drivers) requires: syscalls set the cost of a write, and batching halved Rust's and cut Go's by 29%. A slice grown by `append` copies itself (`runtime/slice.go`), which is why Go collapsed from 30,000 writes/s without the bound.
- **Traces, not logs.** A loop records every event, action and syscall as a fixed-field trace record and never writes a free-form log line ([Testing the design decisions](#testing-the-design-decisions)).
- **Disk I/O goes through io_uring**, on a ring per loop that the driver maps and drives with raw syscalls, since Go's standard library has none. The driver registers the loop's I/O slab once (`IORING_REGISTER_BUFFERS`, as regions of at most 1 GiB, a slab that grows adding regions to a sparse table) and its disks' descriptors, and issues `READ_FIXED`/`WRITE_FIXED`. A fixed buffer saves the page pinning and unpinning of every `O_DIRECT` request: on a consumer NVMe drive, 0.1 µs of CPU per 4 KiB I/O at queue depth 32 and 0.2 to 0.3 µs at depth 1, 5 to 8% of the CPU per I/O, for a registration paid once per slab ([io_uring registered buffers spike](#appendix-io_uring-registered-buffers-spike)); a fixed descriptor saves a few ns. Pinned memory counts against the memory-lock limit, so the daemon pod carries `IPC_LOCK`. Without it, or when registration fails, the driver issues plain `READ`/`WRITE` on the same buffers and reports it.

**Memory**

- **Long-lived bulk structures hold no pointers.** The memtable is a copy-on-write B+-tree of 4 KiB pages that refer to each other by `uint32` number; the chunk index and similar tables are slices of integers. Collection then costs under 3 ms of CPU at 20 million entries instead of 1.25 CPU-seconds per cycle.
- **Bulk memory lives outside the Go heap**, in slabs mapped with `unix.Mmap` and held as `[]byte`: memtable pages, index tables, retained Raft entries and I/O buffers. A page is viewed as `*[4096]byte` by slice conversion, without `unsafe`. Three reasons: the collector's heap goal is `GOGC` percent above the live heap, pointer-free memory included, so 16 GiB of memtable on the heap would let the heap grow toward 32 GiB before a cycle; `O_DIRECT` and io_uring need page-aligned buffers, which the Go heap does not promise; and a mapped slab is registered once with io_uring (`IORING_REGISTER_BUFFERS`), SPDK or RDMA. SPDK and RDMA slabs are allocated by their C libraries instead, which cgo's pointer rules allow. Off-heap memory never holds a Go pointer, since the collector does not scan it, and it is managed by hand: a page used after it is freed reads another page's data instead of crashing, so slab handles carry a generation and the simulator poisons freed and freshly allocated memory.
- **I/O buffers are handles**, a `uint32` slot plus a generation into the driver's slab, never `sync.Pool`, which empties at collections, has no bound and cannot be registered for DMA. `sync.Pool` is for scratch off the data path.
- **No Go arenas.** `GOEXPERIMENT=arenas` is on hold upstream, and the layouts above already cost nothing to collect: pages took 0.0% of CPU in collection against 9.3% for a memtable in an arena.
- **`GOMEMLIMIT` budgets the Go heap**, which is the daemon's memory budget minus its mapped slabs, with `GOGC` at its default. Hot-path benchmarks report allocations (`b.ReportAllocs`), and CI fails one that allocates per operation.

**Concurrency outside the loops**

- **Read-mostly tables** (the placement table, the session table) are immutable values published through `atomic.Pointer`: a reader loads the current one without a lock, an update publishes a new one, and the collector frees the old.
- **Latency paths use typed atomics and fixed slot tables** instead of a mutex, a map and a channel: queue-depth-1 p50 67 against 84 µs. Counters written by different cores are padded to a cache line.
- **CPU-heavy background work runs on worker goroutines** (cleaner copies, erasure coding, compaction) that send their results to the owning loop's inbox as events, so each index keeps one writer. In stepped tests their jobs run inline as steps of the simulator's scheduler ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)). The cleaner's copy work costs Go 1.12 to 1.24× Rust.

**Libraries and build**

- **CRC32C from `hash/crc32`** (Castagnoli table), which interleaves three SSE4.2 streams on amd64 and uses the CRC32 instructions on arm64.
- **Encodings are hand-written** over `encoding/binary`'s `Append*` and `PutUint*` functions into buffers the caller provides, with no reflection (`encoding/gob`, `encoding/json`, `binary.Read`/`Write`) on the data path. Every decoder has a native fuzz test (`go test -fuzz`).
- **cgo only in the SPDK and RDMA drivers**, behind build tags so the default build is pure Go, and every crossing carries a batch: many submissions per call, many completions per poll. `unsafe` only in driver packages, for rings, cgo and registered memory.
- **Release builds use PGO** (a committed `default.pgo` from a representative profile) and ship for `amd64` at the default `GOAMD64=v1` and for `arm64`. `GOAMD64=v3` gained under 3.5% and would exclude homelab CPUs without AVX2, such as Goldmont Plus Celerons, and `hash/crc32` selects its instructions at run time either way.
- **The toolchain is pinned** in `go.mod`, and `scripts/lint.sh` runs `gofmt`, `go vet` with the sans-IO analyzer, `staticcheck`, `govulncheck` and a dependency license check.

### Package layout

One Go module, `github.com/nvcnvn/rock-solid`, with everything but the binary under `internal/`:

| Package | Contents | Sans-IO |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `internal/core` | `Event`, `Action`, `Context`, `IOBuf` and the buffer pool interface, IDs and the PG hash, CRC32C, seeded RNG, outstanding peer requests, the `Resumed` detector and the timer heap | Yes |
| `internal/disklog` | Segments, record codec, group commit, recovery, segment usage table, cleaner | Yes |
| `internal/lsm` | Page-tree memtable, sorted runs with Bloom filters, compaction, point-in-time views and restore; later a block cache | Yes |
| `internal/lease` | Per-node-pair lease grants with support epochs, expiry with the drift margin, the restart wait; used by primary read leases and Raft leader leases | Yes |
| `internal/chunkstore` | Chunk index, replica primary, pools, placement, peering, primary read leases, failure detection | Yes |
| `internal/raftshard` | etcd-raft `RawNode` wrapped in `OnEvent`, its `raft.Storage` over the disk log | Yes |
| `internal/meta` | MetaStore shard state machine, key encoding, cross-shard transactions, sessions, inode owner (open registry, cache modes, recalls) | Yes |
| `internal/fuseproto` | FUSE protocol codec: request decoding, reply and notification encoding, saved `INIT` parameters | Yes |
| `internal/sim` | Deterministic scheduler, simulated disk, network and clients, invariant checker, history recorder; in `internal/sim/toy`, a toy primary-backup protocol with bugs to plant; in `internal/sim/lincheck`, the Porcupine check of a history, kept out of the simulator since Porcupine runs goroutines | Yes |
| `internal/loop` | Event loop runtime: the kernel seam over epoll, the clocks and the timerfd, inbox, round driver | No |
| `internal/loop/looptest` | Stepped kernel on scripted clocks, and the loops as actors for `internal/sim`'s scheduler | No |
| `internal/driver/uring`, `…/tcp`, `…/spdk`, `…/rdma` | One package per backend; SPDK and RDMA use cgo behind build tags | No |
| `internal/frontend/fuse`, `…/ublk`, `…/s3` | Protocol adapters onto the core | No |
| `internal/csi` | Kubernetes CSI driver | No |
| `cmd/rock-solid` | The single binary that wires the drivers and frontends picked by flags | No |
| `tools/sansio` | The `go vet` analyzer for sans-IO packages, with a narrower rule set for the loop and driver packages | No |
| `models` | TLA+ specifications | |

## Testing strategy

Most bugs live in interleavings that a real cluster hits rarely and can't reproduce. So most testing happens in deterministic simulation, and real hardware confirms what simulation can't model.

| Layer | What it catches | Tools |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Deterministic simulation testing (DST) | Interleaving bugs across crashes, partitions, disk faults and lost recalls; reproducible from a seed | Own simulator over the sans-IO core; event loops and drivers stepped on scripted clocks by the same scheduler |
| 2. Protocol model checking | Design bugs in epoch changes (per-PG intervals, peering, read leases, Raft fortification), sessions and fencing, cache-mode recalls and purges (phase 2); snapshot cuts and pool moves (phase 11); cross-shard commit (phase 13) | TLA+ specifications checked with TLC in CI, in the style of etcd-raft's |
| 3. Consistency checking | Reads that no valid ordering could produce; close-to-open violations | Porcupine (Go), Jepsen Elle |
| 4. Real-cluster fault injection | Kernel, FUSE, firmware, fsync and RDMA behavior | device-mapper, SPDK error/delay bdevs, eBPF error injection (`bpf_override_return`), `tc netem`, `nftables`, `kill -9`, power cuts |
| 5. Heterogeneous matrix and property tests | Placement and rebalance bugs on mixed hardware | Generated matrix, seeded property tests, native Go fuzzing of every decoder |
| 6. Conformance suites | POSIX and protocol compatibility gaps | `pjdfstest`, `fsx` (single and multi-client, with end-of-file pollution `-e` and mapped operations), xfstests `generic/029` and `generic/030`, `csi-sanity`, Kubernetes storage end-to-end tests, Ceph `s3-tests` |
| 7. Performance regression | Latency, CPU and recovery regressions | `fio` and metadata benchmarks nightly → VictoriaMetrics → Grafana; eBPF off-CPU and latency profiles to explain regressions |

**1. Deterministic simulation.** All nodes, disks, clients and the network run in one goroutine from one seeded random number generator. The simulator drops, delays, reorders and duplicates messages, and crashes and restarts nodes. A failing seed replays exactly. Run thousands of seeds per commit in continuous integration (CI), and millions overnight on idle homelab machines. A change that adds a seeded test runs it over 10,000 seeds once, as Pebble stresses every test a pull request adds.

The simulated disk models:

- loss of unflushed data on crash: each unflushed block write lands or not on its own, in the order it was executed, so a crash can leave holes (a lost write before one that landed) and can keep an older write to a block without the newer one. That is stricter than the models of Pebble and RocksDB. Pebble's crashable memory filesystem keeps each unsynced 4 KiB block of a file at random, so it leaves holes too, but a block it keeps holds the file's latest contents; RocksDB's `FaultInjectionTestFS` keeps a random prefix of each file's unsynced data, so it makes neither;
- torn writes (only some 512 B sectors of a larger write land, a 4 KiB block included);
- injected errors, chosen by action kind, block range, action index and a seeded probability, as Pebble's `errorfs` chooses them: a failed write, a failed flush that leaves every unflushed block undecided, a failed read, and a read that completes with a changed byte, as RocksDB's fault injection returns damaged buffers;
- silent bit flips and misdirected writes;
- fail-slow: a disk 100x slower that never errors.

A failed write or flush is final for the open log: nothing after it is acknowledged, and the node runs recovery again rather than retrying the flush. Besides random seeds, a test crashes, and separately fails, the k-th disk action of a short workload with a checkpoint, a compaction and a cleaner pass, for every k, as Pebble's `TestErrors` and `TestDBCompactionCrash` do, so rare actions such as a manifest piece, a superblock copy or recovery's own header zeroing are never left to chance.

Group commit decodes each record it is about to write and asserts that it holds exactly the submitted items, so an encoder bug fails in the round that made it instead of in a later recovery. The LSM tree does the same for runs, as RocksDB's `paranoid_file_checks` and `force_consistency_checks` do: a flush decodes every piece it built, a merge checks its output's key order against its inputs, and the run list stays ordered by sequence number with no repeats. Both are settings (`Check`) that the packages' tests and the simulator turn on and release builds leave off; a setting rather than a build tag, as Pebble's `invariants` tag is, so the simulator chooses per run with the same binary. The cheap checks stay on in release builds ([The LSM tree](#on-disk-format)): entry counts on every flush and merge, and a CRC32C of every in-memory piece a merge reads or a flush rewrites, one pass 3.8 to 5.1% of the merge's CPU in the [metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike), and the two the tree makes 12% of its own, faster merge on arm64.

**Planted bugs.** The simulator proves itself on a protocol whose bugs are known. `internal/sim/toy` is a primary-backup protocol on three nodes: the primary writes each value to its disk and both replicas', resends to the replicas until one replies, and acknowledges once the value is durable locally and on one replica; every message carries the primary's incarnation. Its invariant is that every acknowledged value is durable on at least two disks, so a crash of every node and the loss of any one disk still leaves it readable, checked after every step. A setting plants one of two bugs: acknowledging once only the primary's copy is durable, and taking a replica's reply to an earlier incarnation's write as one to the restarted primary's own write of the same sequence number, which only a primary crash with that reply still in flight exposes. Each bug must be found within the seeds every commit runs, its failing seed must replay to a byte-identical trace and the same failing step, and the fixed protocol must pass the same seeds ([test scenarios](test-scenarios.md#planted-bug-check)). The toy is a sans-IO package like the others, checked by the analyzer and allocating nothing per event.

**Metamorphic runs.** An index must read the same whatever its internal choices, so `internal/lsm` and the chunk index have a metamorphic test, as Pebble's (`internal/metamorphic`). A seed generates one ops file: writes, removals, range removals, gets, floors, scans and point-in-time views, with flushes, failed checkpoints, compactions and restarts as ops; keys are reused three times in four so ops collide. The ops run under a list of option sets, each pushing one choice to an extreme: pieces of 256 bytes (512 for the chunk index, whose smaller roots list too few pieces for a flush) or 1 MiB; a flush never or after every few writes; compaction never, after every flush, held across a flush and installed between its pieces, two merges at once, a fixed run limit, or only once 40 runs wait so that a merge leaves older runs out; run filters off, or probed and answering every probe "maybe"; the scan's linear merge in place of its heap; every flush failing once before it succeeds, writes and reads between a flush's pieces, a restart after every checkpoint while no view is open, and range removals replaced by the point removals of the keys they cover. Each run writes one history line per op, and the histories must equal each other and a map model's. One runner, `internal/sim/metamorphic`, serves both indexes: it plays the structural ops at each set's cadence, keeps the live pieces and the newest manifest as the disk log would, and diffs histories. A failing seed is reduced to the fewest ops that still fail, and the reduced ops file becomes a data-driven regression case under `testdata/` before the fix. In the [metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike), two of five planted bugs showed only under the option set at an extreme.

Simulated FUSE clients model the kernel behavior the spike measured: a page cache that refills on reads, direct-I/O fixed at open, and mapped pages that are not revalidated.

Simulation faults for coherence: lost or delayed recalls, clients paused past their lease, clock skew, owner failover in the middle of a recall, a planned leader transfer while opens arrive, a fence that reaches some primaries late, a control-plane leader failover while sessions renew, a renewal answered by a deposed leader, a client that reaches the control plane but not an owner, a control-plane leader stalled past `session_lease`, and a daemon crash with requests read but unanswered, followed by their resend, including a dead daemon's chunk write delivered after its successor's reissue and the pod's next write to the same block.

Simulation faults for placement: an old primary partitioned with clients that have not seen the new table, a disk whose reads, writes or flushes stop completing while its node keeps heartbeating, as a member, as a primary and holding only shard replicas, an owner loop that stops answering, interval changes in bursts faster than peering, a member removed and added back within one recovery, a member's disk formatted again or swapped for a new drive and reported under its old disk ID, a cloned disk reporting a log ID that is already up, a node flapping every few seconds, a member that returns just before or just after `refill_after` or `degraded_refill_after`, a refill of a PG below `min_live` while its down member returns, writes acknowledged by a refill member that has not finished recovering, followed by a crash of the PG's other member, a damped member released because its PG would stay below `min_live`, a primary restarted within `node_down_after`, a primary holding client writes that pauses past `node_down_after`, skips the tables in which another primary led its PG and leads it again, a chunk's counter close to its limit, a PG split under load, a disk that turns fail-slow and then recovers, a lease holder's or grantor's `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` slewed by up to ±22.6% while its `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` drifts by up to ±4%, a grantor that reboots within `rate_window` with its raw rate changed, round trips from 50 µs to 10 ms, grants delayed by asymmetric queueing of up to 50 ms, a holder that resumes from a suspend whose length the kernel rounded to whole seconds, or one too short to reach the `Resumed` threshold, a holder whose VM stops for longer than a lease with every clock frozen, the messages sent to it queued or lost and its disruption marker changed or not, a node crash during peering, a node restarted within a lease it granted, unaligned writes from two nodes into one block, pipelined, across a primary change with a base read in flight, and a merge's base block that fails its checksum.

Simulation faults for the MetaStore: a quiescent leader's node partitioned from one follower, a leader's node suspended past its lease, a leader transfer while lease reads arrive, a configuration change while a lease is held, a voter replaced while another voter is down, the replaced voter's node returning in the middle of the swap, a crashed leader's groups campaigning together after their grants lapse, and an election while client and internal transactions are in flight, which their callers then resend.

Simulation faults for redundancy: crashes and disk losses in the middle of an encoding (on write or when idle), a return to copies or a recovery, PGs dropping below `min_live`, and losses beyond what a pool survives.

Simulation faults for freeing space: crashes in the middle of a purge or a truncate, including between a truncate's transaction and its punch of the partial block, an unlink racing a write from another node, a truncate followed by an unlink before the purger runs, an entry added inside the run the purger is consuming, a purger's `Txn` repeated after an election, and, registered with the snapshot cut in phase 11, a snapshot taken while a purge runs. Simulation faults for a full pool: the pool reaching `write_limit` while daemons buffer and other nodes write through, a heartbeat carrying a space grant lost or delayed, a control-plane failover while daemons stream under their grants, a client partitioned from the control-plane leader but not from storage, streaming under its grant, a daemon crash in the middle of a reuse window, a refill after a disk failure that moves members onto the fullest disks while grants are out, a refill blocked by a full disk, a snapshot cut while a volume overwrites near full (its overwrites then need new space; registered in phase 11), a block write held while a discard, a purge or a new disk frees space, and a write that crosses from a granted chunk into a refused one.

Simulation faults for snapshots and pool moves: a node that never acks the pause, coordinator crash between pause and resume, shard leader change at the cut, snapshot deletion while a clone reads through or flattens, and a crash in the middle of a move's copy.

Simulation faults for the on-disk format: crashes during cleaning, compaction and memtable flushes; overwrites, punches and snapshot cuts that land between a cleaner worker's copy and the owner applying it; and between a sorted run and the manifest that lists it; crashes while merges write their lanes beside checkpoint flushes, before and after a step's manifest; a freed segment reused before and after a segment header names the freeing manifest, its new header landed or not, with TRIM run, not run, or reading back as ones; a torn record at the log's tail holding chunk writes and Raft entries together; a lost chunk index that must be rebuilt from the log. The disk log's crash and corruption cases are ported from RocksDB's log tests (which keep LevelDB's) and etcd's write-ahead log tests, each naming its source and commit.

Invariants checked after every step:

- every acknowledged write is readable, and no read returns a version that is later lost;
- every read returns exactly the bytes of the acknowledged writes and punches applied in version order, checked against a byte-range oracle, live and at every snapshot, and no version peering keeps follows a dropped version of its chunk;
- no read returns a version before it commits, and no two members hold different data under one version;
- no PG serves reads from two primaries at once, no two replicas of a shard serve lease reads at overlapping times, and no write tagged with an interval completes after a later interval of its PG has activated; no step check trips between two nodes neither paused nor rebooted, and each such pair holds a lease within `rate_gap` plus two round trips of first contact;
- no write is refused for its interval while its PG's interval has not changed, no acknowledged version is ever dropped, and no version an activated interval kept is dropped later;
- no disk ID names two log IDs, and no log ID is up under two disk IDs;
- a truncated or punched range reads as zeros, no byte past a file's size is non-zero on disk once its purge has run, and once a purge settles, no chunk of a deleted inode or volume remains outside what snapshots hold;
- every chunk reaches its pool's redundancy (copies, or all `data + parity` shards) within the pool's failure-domain rule once recovery settles;
- an encoded version becomes readable, and copies are dropped, only after all its shards are durable;
- a chunk with fewer surviving copies or shards than it needs returns an error, never wrong data;
- at most one lock holder per lock; at most one writer per RWO volume;
- no file's `mtime` goes backwards except by an explicit `utimensat`, whatever the writers' wall clocks show, neither as stored nor as one node reports it, across entering and leaving WriteShared;
- no client reads stale data from a file in WriteShared mode, no session is fenced while its client's own lease still runs, no fenced session's write is applied, and no lock is granted to a new holder while a primary would still accept the fenced holder's writes;
- a replayed mutating request returns its first final result and changes nothing, and an internal transaction repeated after an election changes nothing it did not read;
- an orphaned inode is never freed while a live session has it open;
- a snapshot holds every write acknowledged before its cut and none issued after, in both data and metadata;
- a frozen version is never dropped, and the cleaner never frees a block, while the live version, a snapshot or an unflattened clone needs it;
- every cleaner pass frees more segments than it fills, and no segment is reused while it holds a live block, or a piece, run or record that the newest manifest named by a durable segment header needs (a record after its replay points); after every recovery the next open sequence number is at least the loaded manifest's `NextSeq`, and the usage table equals the log's own;
- a cleaner move is applied only where a map still points at the location it copied from, live, in replay and in a rebuild, so a write that lands during cleaning is never replaced by the older copy;
- a disk that is full except for its reserve can still finish a checkpoint, a merge step and a cleaner pass, and the bytes held beyond the manifests never exceed the reserve;
- no ordinary write fails with `NoSpace` while the cleaner has a victim;
- a flush under a space grant is never refused for space, and on every disk of a pool (1 + k) times the grants outstanding, plus the flushes not yet in fill reports, never exceed the gap between the larger of its fill and `write_limit` and its log reserve;
- a disk passes `write_limit` only through granted flushes, overwrites of blocks it holds live and unshared, punches and recovery;
- a PG whose copying has made no progress for `stuck_after` makes its pool `Stuck`, and a node reboot changes no capacity figure;
- every failed write the daemon acknowledged reaches each handle open on its file at the time, once, at its next `write()`, `fsync` or `close`;
- no block write that needs new space completes with an error while the pool is full, unless an operator failed it;
- a chunk index rebuilt from the log equals the one recovered from its manifest, a chunk's live map never holds two entries for one block, and a shard recovered from its manifest and log equals its state before the crash;
- at most one Raft leader per shard per term, and a committed entry is never lost;
- no node's table epoch or PG interval number goes backwards, and no two writes of a chunk get one version.

**Crash oracle.** As RocksDB's crash test does with its expected state and trace, the simulator keeps, per disk, the state every index had at the last acknowledged LSN and the records written since, in LSN order. After a crash, recovery reports an LSN between the acknowledged one and the last in flight; the oracle replays its trace to that LSN, and every index on the disk, the chunk index and each shard replica, must equal the result, with no hole. The real-disk crash test of phase 5 keeps the same oracle in its driver, outside the daemon it kills.

**Driver tests.** The event loop and the drivers cannot be sans-IO, but most of what they decide is made from what a pass reads, so they are tested in three tiers ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)):

| Tier | Runs | Covers |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stepped | Every commit, seeded; the io_uring cases on a host or in a container that allows io_uring | `internal/loop/looptest` puts a stepped kernel behind the [kernel seam](#runtime-in-go): `Wait` polls the loop's real epoll set without blocking, `Clocks` returns readings from the simulated network's per-node clock model, and scripted jumps (a suspend, a slew, a VM pause, a marker change, the advance to the next deadline) land inside `Wait`, as if the loop had slept through them.
