The simulator's scheduler steps many loops and their worker jobs from one goroutine in a seeded order over real AF_UNIX socketpairs, which deliver inside the writer's own call (`net/unix/af_unix.c`). Kernel I/O still under way (io_uring submissions, unsent TCP bytes, worker jobs not yet run) counts as in flight: the scheduler waits for it in a real `epoll_wait` with a real-time limit and does not advance scripted time, so no `PeerLost` deadline fires while a segment crosses loopback. A seed gives the same results and the same event order per loop; traces name connections by IDs the loop assigns, not by descriptor numbers, so once the loop and driver packages pass their rule set two runs' traces are `cmp`-equal. Over io_uring and TCP it gives the same results and counts, but pass boundaries can differ |
| Real kernel | Every commit in CI, natively or in a container whose seccomp profile allows `io_uring_*` (Docker's default refuses `io_uring_setup`) | Syscall counts per pass, allocations and goroutines under 1,000 connections; a loop that really blocks and wakes; no false `Resumed` from stalls under CPU throttling; the timerfd's clock and absolute deadline from its trace records; the io_uring ring on a file-backed disk; TCP errors made on loopback; cgroup sizing under `docker run --cpus=2.5`. These tests assert counts, orderings and lower bounds, never exact timings, as TigerBeetle's IO tests do |
| VM, nightly | The [VM pause](#appendix-vm-pause-spike) harness on QEMU and Firecracker | The VMClock rows with the real marker, a wall-clock step, device-mapper targets |

The stepper differs from production in three ways, each covered elsewhere. It runs one goroutine, so the inbox gets a stress test under `-race` with real producers and a consumer loop that really blocks. Its clocks are scripted, so the clock slew and VM pause spikes measured what real clocks do and the VM tier keeps checking it. It never sleeps, so a stepped loop that calls `Wait` to block while `Pending` reports work fails the test at once, and the real-kernel tier covers wake-ups that really sleep. The drivers' pure pieces, building io_uring submission entries, decoding completions and the frame codec, get table tests and fuzzing rather than a fake.

`testing/synctest` is only for goroutines off the data path that wait on channels, `time.Sleep`, Go timers or `context` deadlines and make only syscalls that return at once: worker retries, the CSI server's timeouts, the daemon's restart deadlines. It fakes only the `time` package, and a goroutine inside a system call such as `epoll_wait` keeps its bubble's clock from moving, so it can test neither a loop nor a goroutine that blocks in the kernel.

**2. Protocol models.** A TLA+ model describes a protocol as a state machine: the variables each node holds, and every step any node, message or fault can take. TLC walks every reachable state of a small instance and prints a step-by-step trace of any state that breaks a property. It checks the design; the simulator checks the code. The bugs models find lie in fault and recovery paths, at depths a simulator rarely reaches: Amazon's models found a 35-step data loss in DynamoDB's replication and membership protocol that had passed design reviews, code reviews and fault injection on a simulated network ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)). So each protocol is modelled before its code: the four of phase 2 first, and each later one in the phase that builds it ([roadmap](#roadmap-and-open-questions)). Every model is checked at three depths:

- **`ci`**: exhaustive, for the safety properties and for liveness with a time bound, written as an invariant over the model's clocks, in under 5 minutes with 4 TLC workers. CI runs it whenever `models/` or the TLC version changes, together with the negative variants: each removes one rule and must stop at a counterexample. At the 30,000 to 430,000 distinct states a second measured on 4 workers, 5 minutes covers 10 to 130 million states, enough for CockroachDB's two published models and MongoDB's two-shard transaction model, though not for a spec the size of etcd-raft's, which did not finish in 15 minutes even cut down to 3 fixed servers. When a `ci` configuration outgrows the budget, its largest constant moves to `deep`. A spec too large for one `ci` configuration gets several, each heavy on one part, rather than being split into specs: the epoch change model's property that no PG serves reads from two primaries needs peering and leases together, so it has `ci-leases` and `ci-peering`.
- **`deep`**: one more node, chunk, crash or term, plus the unbounded liveness properties, checked exhaustively once per change to its spec, configuration or TLC version, and capped at 4 hours. Liveness is the expensive part: CockroachDB's parallel-commit model checks its safety in 19 s and its temporal properties in 13 minutes.
- **Nightly**: TLC's random simulation (`-simulate`, depth 100 or more) with a fresh seed, about an hour per model, on machines not running the performance runs. An unchanged spec gives the same exhaustive answer every night; only simulation finds anything new.

The [model-to-simulator map](build-checklist.md#phase-2-protocol-models) records each configuration's constants, depth, distinct states and wall time, and names the simulator invariant that checks each property in the real code. TLC is pinned to release 1.7.4 ([tech debts](tech-debts.md)).

**3. Consistency checking.** Every client operation is recorded as `(invoke_time, complete_time, op, result)`, from simulation and real clusters alike. For write-shared files and for chunks the checker searches for a linearizable order with Porcupine; for other files it checks close-to-open: an open that starts after another node's close sees that close's data. In simulation the times are the scheduler's own, not a node's `ctx.Now`: nodes' clocks drift and slew apart, and a linearizability check needs one timeline. An operation and its result are fixed arrays of words, so recording allocates nothing within the capacity the history is made with, and the history converts to Porcupine's operations and back without loss. An operation that never completes, because its node crashed or its client gave up, stays in the history with no completion and goes to Porcupine with an infinite return time, as Jepsen records an indeterminate operation: it may have taken effect at any point after its call, or never. Dropping it instead would flag every later read that saw its write. A client's pipelined operations overlap under one client ID, since Porcupine uses the ID only to draw the history. When no order is legal, the check names an operation the longest legal prefixes leave out, the same one every run although Porcupine gathers those prefixes in a map: the first to return of the operations in none of them, or, when every operation fits one, of those left out by each key's longest prefix with the smallest operation indices.

**4. Real cluster.** Run Jepsen-style nemesis schedules nightly: random kills, partitions, disk errors and slow disks during a multi-node file workload, checked at the end.

- **FUSE behavior** is pinned by the spike harnesses in [spikes/fuse-coherence](../spikes/fuse-coherence/) and [spikes/fuse-restart](../spikes/fuse-restart/), run in CI against each supported kernel.
- **Daemon restarts:** `kill -9` and rolling upgrades of the daemon under multi-client `fsx` and a metadata workload. No request hangs, no mutating request runs twice, and lost buffered writes surface as `EIO`. The same for the node plugin: killed and upgraded alone, no mount sees it; `rock-solid upgrade` from the previous release keeps every mount and device and never restarts a node's daemon and plugin together.
- **Held block writes:** a pod on an RWO block volume keeps writing into a full pool for 6 hours, and another node's daemon is stopped for 30 minutes with its ublk devices waiting for recovery. Both nodes stay Ready with kubelet's pod lifecycle checks healthy, other pods on them start and stop, `NodeGetVolumeStats` answers, and once room returns or the daemon restarts the held pods carry on and their filesystems check clean.
- **Power cuts** via the Intelligent Platform Management Interface (IPMI) or a smart power strip are the only honest fsync test. Write checksummed patterns with `fio --verify=crc32c`, cut power, boot, verify. Some consumer NVMe drives acknowledge flushes they haven't done.
- **Soft-RoCE (`rdma_rxe`)** runs the RDMA code path on any ordinary network card, and `tc netem` can inject faults under it. It is a functional stand-in only: it is slower than kernel TCP at every message size, and it treats `retry_cnt=7` as "retry forever", unlike real NICs. RDMA performance decisions need a real card.
- device-mapper can't see SPDK I/O, so the SPDK path uses SPDK's own error and delay bdev modules.

**5. Test matrix.**

```
transport:  TCP-only | RDMA-only | mixed
disks/node: 1 | 4 | mixed sizes | mixed speed (NVMe + SATA SSD)
cluster:    3 nodes | 5 nodes | add node mid-run | remove disk mid-run
clients:    storage nodes only | plus compute-only nodes
backend:    io_uring | SPDK
pool:       none | rep2 | rep3 | ec2-1 | ec4-2 at 2 per node
```

Property tests on placement: after any random add, remove and failure sequence, every placement group keeps its pool's width with no more than `max_per_domain` members per failure domain, targets follow each disk's capacity, so after a disk is added to a pool holding data the second computation proposes no move, the balance loop converges with every disk within its fill margin, available + held then lies between balanced ÷ (1 + the largest margin) and balanced, and chunks moved stay near the minimum. Property tests on the capacity figures: available + held ≤ balanced ≤ Σ capacity ÷ b, the domains' stranded rooms sum to Σ capacity − b × balanced, the stranded fraction does not change as a pool fills at balanced proportions, and for copies, balanced without the largest-capacity domain is the least over single domains and at most balanced after any one disk loss.

**7. Performance.** Track metadata operations per second (create, stat, rename), cached and write-shared read latency, 4K block latency at queue depth 1, CPU per operation, 99.9th-percentile latency during recovery, and cleaning write amplification with disks held at 80% and 90% full. Latency runs include idle gaps, because a drive that has idled for 100 ms adds about 1 ms to the next durable write (NVMe flush spike, N6). A 20% overnight regression is treated as a bug.

**Starting hardware.** One big machine is enough at first: a few virtual machines, QEMU's emulated NVMe devices (SPDK runs against them for correctness, not speed; this needs root, `vfio-pci` and hugepages inside the virtual machine). SPDK's file-backed aio bdev needs none of that and runs in CI as a normal user, and Soft-RoCE between the virtual machines.

### Testing the design decisions

Every decision above is held by a test, and the test is written with the decision, before or with the code that implements it. Which kind of test depends on what the decision is about:

| Decision is about | Held by | Examples |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A byte layout (superblock, headers, keys, chunk IDs, the PG hash) | Golden fixtures under `testdata/` that the code must reproduce byte for byte, round trips, an exhaustive single-bit-flip test, a torn-write property test over every subset of landed blocks, and a native fuzz test on every decoder | `internal/core/testdata/chunk_id_golden.txt`, `internal/disklog/testdata/log_format_golden.txt` |
| What a state machine emits (one flush per round, write and flush emitted together, `commit_depth` rounds in flight, peering before serving, a freeze entry with the first write after a snapshot) | Trace checks: a test feeds events, records the actions, and asserts on the trace, in the unit test and in the simulator over thousands of seeds; cases whose input is data (compaction picking, fragments, recovery from a scripted disk, a reduced failing seed) are data-driven files (`github.com/cockroachdb/datadriven`, as Pebble and etcd-raft use) that `go test -rewrite` regenerates for review | "exactly one `DiskFsync` per round"; "no `NetSend` of a write before the peering reply" |
| What must hold across a whole run (acknowledged writes readable, one primary per PG, versions never reused, a live map with one entry per block) | Simulator invariants checked after every step, plus a TLA+ model for each protocol | the invariant list above |
| An internal choice that must not change what an index reads (piece size, flush and compaction timing, filters, failed checkpoints, restarts, range against point removals) | A metamorphic test: one ops file per seed run under option sets at their extremes, histories compared with each other and with a model, failures reduced into data-driven regression files | `internal/lsm`'s metamorphic test; `testdata/regressions` |
| What the runtime does (one loop per core, no goroutine per connection, one `writev` per descriptor per pass, bounded passes, no allocation per operation, loops woken only through epoll, clocks read only through the kernel seam) | Static checks where the rule is about code: the sans-IO analyzer and its rule set for the loop and driver packages. Stepped runs: a seed gives the same results and per-loop event order twice. Trace checks where it is about behaviour: the event loop records every syscall it makes as a trace event, through a recording layer under its `unix` calls, and a test asserts on the counts per pass (`writev` ≤ 1 per descriptor, bytes read ≤ the budget, `epoll_wait` once). Allocation checks: `testing.AllocsPerRun` and `b.ReportAllocs` must report 0. Goroutine checks: `runtime.NumGoroutine` is the same with 1 and with 1,000 connections, measured in the loop's test | `tools/sansio`; the event loop item of the [build checklist](build-checklist.md) |
| A number the design relies on (CRC32C per block, collection cost of the page tree, cost per group) | A benchmark with the spike's figure as its bar, run nightly, and a 20% regression is a bug | `BenchmarkCRC32CBlock` against 167 ns on x86-64 and 359 ns on arm64 |

**Traces, not logs.** On the data path the daemon writes no free-form log lines; the sans-IO analyzer rejects `log`, `log/slog` and `fmt.Print*` there. Every event a loop handles, every action it executes and every syscall it makes is a fixed-field trace record, the same record the simulator keeps for its action trace, so one checker library reads both: a seed's trace in a unit test, and a real daemon's trace from `--trace`, which writes the records to a ring in memory and dumps them as text on request. The format is one record per line, `tick loop kind fields...`, with fields in a fixed order per kind, so two runs of a seed are compared with `cmp` and a check is a scan over lines. A fourth kind, `output`, records what a machine hands its owner (the log's `Durable`, `Failed`, `CheckpointDone`), which no driver delivers and so is not an event. In the simulator `tick` is the scheduler's global time, so the nodes' records merge into one file in order whatever each node's clock reads, and `loop` is written `node.loop`; a daemon writes its loop number alone. Each loop's ring holds `trace_records` records (default 65,536, 64 bytes each), and the dump runs on the loop that owns the ring, as an inbox request, so it never sees a record half-written. The daemon's operator logs (startup, configuration, a disk marked down) go through `log/slog` off the data path.

## Alternatives considered

Each choice below favors a short hot path, a single binary and testable correctness over peak features. The section that owns each decision explains it in full; this table is the index of what was weighed.

| Decision | Chosen | Alternatives weighed | Why the chosen one |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| First product | RWX filesystem | Block first; S3 first | RWX is where homelab users have no good option; block alone competes with Longhorn v2 and Mayastor |
| Language | Go | Rust; C++ (Ceph, 3FS) | Easier to read, review and extend, and the language of etcd-raft, the CSI tooling and Porcupine. Against Rust it costs 1.07× the CPU on the replicated write path with the [runtime rules](#runtime-in-go) ([Go and Rust codegen spike](#appendix-go-and-rust-codegen-spike)), 1.5 to 1.7× with a goroutine per connection ([tuned Go spike](#appendix-tuned-go-spike)), and 1.1 to 1.25× on compute-bound code ([Go CPU spike](#appendix-go-cpu-per-operation-spike)). Collector pauses stay under 1 ms, but a memtable with one pointer per entry spends 23 to 35% of the CPU in collection, so bulk structures are pointer-free pages ([Go memtable spike](#appendix-go-memtable-spike)). Rust avoids the collector and reaches SPDK and RDMA through bindgen; Go reaches them through cgo in two drivers |
| File client | FUSE | Own kernel module (Weka, CephFS); NFS gateway pod (Longhorn RWX) | The spike shows FUSE can do per-file cache switching. FUSE is safe to iterate on, and a crash doesn't take down the kernel. A gateway funnels all traffic through one pod |
| Where write-back lives | In the daemon | FUSE `writeback_cache` (kernel page cache) | The kernel option is mount-wide, hides writes from the daemon and can't be revoked per file, so write-shared mode couldn't be enforced (spike T9) |
| Full pool under write-back | Space grants per node and pool from the control-plane leader on the session heartbeat, at most half of available, divided by water-filling with buffered data as a floor and no reuse above the water level; each reused for at most k = 2 grants' worth of flushes between answered heartbeats, with an early heartbeat at half the budget, so Σ (1 + k) × grants + F fits every disk's gap above `write_limit`; write through without one; a later failure reported once per handle at its next `write()`, `fsync` or `close` ([Client design](#client-design)) | Late reports only (Linux NFS client, CephFS, JuiceFS); writing through only once near full (CephFS `nearfull`, ext4 leaving delayed allocation); grants per disk; a near-full flag on primary replies; reuse for the whole session lease; reuse for one heartbeat interval with a peak write rate per disk; Lustre's one eighth of what is left, first come; shares proportional to asks | Local filesystems return `ENOSPC` from `write()`, and applications that ignore `close()` would lose acknowledged data silently. A near-full switch or flag is learned after bytes were acknowledged. Per-disk grants are exact but need requests to every storage node; `write_limit` is soft, so a pool grant capped by the room above it is enough ([capacity and ENOSPC study](../studies/capacity-and-enospc/README.md)). Reuse for a whole lease let a node cut off from the leader but not from storage land 11 GB per disk against a 9.9 GB gap; a time window depends on clocks and leaves a 32 GB NVMe disk 0.17 GB of grants against 1.06 GB under the budget. First come keeps space with whoever asked first, and proportional shares let one streaming node crowd out small writers ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)) |
| Coherence | Tiers 1 → 2 → 3 | Close-to-open only (NFS, JuiceFS); full capabilities from the start (CephFS); caching left to the user (DAOS) | Ships useful RWX early; the Weka-style mode switch gets most of the benefit of capabilities with three states and one message type |
| Open-file state | Soft state at the inode owner, handed over on planned leader transfers and rebuilt after a crash | Durable open records in Raft | Keeps `open` to one round trip with no fsync, which matters for small-file workloads |
| Session expiry | Sessions owned by the control plane: renewed on each node's heartbeat to its leader, expired on the leader's clock, fenced in one versioned session table that every storage node acknowledges ([Sessions and fencing](#sessions-and-fencing)) | Renewal with every inode owner, each expiring sessions on its own (CephFS with several metadata servers, Lustre's servers); a session key in one MetaStore shard; node liveness as the session; CephFS's epoch barrier on the next holder's writes instead of acknowledgments | One verdict per session and a renewal cost per node, not per owner or shard. Per-owner expiry fences everywhere a client cut off from one owner; a session shard adds a hop and a second group whose failover pauses renewal; node liveness would turn 5 s stalls into application errors; the barrier saves an acknowledgment round that costs milliseconds at 10 nodes ([session expiry study](../studies/session-expiry/README.md)) |
| Kernel bypass | Opt-in flag | Mandatory DPDK and SPDK (Weka, Mayastor, Longhorn v2) | Homelab links are network-bound, so bypass buys latency, not throughput. Mandatory hugepages and pinned cores break "plug and play" |
| Core abstraction | Two narrow primitives: ChunkStore + MetaStore (Tectonic, Colossus, 3FS) | One rich object layer (Ceph RADOS) | Each frontend pays only for the metadata it needs |
| Metadata store | Embedded multi-Raft | External FoundationDB (3FS), TiKV, Redis (JuiceFS), etcd | The single-binary goal rules out a second system to operate. An external etcd remains an option for early prototypes |
| MetaStore `Txn` | Six ops (`Put`, `Delete`, `DeleteRange`, `Check`, `CheckEmpty`, `Update` with `Set`, `Add` and `Bump`) evaluated at apply time, checks first and writes in list order; a replay record stores only final results; internal transactions check everything they read; followers redirect ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Compare-and-set, put and delete only; etcd's compare, then success or failure ops; duplicate writes to one key refused (etcd); proposer-evaluated writes (CockroachDB); forwarding to the leader (etcd-raft's default) | With compare-and-set only, concurrent creators in one directory commit one per round, 333 to 1,667 creates per second, on its `mtime`; `Update` never conflicts, as FoundationDB's atomic ops. etcd's failure branch serves reads on failure, which `Result.Actual` covers. Proposer evaluation would make a write wait for the one before it on its key. Forwarding hides the result from the proposer and bypasses the owner's open registry ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)) |
| Consensus shape | Leader per shard, one shard at first | Single global Raft group; leaderless Egalitarian Paxos or Accord; quorum or CRDT with no consensus (Garage) | CAS is required for locks and rename, which rules out quorums and CRDTs. Per-shard leaders already remove the single-leader bottleneck |
| Raft implementation | etcd-raft (`go.etcd.io/raft/v3`) used directly, `Ready`-style, in a fork that seeds election timeouts | A Raft written from scratch; hashicorp/raft; etcd-raft's `Node` with its goroutine | Only the `Ready` shape fits `OnEvent` and lets the driver share flushes, batch per connection and quiesce shards; hashicorp/raft and `Node` run goroutines and own their storage. Using upstream keeps its tests and years of fixes; a Raft from scratch would start without them |
| etcd-raft version | v3.6.0, the base of the pinned fork | v3.7.0 (latest, protobuf-go pointer messages); v3.7.0 with recycled decoded messages | v3.7.0 makes 6× the allocations per message (16.2 against 2.7), 1.64× the CPU per proposal and 22% against 14% collection CPU, mostly inside etcd-raft where recycling cannot reach, and its `[]*pb.Entry` storage API defeats off-heap entries; v3.7.0's ReadIndex fix is cherry-picked into the fork ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)) |
| Bounding a round | Size only, `max_group_commit_bytes` 1 MiB; one loop per core; collect only changed shards | A time or CPU bound per round; a smaller size cap (256 KiB); one loop per node | At a fixed number in flight latency is in-flight ÷ throughput, and under open load rounds never reach 1 MiB; a 1 ms CPU bound changed nothing, 256 KiB cost a third of throughput, while per-core loops took p50/p99 from 11/51 ms to 3.1/5.0 ms at 60,000/s ([raft rounds spike](#appendix-raft-rounds-spike)). RocksDB caps a write group at the same 1 MiB (`max_write_batch_group_size_bytes`) |
| Cross-shard transactions | Transaction record plus intents (CockroachDB style) | Percolator with a central timestamp oracle (TiKV); a central sequencer (FoundationDB) | No extra central service; the commit point rides on one shard's Raft |
| Redundancy | Per pool, chosen by the user: 1 to 3 copies, optionally erasure coding; defaults favor capacity without lowering failure tolerance | One scheme for the whole cluster; a replica count per volume (Longhorn) | Placement groups spread every volume over every disk, so a redundancy scheme has to belong to a whole set of PGs, not one volume. Pools also let one cluster mix scratch, block and media data |
| Erasure coding of mutable data | Never in place: encode whole new chunks and idle chunks, overwrite only copies (Windows Azure Storage) | In-place overwrites of stripes (Ceph `allow_ec_overwrites`, Vitastor); erasure-code everything with redirect-on-write (Weka) | Avoids read-modify-write and the write hole without cluster-wide stripe garbage collection. Closing the write hole in place is hard: Vitastor's chaos test failed on every seed of its two-phase erasure-coded configurations before its [3.2.0 fixes](https://vitastor.io/en/blog/2026-08-31-v3.2.0.html) |
| Snapshot cut | Pause every writer of the volume for one round trip, then resume under a new snapshot epoch | Multi-version MetaStore and chunks with hybrid logical clock timestamps (CockroachDB); tagging writes with the new snapshot epoch without a pause | Reuses the recall fan-out and Raft's point-in-time view, with no timestamps in the key encoding. Tagging alone can capture an effect without its cause |
| Restore and clone | New volume with a parent pointer, flattened in the background when `flatten` is on (Ceph RBD) | Copy all data at restore | Restore is instant and costs no space; Velero's temporary PVCs never pay for a copy |
| Replication protocol | Primary fans out to replicas in parallel (Ceph, Vitastor) | Chain replication or CRAQ (3FS); leaderless quorum writes | One added hop instead of one per chain link; the primary also gives each chunk a single write order |
| Placement | Chunks hashed into placement groups; table of PG → members and interval number, versioned by a table epoch and computed by the control plane | Per-chunk table; placement every node computes (Ceph CRUSH, Weka's algorithmic ownership) | Exact balance on small clusters with mismatched disks, and explicit, throttled moves; Ceph adds `pg-upmap` tables to CRUSH for the same reason. Placement groups keep the table to a few hundred KiB, and table epochs and intervals are easy to simulate |
| Pool capacity | Available from the placement table (the smallest room ÷ share); balanced, stranded and reprotectable figures over each domain's capacity, room plus the pool's own data; placement targets each disk's capacity, capped by the failure-domain rule, with a fill margin of max(5% of target, 1 member) and moves from the disk furthest above its target ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity), [placement table](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Free raw space ÷ overhead (MinIO); Ceph's `MAX AVAIL` from CRUSH weights; Vitastor's linear program for placement; refusing layouts that strand space; figures and targets over free room; a 5% margin with no floor, or a floor of 2 members (CockroachDB's) | Room ÷ overhead overstated by 1.36 to 3.3× on unequal failure domains, and Ceph's rule computes the same figure there, while available matched the first refused write within 0.3%; size-proportional targets never converged on such layouts, capped ones reached the bound within 1% without a solver ([capacity model spike](#appendix-capacity-model-spike)). Over free room the figures drift as the pool fills and each finished move shifts the targets, costing 1.5 to 2 times the moves; 5% is under one member on a small disk, so the loop could not converge ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)). Stranded space is usable by another pool on the same disks |
| Pool conditions | `Full` and `NearFull` on the admission predicate, grants counted, `≥` in blocks, clearing 1 point down; `Stranded` over 10% and 5 points above the confirmed layout; `Unbalanced` when placement has stopped and available + held < 0.9 × balanced; `CannotReprotect` for held data above what the survivors of the worst likely loss can re-protect, never for `none`; `Stuck` after 60 s without copying ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)) | Ceph's strict per-disk states without hysteresis; `Stranded` whenever over 10%, or never a condition; `Unbalanced` after a timer (Ceph's 60 s, Vitastor's 600 s) or at 5%; `CannotReprotect` on the largest domain by size, on one-copy pools, or on losses that leave too few domains | Strict states leave windows where writes are refused but the pool is not `Full`, and without a gap each discard on a full block pool re-raises it. Stranding on mismatched homelabs is permanent, 25 to 57% on three nodes, so it is confirmed at creation and the condition reports only what changes later. A move lasts as long as its data, and the leader knows when placement stops; 5% is within 0.2 points of what the spike's loop left after every addition. By size picks the wrong domain, and losses below the width would fire from the first byte on the default `rep3` with three nodes ([capacity conditions study](../studies/capacity-conditions/README.md)) |
| On-disk format | One log per disk for chunk data, Raft logs and LSM indexes, with a cleaner | Journal plus in-place data with a reference-counted extent map (Vitastor, BlueStore); an embedded key-value engine for indexes, shards and Raft logs (a copy-on-write B-tree such as `redb` or bbolt, an LSM tree such as `fjall` or Pebble); chunk data inside that engine | One write and one flush per durable write, shared by every shard on the disk; the disk driver touches every byte, so SPDK and the simulator cover metadata too; snapshot block sharing, checksums and torn-write detection come free; sequential writes and TRIM suit consumer SSDs. Used as a Raft log, `redb` wrote 2 to 112× the payload and `fjall` stalled for about 100 ms and kept 179 MB on disk for 4.9 MB live ([KV engines spike](#appendix-kv-engines-spike)). Costs a cleaner and an LSM tree |
| Log streams | Three: hot (new copies and their Raft entries), cold (cleaner copies and shards), metadata (sorted runs, compactions, manifests) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Two streams; more by temperature (F2FS move counts, SepBIT age classes); Raft entries in a stream of their own | Runs mixed with cleaner copies raised block-volume write amplification by 25 to 54% at 80 to 90% full, against 3 to 15% in their own stream. Age classes cut hot/cold write amplification by up to 30% but raised uniform write amplification by up to 63%. A Raft stream saved 6% but would split each commit into two rounds and two flushes ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)). WiscKey, RocksDB's BlobDB, Titan and Pebble each write their GC output to one place; their temperatures choose a cheaper device, which pools do ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)) |
| Whole-chunk layout | Chunk slots: 16 data-only slots per 64 MiB segment, each round's record in the hot stream ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Whole chunks in 64 MiB log segments; smaller segments for the whole disk (4 or 16 MiB); slots carrying their own record headers; a stream of their own | Slots cost no cleaning (1.004 at any fill, against 4.9 at 90% full in the log), and a stream of their own changes nothing (6.2 against 6.1), because whole chunks die as randomly as small writes. 4 MiB segments still cost 1.5 at 90% and multiply the usage table's snapshots by 16. Slots with their own headers fit 15 per segment and cap a pool near 93% full ([cleaner streams spike](#appendix-cleaner-streams-spike)) |
| Chunk index | A non-overlapping live map per chunk, trimmed and split on insert, plus a frozen copy of the map per snapshotted version (BlueStore, Btrfs) | One entry per version, resolved at read time (RocksDB-style merge) | A read of the live version costs one lookup per block whatever the chunk's history. The price is a map copy when a snapshot freezes a written chunk, and that a primary maps a write only once it commits |
| Sub-block writes | Merged at the chunk primary against the newest version it has ordered, pending ones included; whole merged blocks sent to the replicas; the daemon sends whole blocks where it holds the bytes ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | Client read-modify-write with compare-and-set on the version (VitastorFS); a byte-range delta merged on every member (Ceph BlueStore); merging against the committed version only | In the [sub-block writes spike](#appendix-sub-block-writes-spike)'s model, merging on committed versions or on the client without compare-and-set lost bytes on every seed, and with it the client retried 0.64 to 1.28 times per write, since the version is per chunk. Deltas cost a base read on every member and leave members agreeing only if their histories do. The primary's merge costs one base read where nothing caches the block, and needs peering to drop only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions |
| Small files and fsync'd appends | A whole 4 KiB block per copy; a fsync'd append rewrites its tail block ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Inline data in the inode (CephFS, deprecated since Octopus; ext4, Btrfs, F2FS, Lustre's data on MDT); byte-granular patch entries merged on read and folded later (JuiceFS slices, the ZFS intent log); short last blocks; smaller blocks | The MetaStore's runs live in memory, so 1.5 KiB inline costs 12.5 times the per-file budget, 43 → 3.4 million files per 16 GiB, and CephFS's kernel client uninlines racily on every open for write. Patches gain nothing at queue depth 1 unless their bytes ride in header blocks (83× → 42× for a 100-byte append), which would make headers live, and cost one entry per block, read merging and folding. Short blocks save 2.6× on a 1.5 KiB file for byte-counted liveness. Smaller blocks multiply checksums and index entries. Patches and short blocks are [open questions](#roadmap-and-open-questions) ([sub-block writes study](../studies/sub-block-writes/README.md)) |
| Stale requests | A member accepts a request only in its PG's current interval, which the table carries per PG; a primary takes a client's write while its own term lasts; tables committed and pushed as deltas with the resulting table's CRC32C ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | One table epoch for the whole cluster, every older one rejected; comparing the PG's members in the two tables; each node working out intervals by walking every table (Ceph); recording each primary's activation in the table before it activates (Ceph `up_thru`) | A table change disturbs only the PGs it changes: 4.4 to 46 times fewer disturbed writes in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)'s homelab year on 10 nodes, and counter bumps, restarts and staged moves stay local to their PGs; on 3 nodes node events still change every PG. Member lists can change and change back; walking every table would make every delta mandatory; `up_thru` costs a table change on every peering, where skipping intervals below `min_live` and waiting for the rest suffices ([PG epochs study](../studies/pg-epochs/README.md)) |
| Hung disk detection | Each disk's oldest unfinished read, write or flush, aged on its owner loop, carried on every heartbeat and answered from memory to a direct probe after a primary's `member_timeout` report; a disk stalled past `member_timeout` is down ([Hung disks](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | A probe that only checks the node or loop answers; a probe that writes and flushes a block; the kernel's NVMe `io_timeout` (30 s, then a controller reset); fail-slow alone | A ping passes on a hung drive, and fail-slow takes a whole `slow_disk_window`. A probe write queues behind the hung I/O and needs the same timeout anyway, while adding I/O to every probe; the age is already in the loop's state, and the heartbeat covers disks no primary waits on |
| Refill after a member goes down | A down member stays in its PG and is refilled after `refill_after` (10 minutes), after `degraded_refill_after` (1 minute) when its PG is below `min_live`, at once when its disk's log is gone, and never while peering waits for it anyway; a refill member counts toward `min_live` while it recovers; a node or disk marked down `flap_count` (5) times in `flap_window` (10 minutes) stays down until it has been normal for a whole window, unless a PG it belongs to would stay below `min_live` with nowhere to refill ([Failure detection](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | Refilling at once; refilling a PG below `min_live` at once; Ceph's wait with no exception for a PG below `min_size`, which then accepts no writes for the whole wait; counting a refill member only once recovered; Ceph's damping, which stops the OSD process; damping with no `min_live` guard; no damping | Outside PGs left below `min_live`, a reboot or a network hiccup costs only the writes it missed, where refilling at once copied every member of the node away and back, 1,202 tables per flap in the [PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike); RADOS separates down from out for the same reason (Weil et al., §2.3). A PG below `min_live` is already stopped, so it waits only `degraded_refill_after`, which a 5 s hiccup or a 1 s disk stall never reaches, so `rep2` copies away and back only for outages a minute long. Counting the refill member at once lets writes resume when its interval activates, each on `min_live` copies, rather than after the copy. Damping without the guard would stop a two-node `rep2` pool's writes for a whole window after the node is healthy. Keeping the process up lets a damped node keep serving its clients and rejoin by itself |
| Default `write_limit` | 90% for every pool, with drive wear projected per disk and 80% advised for random-overwrite block pools on consumer drives ([Volume size and capacity](#volume-size-and-capacity)) | 80% for block pools; a default per drive class | Pool defaults pick the option that uses less space; the wear depends on the workload and the fill a disk actually sits at, which a default cannot know, while the projection shows it. 90% is where the cleaner spike's write amplification is still 7.2 rather than 17.3 at 95% |
| Disk identity | A disk ID bound to the log ID drawn at format time and never reused; an unknown log ID joins as a new disk ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | A disk ID kept by the node and device path, so a reformatted or replaced disk returns under it and peering must tell an empty log from one that missed nothing (Ceph's `osd destroy` and reuse of the ID); a disk ID written into the superblock | Peering's proofs come from what members hold and list, so a member must have kept its whole log; a new ID costs a refill the lost data needs anyway, and keeps the rule in the control plane instead of in peering. An ID in the superblock would add nothing the log ID does not already give |
| Drive classes | Solid-state drives, NVMe or SATA; HDDs later, as one log over a flash partition and an HDD, with every stored address in the log's own space so the tier rewrites no index entry ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | HDDs now, each a disk with its own log; HDDs only in pools of their own `disk_class`; a cache tier in front of HDD pools, as Ceph had; automatic hot/cold tiering between pools | On an HDD alone every flush and every turn between open segments costs a seek, so the MetaStore shards on that disk commit at HDD speed. The two-device log is BlueStore's and ZFS's answer, but it changes disk failure and recovery, which can wait for users with that hardware, and log-relative addresses make waiting cost no format migration. Ceph deprecated cache tiering for its complexity and poor performance |
| Version layout | 64 bits: the chunk's PG interval number in the high 32 over a counter per chunk in the low 32, restarting at 1 in each interval; a counter that runs out takes its PG to a new interval, as Vitastor's per-PG epoch over a per-object counter ([Data layer](#data-layer-chunkstore)) | The assigning primary's table epoch over a 40-bit counter (24 + 40); the table epoch at the interval's start; 24 + 40 or 28 + 36 with interval numbers; Ceph's `eversion_t`, the map epoch over a never-reset 64-bit counter per PG, in 12 bytes; 128-bit versions; a plain counter per chunk | Two primaries can never give one version to different data, so peering and recovery compare versions, not contents. Table epochs follow the whole cluster: one node flapping with staged refills would fill 24 bits of them in 19 hours and 32 bits in 207 days, where the busiest PG's intervals take 1.1 years to fill 24 bits and 272 years to fill 32 ([PG epochs spike](#appendix-pg-epochs-spike)). Running out of intervals is fatal, a counter running out costs one PG a re-peer, at most hourly at a million writes a second to one chunk. 128-bit versions would add 8 bytes to every record entry (33 to 41) and extent (26 to 34) |
| LSM tree shape | Memtable, in-memory sorted runs over the whole key range and universal compaction from the first commit | A memtable alone, checkpointed by full snapshots, with runs added later; leveled runs partitioned by key (Pebble, RocksDB's default) | A full snapshot per `max_replay_bytes` of records costs the index's whole size each time, 256× the entries for a 16 GiB shard; a run costs what changed. Leveled kept 1.5 to 3.5 runs but rewrote 2.8 to 17.7× the flushed bytes on a 44 MB index, and replaces a run's pieces one key range at a time, where whole runs die together in the metadata stream ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)) |
| Compaction choice | RocksDB's universal compaction: size amplification 200%, size ratio 1%, then the auto-tuned run limit, merging only runs adjacent in age ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Cassandra's size-tiered buckets over runs adjacent in age; the same buckets with universal's fallbacks; universal with a fixed run limit; buckets over runs sorted by size | Buckets over adjacent runs left a run per flush under alternating, bursty and log-uniform flush sizes; universal kept 2.3 to 6.7 runs on average under all of them at 2.3 to 6.3× the flushed bytes and 1.3 to 1.7× the index in space. A fixed limit of four costs up to 100× on a 1.6 GB index. Sorting by size needs a sequence number per entry, 8 B, and a read path that compares versions across every run, as Cassandra does with cell timestamps ([LSM compaction study](#appendix-lsm-compaction-study)) |
| Loops and `GOMAXPROCS` under a CPU limit | Loops = Go's default `GOMAXPROCS` (`max(2, ceil(limit))`) − 1, `GOMAXPROCS` left to the runtime ([Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | Loops = limit with `GOMAXPROCS` = limit + 1; loops = `GOMAXPROCS` = limit | The only shape that both keeps a spare P for the loops' wakeups and never exceeds the quota. Limit + 1 Ps was throttled in up to 6.5% of periods, and as many Ps as loops starved the collector and workers and held loops up to 25 ms ([GOMAXPROCS quota spike](#appendix-gomaxprocs-under-a-cpu-quota-spike)). It costs one core of loop capacity, which a higher limit or no limit buys back |
| Removals in the chunk index | Range removals in the LSM tree; a write removes the keys of the extents it covers with one, when there are two or more | Point removals left to merges; merges triggered by removals (RocksDB's `CompactOnDeletionCollector`, Pebble's tombstone-density compaction) or by a lookup's step count; a floor that keeps each layer's candidate | Point removals cost 200 to 730 µs per lookup of a chunk rewritten over small extents, unmerged after 192 GiB of writes; Pebble shows the same, 1,025 points per floor until a full compaction. A triggered merge leaves the memtable phase at 85 to 230 µs per lookup and rewrites the run holding the old keys. Pebble found the run-wide removal ratio we tried "somewhat ineffective" and counts dense blocks instead. Range removals take two searches from the moment the write applies ([LSM runs spike](#appendix-lsm-runs-spike), [LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)) |
| Kinds of removal | A removal, and a range removal | RocksDB's and Pebble's `SingleDelete`; Pebble's `DeleteSized` | `SingleDelete` drops a deletion where it meets its one write. Our flush and merge already drop a removal once no older run holds its key, without the caller's promise, and replay applies records again, the duplication `SingleDelete` forbids. `DeleteSized` feeds garbage-ranked picking, which universal compaction does not do |
| Memtable range removals | Non-overlapping fragments in a second page tree, a later write cutting its key out | Pebble's and RocksDB's unfragmented list in a second skiplist, fragmented and cached on read | Fragmenting on read exists to keep sequence-number stacks for snapshots; our views pin pages and runs, so the union of a layer's fragments is enough and is kept fragmented as it is written ([LSM deletions study](#appendix-lsm-deletions-study)) |
| Run format | LevelDB's block format with restarts every 16 entries, one block per piece while runs are in memory, the root listing the others; range removals and the filter in blocks of their own | One entry per line with a full key; Pebble's columnar blocks | Prefix compression brings a run to 133 B per file against 199 B in the memtable, and the format is Pebble's, so its block tests port unchanged. Pebble moved to columnar blocks for faster seeks and steps (a binary search over every key with no decoding, cheap reverse steps) and for CockroachDB's key schema, which puts MVCC timestamps in a column of their own. With [run filters](#on-disk-format), a lookup searches about one run, so the search inside a block is no longer the main cost |
| Run filters | A cache-local Bloom filter per run, 10 bits per key, of whole keys or a prefix the index names (the chunk index's `(pool, h, chunk_id)`, the MetaStore's keys and inode prefixes), on every run from the first | No filters and a cap on runs; binary fuse (Pebble) or Ribbon (RocksDB) filters; more bits for newer runs (Monkey, Pebble's progressive policy); no filter on the oldest run (RocksDB's `optimize_filters_for_hits`, Pebble's default reads) | Over 24 runs, filters cut a negative MetaStore lookup from 13 to 1.9 µs and a chunk-index read from 32 to 5 µs for 0.8 to 2.9% more memory, which beats six unfiltered runs. A cap needs leveled merges that rewrite entries several times as often. Binary fuse halves a miss's probe time but needs every hash and about 24 B per key at once, and a run is one object of up to 58 GiB. False positives cost 0.2 searches at 24 runs, so extra bits buy little. The oldest run's filter is in memory and misses are common ([LSM read path spike](#appendix-lsm-read-path-spike)) |
| Block cache | One CLOCK cache per index owner loop over off-heap buffers; data blocks of 4 to 32 KiB in each piece; fence pointers and filters pinned in memory outside the cache | Pebble's Clock-PRO, sharded four ways per CPU; RocksDB's HyperClockCache or LRU cache, index and filter blocks in the cache | One loop owns an index, so a cache needs no shards or locks. Pinned fence pointers and filters keep a lookup to at most one block read per run it searches, as RocksDB's default does ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) |
| Peer connections | One per pair of event loops, opened on demand, with requests routed to the owning loop (Seastar) | One per node pair | Keeps a request and its reply on the two loops that own its ends; one connection per node pair would add two inbox hops per operation or pin a node pair to one core |
| Disk logs and event loops | One log per disk, owned by one loop; other loops hand it their records once per pass through its inbox (TiKV's store writer and raft-engine) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A log per loop on every disk (ScyllaDB's commitlog per shard); every shard pinned to its disk's loop | Keeps one flush per disk per round and the format's single LSN sequence, recovery and cleaner. Costs two inbox wakeups per loop per round: up to 20% more CPU per item with one or two producer loops and none measurable with four or more ([log per core spike](#appendix-log-per-core-spike)). Logs per loop would give each disk 4 to 16 flush streams, past the two a consumer drive overlaps, and change the format. Pinning shards to their disk's loop would put all of a one-disk node's metadata on one core |
| Raft leader leases | Fortification on the per-node-pair leases that back primary read leases, as CockroachDB's leader leases on store liveness ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Node-liveness epochs in the control plane (CockroachDB's epoch leases); etcd-raft's `CheckQuorum` lease with `ReadOnlyLeaseBased`; ReadIndex for every read | One lease mechanism for PG primaries and Raft leaders, renewed per node pair whatever the group count, so quiescent groups keep their leases. No central record whose outage would expire every lease at once, and no synchronized clocks. etcd-raft's lease counts ticks a quiescent group does not take and can overlap a new leader by up to one election timeout. ReadIndex costs a round trip per read |
| Replacing a Raft voter | Placed by the control-plane leader in the shard map; replaced after `refill_after`, at once for a lost log; the new replica joins as a learner, then one `ConfChangeV2` swaps it for the dead voter through a joint configuration ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | Adding the new replica as a voter, then removing the dead one; removing the dead voter first, then adding a learner and promoting it; voters chosen by each group | An empty voter in a group with one voter down puts a member that is still catching up into every quorum, so commits stall until it has a snapshot, as the Raft paper's §6 warns. Removing first takes two changes the leader drives one after the other, where the joint change is one proposal that etcd-raft leaves on its own. Groups cannot see each other's replicas, so only the control plane can spread them over disks |
| Election after a lease lapse | A follower whose fortification ends with its grant campaigns, pre-vote first, after a random `raft_lapse_campaign` (150 to 300 ms) ([Metadata layer](#metadata-layer-metastore)) | A whole `raft_election_timeout` (1 to 2 s) after the lapse; a shorter election timeout for every group | The lapse already shows the leader's node gone, so a whole timeout would only add 1 to 2 s per try to a failover that pauses the writes of a client whose clock runs fast once it passes about 7.1 s, and the [multi-Raft spike](#appendix-multi-raft-spike)'s failovers took 1.4 to 5 timeouts over many groups; the Raft paper's 150 to 300 ms keeps split votes rare (§9.3). A shorter timeout everywhere would also elect when a busy loop merely delays heartbeats, which the lapse never does |
| Lease clock | Both `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` and `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`: the holder ends a lease on whichever runs faster, the grantor on whichever runs slower; a holder ends its leases on resume; a holder bounds each grantor's rate from grant round trips, per grantor node and boot, takes no lease before a bound from two grants `rate_gap` apart, and widens its margin by the bound's excess over `1 + lease_drift / 2` ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` with a 1% margin; `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` with a margin wide enough for every slew (37%); `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` alone; shortening each lease by the rate `adjtimex` reports; stopping leases while the two clocks disagree; requiring chrony's `maxslewrate`; synchronized clocks with a maximum offset (CockroachDB); before the first bound, a lease on `lease_drift` alone, a wider margin, shorter leases, no lease until a whole `rate_window`, a bound from the next heartbeat's grant, a longer restart wait, or a startup rate check against a majority of peers; the margin `max(lease_drift, 1 − 1/B)` | The kernel lets a time daemon run `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` 22.6% fast or slow and chrony slews at 8.3% by default, so a 1% margin lets a 4 s lease overlap a new primary by up to 1.85 s. A margin for every slew rests on a reading of every kernel knob and misses the unbounded `ntp_tick_adj`; `adjtimex` state changes mid-lease; stopping leases stops reads for the length of a slew, which under chrony with internet servers lasts minutes; the time daemon is the operator's. `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW` escapes the daemon but not a boot calibration the kernel never bounds and only the daemon corrects, so each clock covers the other, and the rate bound covers both erring together. Calibration errors over 1% are rare but have shipped (4% on Skylake-X), so a lease on `lease_drift` alone before the first bound outlasts its grant by 39 ms at ±1% and 279 ms at 4%, and no wider margin is safe for an error the kernel does not bound; shorter leases only scale the overlap. Refusing leases for a whole window costs 10 s of reads at every start and detection, and the next heartbeat's grant 1 s, including after every QEMU snapshot, where `rate_gap` costs 100 ms. A longer restart wait covers only a restarted grantor, and a majority check judges a node where safety is per pair. `1 − 1/B` alone is exact but leaves no headroom: a 0.4% rate change after measurement already overlaps ([lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md)). A maximum offset would put a 500 ms stasis at the end of every lease, against 1% here, and stop nodes once a time-service outage lets oscillators drift apart, after about 40 minutes ([clock slew spike](#appendix-clock-slew-spike), [clock skew study](../studies/clock-skew/README.md)) |
| Lease across a VM pause | A holder ends its leases when the VMClock disruption marker changes, read from a mapped page each pass, and when a grantor's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC_RAW`, carried on every heartbeat, moved further than the margin of the lease taken from its last grant allows, `1/(1 − m)` times the holder's; peer messages are handled before reads ([Epoch changes](#epoch-changes-and-failure-detection)) | The rate bound alone; reading the emulated RTC each pass; reading `ptp_kvm`'s host clock each pass; waiting on `/dev/vmclock0` with `poll()` or Firecracker's `vm_generation_counter`; confirming each batch of reads with every grantor; requiring hosts that never stop storage VMs | A restore, a migration or a QEMU stop of a guest on kvm-clock longer than 3 s freezes every guest clock, so the rate bound lets a holder serve up to a heartbeat on a lapsed lease and then shortens every lease for up to 4 s. The marker changes before a restored or migrated guest runs and costs 3 ns, but a stop in place leaves it alone; under Firecracker and QEMU with `+invtsc` the stop shows in the holder's own clocks, and on kvm-clock the grantor's clock catches it from the messages queued during it. `ptp_kvm` keeps host time through every pause but costs 1.6 µs a read. The RTC counts whole seconds, costs microseconds and is missing in Firecracker; `poll()` cannot wake a loop on QEMU and races the reads it should precede; the generation counter covers only Firecracker restores. Confirming reads costs a round trip per batch, and which hosts stop their VMs is the operator's choice ([VM pause spike](#appendix-vm-pause-spike)). A fixed threshold at `1 + lease_drift / 2` trips on every heartbeat from a grantor more than 0.5% fast, so its rate bound never forms ([lease margins study](../studies/lease-margins/README.md)) |
| Metadata keys and inode IDs | Keys grouped by inode under one-byte tags, fixed-width fields and one variable last component; inode IDs of a slot plus a sequence number, reserved 64 at a time by a `Txn` of their own and prefetched, with shards split at slot boundaries; values over 1,000 bytes in piece keys | One global transaction per create (3FS on FoundationDB, JuiceFS on TiKV); inodes inside directory entries (CephFS, HopsFS); escaped variable-length parts and varints (CockroachDB); the slot counter compared in every create | `create` and `mkdir` stay single-shard with no global transaction service, and `st_ino` stays stable. Escaping is needed only when several parts vary; a counter in every create would conflict between concurrent creators ([MetaStore API study](../studies/metastore-api/README.md)) |
| Snapshot epoch of a chunk's first write | A `freeze` of version 0, the absent chunk, in the record of its first write after a cut ([Versions share blocks](#on-disk-format)) | The snapshot epoch in every write and punch entry, as Ceph updates `SnapSet.seq` on every write; the version's high bits | No format change, and 37 bytes once per chunk created in a volume with snapshots instead of 8 bytes on every write entry. The version's high bits count the PG's intervals, not a volume's snapshots ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike)) |
| Replay past pending writes | The replay point stays at the oldest pending write, and replay applies the committed records after it again, in LSN order ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Skipping a write whose blocks hold a newer version; a checkpoint that waits for pending writes to commit | Applying again is harmless when a primary maps a chunk's writes in version order, so the index needs no check ([chunk epochs spike](#appendix-chunk-epochs-spike), R1). Writes of different chunks commit out of LSN order, so no wait makes the mapped set a prefix of the log without hiding acknowledged writes. A version check cannot catch a dropped write, whose version is newer than what it replaced. The cost is replaying what was logged while a write waited, at most `node_down_after` |
| Changing a volume's pool | In place: cut, read-through to the old pool, background copy | Snapshot and restore into a new PVC only | Keeps the PVC, so applications are not rebound; reuses the snapshot machinery |
| Manifest | An edit per checkpoint and a snapshot once the edits since the last exceed the smaller of it and the next (Pebble's `RotationHelper`); the last piece lists the snapshot and edits ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A full snapshot in every manifest; RocksDB's space-amplification rule (500%); rewriting only the usage table's dirty blocks (Sprite LFS ifile, F2FS SIT) | The full table grows with the disk, 0.42% of the log per checkpoint at 1 TiB and 6.6% at 16 TiB, while an edit is bounded by the checkpoint's writes: 0.01 to 0.2% with Pebble's rule. RocksDB's rule writes a third less but recovery reads up to six snapshots, not two. Random overwrites dirty nearly every table block each checkpoint ([durability study](#appendix-lsm-study-durability-spike)) |
| Tail rule | A gap ends the log only if no later header names it durable, every later header lies within `commit_depth` LSNs, and no segment header names a later manifest; otherwise Corrupt ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | The depth window alone; RocksDB's point-in-time recovery (default: open with the prefix before the corruption) or skipping corrupted records; a clean-shutdown record for a strict mode (RocksDB `kAbsoluteConsistency`, Pebble's strict tail for closed WALs) | The durable LSN, Pebble's `syncedOffset`, proves corruption whenever a round began after the missing one was durable and does not depend on the configured depth. Truncating drops acknowledged writes a replica must not lose silently (Protocol-Aware Recovery). A clean-shutdown mode helps only planned restarts |
| Replay with several open segments | A lane per merge worker beside each stream's shared lane, named in the segment header; replay reads each lane from its last segment starting at or before the replay point and merges the lanes by LSN, reading merge lanes for their LSNs only ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | The per-stream rule; merge output in the shared metadata segment; the manifest recording each open segment and its next block (F2FS's current segments); merge records outside the LSN sequence (Pebble's and RocksDB's compaction output); a lane per merge | Per stream, replay missed a record past the replay point in 74 to 99% of a model's crashes with merges running; per lane, none. The shared segment stranded 30 to 57 segments against 8 to 10. Recorded positions read half as much, about 70 MiB less per start at one merge worker, for an open list in every manifest and still lanes to resume in. Merge records without LSNs would change the record header, the tail rule, blob references and rebuild to save a step per worker of reading. A lane per merge leaves each ended merge's last segment a lane head with its tail stranded ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)) |
| Freed segments' headers | `NextSeq` in every manifest, counted from durable rounds: a header below it is judged by the usage table, one at or past it was opened after the manifest; freed segments reusable at once ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Every header of this log counts as used; zero a freed segment's header before the freeing manifest; an open sequence in every usage row; the header's first LSN against the base LSN; F2FS's prefree segments | Pebble and RocksDB judge files by the manifest's next file number the same way. Counting every header as used keeps freed segments used forever; zeroing needs an ordered write before each manifest and still leaves block 1; a sequence per row costs up to 2 MiB per snapshot and says nothing of free segments; block 1 may not have landed. Prefree removes one crash order for a state and a wait in allocation, and still needs `NextSeq`. Taken when the checkpoint begins, the number fails the random-crash property at its first seed ([log segments study](../studies/log-segments/)) |
| Stale records past a recovered tail | Zero the first block of every record header found past the end, then flush ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A new log number per restart, as Pebble and RocksDB start a new WAL: a fresh segment per stream with the restart LSN in its header | Zeroing costs one write per found header and the flush recovery needs anyway; fresh segments would strand the rest of every open segment at each restart and add a rule to replay |
| Rounds in flight | `commit_depth` 1 by default; durability a prefix ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | Depth 2 across streams; RocksDB's cap on a group led by a small write (size + 128 KiB) | Pebble and RocksDB keep one sync in flight per log; their throughput comes from the group collected meanwhile. On the consumer NVMe drive depth 2 kept a hot 4K commit next to 1 MiB cold records at 2.3 ms, no better than one round holding both records under one flush, because a flush drains the drive's whole write cache ([stream overlap spike](#appendix-stream-overlap-spike)) |
| Checkpoint lag | Producers other than the checkpoint and the cleaner wait once twice `max_replay_bytes` of their records follow the newest manifest ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | No bound; refusing items with an error; counting the checkpoint's own records toward the hold | Pebble and RocksDB stop writes at two memtables by default; waiting keeps items and acknowledgments in order, where an error would make every producer retry; a checkpoint larger than the bound would hold writers past its own manifest |
| Superblock repair | From its copy only while block 0 is unreadable or this format's | Repair from any valid copy (ZFS labels); copies used only by an offline tool (ext4, XFS, Btrfs) | A bad block 0 needs no operator, and a disk repartitioned or given to another system is never written; ZFS has rewritten its labels over a disk reformatted with UFS |
| Cleaner index updates | One index update per move, applied by the index's owner only where a map still stores the copied blocks at their source (WiscKey; Titan's GC) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A forwarding table the cleaner updates instead of the index, per block run or per original record (Pebble's blob file rewrite); relocating blocks only when a compaction rewrites their index entries (RocksDB's BlobDB, Titan's level merge) | The index is in memory, so a move is one put, 42 to 72 ns. A remap per original record wrote 19 to 33× fewer entries, but sent 63 to 96% of reads through an extra lookup and forced copies into log order (+6 to 7% hot/cold write amplification); per block run it saved nothing and grew the index by 43 to 91%. Relocating in compaction would rewrite the whole index, and follows key ranges, not free space ([value separation spike](#appendix-value-separation-spike)) |
| Cleaner copies in replay | A `copy` entry names the location it copied from; replay applies it where a map still stores the blocks there, as the owner did live ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A plain write entry; a copy entry checked by version only | A write entry would bring back a block a write replaced before the copy record. A version check holds only while a chunk never reuses a version, and differs from the owner's live test. The source costs 8 bytes per entry; a full copy record of single-block extents still needs 3 header blocks |
| Room for a merge | Output installed in steps of `compaction_step_bytes`, inputs bounded below by the step's last key, output in its merge worker's lane of the metadata stream (ScyllaDB's incremental compaction) ([LSM tree](#on-disk-format)) | Output installed whole with a reserve that grows with the largest merge (RocksDB's `SstFileManager` reservation); a size cap per merge (Pebble's and RocksDB's 25 target files) | Whole, a merge held up to the whole index beyond the manifests, 258 segments of 64 MiB for a 16 GiB shard; in steps, 2, for at most 0.6% more manifest bytes. A size cap suits levels cut into files but leaves whole-key-space runs unmerged ([LSM space spike](#appendix-lsm-space-spike)) |
| Writers when cleaning falls behind | Slow chunk writes to the cleaner's measured net freeing rate below 8 free segments, wait at none, `NoSpace` only when nothing can be freed (CockroachDB's admission control) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | `NoSpace` as soon as only the reserve is free; RocksDB's fixed delayed write rate adjusted by ×0.8 / ×1.25; Pebble's stops only | Failing writes the cleaner is about to make room for turns a lag into errors; a fixed rate needs tuning per drive and fill, and a stop alone gives the latency cliff CockroachDB built admission control to avoid |
| TRIM of freed segments | Paced at 128 MiB/s, one segment per discard, faster only for a backlog older than 5 minutes (Pebble's delete pacer) ([On-disk format](#on-disk-format)) | A TRIM per segment as it frees; Pebble's extra rate when free space is low | Bursts of discards stall some SSDs; a TRIM frees no log space, so low space is no reason to hurry |
| Cleaner threading | Worker goroutines copy; the index's owner applies their moves ([parallel cleaner spike](#appendix-parallel-cleaner-spike)) | Inline on the writing loop; workers that update a shared concurrent index | Inline cleaning cost the writer 5.9 to 11 µs per write and a p99 of 250 to 540 µs. A shared index saved the owner only 0.16 to 0.59 µs per write, and would need a concurrent index and memtable, which a loop per core avoids |
| ublk recovery mode | `UBLK_F_USER_RECOVERY` with `REISSUE`, plus `QUIESCE` for upgrades; a dead daemon's writes fenced by incarnation ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | User recovery alone (requests in flight fail with `EIO`); `FAIL_IO` (every request fails until recovery); no recovery (the device is removed) | A failed write takes the pod's filesystem down (ext4 aborts its journal, XFS shuts down), so only a reissue keeps a restart invisible, as SPDK's ublk target chooses. Block writes are idempotent, and the block layer holds the request until its reissue completes |
| Incarnation fencing | The incarnation in every chunk write and punch, checked when the primary orders the write; held writes ordered again only if no other primary led the PG since ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | The incarnation in the connection hello only, with every hello reaching every loop before its connection's writes; a table of newest incarnations kept across primary changes | 8 bytes per write instead of an ordering rule between loops; the interval rule and the held-write rule make a table that survives primary changes unnecessary ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)) |
| Upgrade rollout | Both DaemonSets `OnDelete`; `rock-solid upgrade` rolls each node's daemon, then its node plugin, then waits for its PGs before the next node ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | `RollingUpdate` on both DaemonSets; daemon and holder as two containers of one pod; a holder DaemonSet with `maxSurge` | Independent rolling updates can restart a node's daemon and holder together and drop every descriptor; containers of one pod restart together on a spec change; two node plugins on one node would contend for kubelet's one CSI socket |
| FUSE protocol layer | Own codec over raw `/dev/fuse`, driven from the event loops ([Daemon restart and upgrade](#daemon-restart-and-upgrade)) | go-fuse (patched to adopt a descriptor and send resends); libfuse with a custom-I/O build; FUSE over io_uring | go-fuse runs a goroutine per request with a synchronous filesystem API and allocates per request. Neither library adopts an initialized descriptor or sends a resend ([FUSE restart spike](#appendix-fuse-restart-spike), [FUSE handoff spike](#appendix-fuse-handoff-spike)). The io_uring transport strands or fails requests when the daemon crashes. The protocol is a fixed binary layout, so a hand-written codec is small and can be fuzzed |
| Block frontend | ublk locally, NVMe-oF/TCP remotely | NBD (single-threaded overhead); iSCSI (old, heavy) | ublk rides io\_uring with little overhead; NVMe-oF is the native remote protocol for NVMe |
| Block volume logical block size | 4 KiB, with 512-byte sectors per volume through the primary's merge ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) | 512 bytes by default (krbd) | Linux filesystems write 4 KiB and QEMU pads its guests itself, so 512 by default would make every small write pay a base read and a 4 KiB block; Vitastor exposes 4 KiB too |
| Block volumes on a full pool | Overwrites of live, unshared blocks and discards pass `write_limit`; writes that need new space are held until the pool has room, with no time limit, and fail only when an operator says so; filesystems the CSI driver creates are mounted with `discard` ([Block frontend](#block-frontend)) | Fail at once (dm-thin `error_if_no_space`); hold 60 s, then fail (dm-thin and LVM thin by default); an emergency reserve that lets block writes past `write_limit` for a bounded amount; thick provisioning of filesystem metadata; `fstrim` on a schedule | A hold that ended when the pool grew completed every held write and left ext4 and XFS clean; failing lost the refused data although `fsync` reported `ENOSPC`, made ext4 abort its journal and refuse every write, shut XFS down at unmount, and left an image consistent only after a log replay, which needs the space anyway ([block ENOSPC spike](#appendix-block-enospc-spike)). So any hold that ends in failure adds an outage and data loss to the wait, and a reserve only postpones the hold while taking room from recovery and space grants. Metadata cannot be provisioned ahead, since ext4 and XFS place bitmaps, extent blocks, inode chunks and directories wherever they allocate. Ceph RBD pauses the same way; the price is a hung volume until space appears, which events and the `Full` condition report. Without discard, deleted files never free pool space; a weekly `fstrim` frees it too late |
| Testing the indexes | A map model plus a metamorphic test: one ops file per seed under option sets at their extremes, histories compared with each other and the model, failures reduced into regression files ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | A model only; RocksDB's `db_stress` with an expected state and options randomized per run | Internal choices (piece size, flush and compaction timing, failed checkpoints, restarts) must never change a read, and a history diff checks that for ops a model would be costly to write, such as views and range removals; in the [metamorphic testing spike](#appendix-metamorphic-testing-spike) two of five planted bugs showed only under the option set at an extreme |
| Operations that never complete | Kept in the history with an infinite return time, so the check may place them anywhere after their call or nowhere ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | Dropped from the history; ended at the crash | A write whose node crashed may have reached the disk; dropping it flags every read that saw it, and ending it at the crash forbids a reissue from taking effect later. Jepsen and Porcupine treat such operations the same way |
| Regression cases from data | `datadriven` files (`github.com/cockroachdb/datadriven`, as Pebble and etcd-raft) | Go table tests; one golden file per test | A reduced failing seed, a compaction-picking case or a recovery script is data; `-rewrite` regenerates expected output for review, and etcd-raft's tests already use the format |
| Checks inside the code | Settings the tests and the simulator turn on (`Check` in group commit and the LSM tree) | Pebble's `invariants` build tag with `Sometimes` taking rare paths at random; RocksDB's debug-build asserts | A setting is chosen per simulator run with the same binary, and rare paths come from option sets drawn from the seed; `Sometimes` uses the global random source, which breaks seed replay |
| Crash model of the simulated disk | Each unflushed block write lands or not on its own, in execution order | Pebble's crash clone (random unsynced blocks of the latest contents); RocksDB's random prefix of unsynced data | The device's volatile cache can persist any subset, so holes and an older overwrite without the newer one must be recoverable; Pebble's model makes holes but never keeps an older overwrite, and RocksDB's makes neither |
| Driver test time | A kernel seam in `internal/loop` (wait, read every clock together, arm the timerfd) with the clocks read after the wait; a stepped kernel on scripted clocks, stepped by the simulator's scheduler over real socketpairs; real-kernel tests in CI and VM tests nightly ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy), [Runtime in Go](#runtime-in-go)) | `testing/synctest` for drivers; reading time through Go's `time` package so a bubble fakes it; interposing the syscalls (libc symbol replacement as madsim does, `LD_PRELOAD`, `ptrace`, seccomp user notification); time namespaces; clock cases only in VMs; a sans-IO loop that emits its syscalls as actions; the `Resumed` detector and timer heap as pure functions with no seam; a fully simulated network and disk with no real descriptors | synctest fakes only the `time` package: inside a bubble an hour of `time.Sleep` moved `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` 18 µs, a timerfd never expired, and a goroutine in `epoll_wait` held the bubble's clock until the kernel woke it. Go's clock is `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`, neither of the lease clocks. Raw syscalls bypass libc, tracing every syscall is slow and not deterministic, and a time namespace's offsets freeze once a process enters it. VMs take minutes per case with no seed. A sans-IO loop duplicates the recording layer at a cost per operation, pure functions alone cannot see the order of wait, clock read and reads, and a fully simulated network leaves the drivers' own code untested ([driver testing study](../studies/driver-testing/README.md)) |
| Simulation | Own simulator over a sans-IO core | Antithesis (a deterministic hypervisor); Jepsen-only testing | We need a detailed disk and page-cache fault model and thousands of seeds per commit on our own machines. Jepsen alone can't reproduce failures |
| Protocol models | TLA+ models checked by TLC in CI: per model a `ci` configuration under 5 minutes, a `deep` one of up to 4 hours run once per change, and nightly random simulation; each protocol modelled in the phase that builds it, before its code; the epoch change model one spec with `ci-leases` and `ci-peering` configurations ([Testing strategy](#testing-strategy)) | Models checked by hand (CockroachDB, etcd-raft); exhaustive deep runs every night; trace validation (etcd-raft); two epoch specs with a refinement check between them; all six models in phase 2 | Models checked by hand went stale against code and against themselves (CockroachDB #54130); an unchanged deep run repeats its answer; MongoDB abandoned trace validation after 10 weeks; one spec with two configurations covers the read property, which needs peering and leases together, without a refinement mapping to keep in step; the snapshot cut and cross-shard commit build on designs phase 2 is still checking, and cross-shard commit is not designed yet ([protocol models study](../studies/protocol-models/README.md)) |
| License | Apache 2.0 | AGPL (MinIO); network copyleft (Vitastor) | Storage is adopted slowly and its license is part of the trust decision; copyleft with a network clause deters adopters. Anyone can embed, fork or ship Apache 2.0 code, and its patent grant covers contributors' patents. Proprietary forks that never contribute back are the accepted price. The name Rock Solid states the priority: correctness before features |

## Roadmap and open questions

Build order puts the simulator before any real driver and ships RWX file storage as the first usable product. Each phase ends with something that runs and is tested. Phases 1 to 5 are broken into reviewable components, libraries and tests in the [build checklist](build-checklist.md).

1. **Sans-IO core and simulator.** The Go module with its lint and sans-IO analyzer, then a single-node ChunkStore state machine with the per-disk log, extent index, partial blocks merged into whole ones, punch, its cleaner and the LSM index (memtable, sorted runs with Bloom filters, compaction), simulated disk with the full fault model, invariant checker.
2. **Protocol models.** TLA+ models, checked with TLC, of epoch changes (requests accepted only in their PG's current interval, peering that stops every interval since the last activated one, keeps what that one decided through members' missing lists and drops only a suffix of a chunk's pending versions, a restarted primary taking a new interval, primary read leases and Raft leader fortification on the same node-pair leases), sessions (renewal on heartbeats to the control-plane leader, expiry on its clock, leader failover) and fencing with fence acknowledgments, cache-mode recalls, and purges, before writing the real protocols. The epoch change model is one spec with two CI configurations, one heavy on leases and one on peering, since its central property needs both. Later protocols get their models in the phases that build them.
